--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page follows a blank introductory leaf and begins the formal introduction to the study of Meister Eckhart's philosophy, specifically addressing the impact of Heinrich Denifle's scholarship.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

---

In the light shed by the research of Denifle <note>Heinrich Denifle (1844–1905) was an influential historian who discovered Latin manuscripts that proved Eckhart’s dependence on traditional church teaching.</note> regarding the philosophy of Eckhart, the luster of his system’s originality has faded considerably. Even those who judge him differently than Denifle—in those instances where he departs from the paths of the <term>scholastic philosophy</term> <note>The dominant medieval method of learning based on Aristotelian logic and church dogma.</note> of his time—will hardly want to label him the “Father of German Philosophy” anymore. It was an error in judgment, an error that is not only easily explained by the circumstances of the time, but also not even particularly to be regretted. At the very least, such a lively interest in the Master would hardly have arisen in such wide circles if it had been known from the beginning that the contrast between his philosophy and <term>Scholasticism</term> was, to a large extent, imaginary. However, the interest he has found should not be regarded as undeserved, even today with our improved understanding. His role in that spiritual movement which we are accustomed to calling <term>Mysticism</term> <note>A tradition focused on the direct, personal experience of the divine.</note> remains—as the judgment of his contemporaries alone can teach us—nonetheless significant enough to secure him a lasting, prominent place of honor in the history of our people’s intellectual life. Despite the sorry state of the textual tradition <note>The "tradition" refers to the way manuscripts were copied and passed down through the centuries, often resulting in errors.</note>, we are still able to imagine to some extent what impression he made through the

<summary>This introduction discusses how modern research has challenged Meister Eckhart's reputation as a wholly original "Father of German Philosophy" by revealing his roots in Scholasticism. Despite this, the author argues that Eckhart remains a vital figure in the history of German mysticism and intellectual life.</summary>

<keywords>Meister Eckhart, Heinrich Denifle, Scholasticism, Mysticism, German philosophy, textual tradition, intellectual history</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s discussion of Meister Eckhart’s historical and linguistic significance, transitioning from the description of his personal charisma to his role in developing German as a language of philosophy.</meta>

...the flight of his thoughts and the nobility of his will once had upon the listeners hanging on his words.

In the history of German literature, his name would always retain a good reputation, even if his undertaking—to clothe those thoughts in our mother tongue, which before him (and for how long after him!) were believed inseparable from their Latin linguistic shell—had been less successful than is actually the case.

To be sure, several centuries earlier, Notker <note>Notker Labeo (c. 950–1022) was a monk at the Abbey of Saint Gall who translated several Latin classical and religious works into Old High German.</note> had already made a bold attempt to gain German translations for <term>philosophical terms</term> <note>original: "philosophische Termini"</note>; however, it does not seem that this had a great effect. The matter is different with Eckhart. One can admit that he did not find everything yet to be done here, and that the German philosophical linguistic material that accumulated during the 14th century does not owe its formation entirely to him; yet one will hardly go far wrong if one considers him here, too, absolutely as the master and the other <term>Mystics</term> as his disciples. Of course, more precise investigations are needed here than have been made so far; Denifle took a completely negative stance against the only existing work by Kramm <note>Referring to the 1884 study by F. Kramm on Eckhart's influence on German language.</note> ¹ and posited a thorough knowledge of <term>Scholasticism</term> as an indispensable prerequisite for such investigations. Certainly not without justification; however, since among those who possess such knowledge, none is likely to be found willing to take on the work, and for those who otherwise have the desire and ability for it, the fulfillment of the prerequisite is quite difficult, we could hardly hope to live to see its appearance if no middle ground were to be found here.

I believe, however, that such a middle ground exists. Already in the course of the 14th century, the Latin philosophical terms were, sometimes more, sometimes less—

---

¹ Journal of German Philology, Vol. 16, p. 1 ff.

<summary>This page discusses Meister Eckhart's pioneering role in translating complex philosophical concepts from Latin into the German language, establishing him as a "master" to later mystics. It also touches on the scholarly debate regarding the difficulty of studying this linguistic transition without a deep background in medieval Scholasticism.</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, Notker Labeo, German language, philosophical terminology, Scholasticism, Heinrich Denifle, Mysticism, linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's introduction regarding the linguistic and philological challenges of editing medieval mystical texts, specifically focusing on the corruption of manuscript traditions.</meta>

fully compiled. In some cases, they have remained independent; in others, they were added early on to existing vocabularies—as was already the case in the 14th century with that of <term>Fritsche Closener</term> <note>A priest and chronicler from Strasbourg known for his German-Latin dictionary</note>—or they were integrated alphabetically into new copies of those works. In my opinion, an investigation and processing of these sources will be able to satisfy the requirements of historical-linguistic interest, at least to some extent.

What most spoils our enjoyment of <term>Eckhart's Sermons</term> is the sorry state in which they have been handed down to us. This state is "sorry" not only because his spoken word is incompletely recorded—which is certainly the case with all sermons written down by listeners—but especially because these texts, being highly susceptible to misunderstanding from the very beginning, have been "improved" for the worse by the hands of scribes far more than other texts.

The deeper one goes to the bottom of the manuscript <term>tradition</term> <note>The chain of copies through which a text survives from the past to the present</note>, the more one becomes convinced that it is indeed impossible to gain an all-around correct and clear picture of Eckhart's philosophical system on the basis of the German texts alone. Even if there were a prospect of finding even more numerous manuscripts than have been used so far, one could hardly hope to be able to produce reasonably authentic texts. And for that reason, it is highly doubtful whether <term>Pfeiffer</term> <note>Franz Pfeiffer (1815–1868), whose 1857 edition of Eckhart was the standard but flawed reference at the time</note> will soon find a successor who will replace his edition—which has long since become an object of speculation for antiquarian book dealers—with one that is corrected, supplemented, and improved as much as possible; in any case, the prospects for this are not favorable. For the time being, one will have to be content with adding the material that is still unknown and showing, from what is already known, how unreliable the texts in the manuscripts often are; perhaps over time, it will then also be possible to achieve a reasonably correct textual form for at least a number of Eckhart's sermons.

<summary>The editor discusses the poor preservation of Meister Eckhart's sermons, noting that scribal errors and "corrections" make it nearly impossible to reconstruct Eckhart's philosophy from German manuscripts alone. He expresses skepticism that a definitive new edition will soon replace Franz Pfeiffer's outdated work.</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, Fritsche Closener, Franz Pfeiffer, sermons, manuscripts, textual tradition, philology, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's introduction, transitioning from the general corruption of Eckhart’s texts to the specific discovery of a high-quality manuscript in Nuremberg that may help resolve these textual issues.</meta>

Contributing to this effort is the primary purpose of the following pages; they bring to light an <term>Eckhart manuscript</term> <note>original: "Eckharthandschrift"</note> that is not only among the oldest but also one of the most comprehensive of its kind. I discovered it in the Nuremberg City Library, shelfmark Cent. IV, 40.

It is a paper manuscript from the 14th century consisting of 106 folios numbered in an old hand, measuring 30 x 21 cm. The wooden covers are bound in red leather. The pages are written in two columns, with an average of 43 lines per column. On the inside of the front cover, it is written in an old hand: <gloss>“This book belongs to the monastery of Saint Katherine of the Order of Preachers in Nuremberg”</gloss> <note>original: "Das puch gehort in das Closter zu sant Katherein prediger orden in nurnberg". The "Order of Preachers" refers to the Dominican order.</note>. It received its old library number, E XIII, around the middle of the 15th century during the cataloging of the books belonging to the <term>convent of Dominican nuns</term> <note>original: "Dominikanerinnenkloster"</note>. (Compare below, p. 122, no. XIII.)

According to this catalog, the <term>manuscript</term> <note>original: "Hs." (Handschrift)</note> was already in the convent’s possession before the <term>reformation of the monastery</term> <note>This refers to the "Observant Reform," a 15th-century movement to return religious orders to a stricter, original way of life, rather than the later Protestant Reformation</note>—that is, before the year 1428.

This collection is composed of smaller gatherings that were acquired from various sources; one can still see from the language that some parts originated from quite far-reaching circles, while others came more directly from Eckhart’s home region. As demonstrated in the annotations, the scribes copied several pieces more than once. They apparently did not notice this; it is easily understood, and indeed, one can only wonder that it did not happen more frequently. Two distinct scribes can be clearly identified; at first glance, one might believe that even more were involved in the work, but upon closer inspection, one moves away from that opinion. The difference likely results from the fact that the text was not written all at once with the same ink and pen, but gradually, with varying distances between the letters and lines.

If one could assume that many parts—if not all—were written down from <term>dictation</term> <note>A common practice where a text was read aloud in a scriptorium for scribes to copy</note>, then not only the numerous <term>clerical errors</term> <note>original: "Schreibfehler"</note> but also many phonetic and linguistic peculiarities would be much easier to explain...

<summary>The author introduces a 14th-century paper manuscript from a Nuremberg Dominican convent, detailing its physical state and history. He suggests that its composition by two scribes, possibly via dictation, explains its linguistic variations and errors.</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, Nuremberg City Library, manuscript description, Dominican convent, Saint Katherine, scribal habits, 14th century, philology</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s introduction regarding the linguistic characteristics and textual integrity of the manuscript from the St. Katherine monastery. The previous page discussed how the collection was assembled from various sources, leading to a mix of regional dialects.</meta>

XI

permitted. For example, the scribe apparently did not intend to consistently use initial *b* for *w* (*bold = wold* <note>original: "bold = wold." In modern German, *wollte* (would/wanted). The scribe substituted 'b' for 'w'.</note>, *besen = wesen* <note>original: "besen = wesen." In modern German, *wesen* (to be/being).</note>, *bonen = wonen* <note>original: "bonen = wonen." In modern German, *wohnen* (to dwell/live).</note> etc.), yet it occurred so frequently that it is reasonable to assume these errors entered the text through hearing <note>The editor suggests the scribe may have been writing based on what they heard spoken or dictated, rather than copying visually.</note>.

If one keeps in mind that the texts were originally composed in a dialect closely related to <term>Low German</term> <note>original: "Niederdeutsch." A group of German dialects spoken in the north, distinct from the High German of the south.</note>, much is explained, yet by no means everything. There remains more than enough in the way of gross oversights, misunderstandings, and so on; most of these are of such a nature that they cannot be resolved with a minor correction. I have only marked those passages that can either be easily improved or not improved at all. If I have not indicated any concerns in one way or the other, it does not prove that none existed for me, but only that <term>as things stand</term> <note>original Latin: "rebus sic stantibus"</note>, the sentence could, in a pinch, be allowed to pass. Otherwise, the text would have been overly cluttered with *(sic!)* <note>A Latin term used in editing to indicate that an error in the source text is being quoted exactly as it appeared.</note> markings. The corruption of the text is not the sole fault of the scribes of our <term>manuscript</term> <note>original: "Hs." (Handschrift)</note>—where, after all, is a sermon by <term>Eckhart</term> <note>Meister Eckhart (c. 1260–1328) was a high-profile Dominican theologian and philosopher whose mystical sermons were often recorded by listeners, sometimes inaccurately.</note> truly well-preserved? Rather, these errors likely represent only a very small portion of their own mistakes.

To see this for oneself, one need only read the final sermon (No. 82, page 84 ff.): aside from minor details, the text is entirely flawless throughout. It is likely not a sermon transcribed by nuns, but one recorded by the author himself. I cannot otherwise explain the good condition of the text, for the subject matter here was no less difficult or prone to misunderstanding than in the other sermons. One could well call this interesting piece a <term>summary of mysticism</term> <note>original Latin: "summa mystica," a play on the "Summa Theologica," implying a comprehensive summary of mystical thought.</note>, as it encompasses the central ideas of mysticism. Some aspects point to Eckhart, but others speak clearly against his authorship; the preacher is, however, a disciple who is entirely under the Master's spell. Furthermore, the sermon is striking for its length. I am inclined to believe that this length did not, in reality, exceed what was customary at that time...

<summary>The editor discusses the linguistic errors and textual corruption in the manuscript, noting that while most sermons are poorly preserved, Sermon 82 is remarkably flawless and serves as a comprehensive summary of mystical thought.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, scribal errors, Low German, Meister Eckhart, Summa Mystica, Sermon 82, manuscript integrity</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s introduction, shifting from a linguistic analysis of the scribe's dialect to a discussion regarding the structural integrity and preservation of the sermons themselves. The editor argues that the texts we possess are often mere shadows of the original spoken word.</meta>

XII

...goes beyond the average measure. For without a doubt, all of **Eckhart's sermons** have been significantly shortened; the <term>note-takers</term> <note>original: "Nachschreiber." These were individuals, often members of the congregation, who recorded the sermons as they were being spoken, often in a form of shorthand.</note> extracted only the main points. These were then frequently shortened again by the copyists, and often nothing more remains of a sermon than one or several **“sayings”**; for it is certain, at least to me, that these are nothing but the remnants of sermons.

Our manuscript offers several interesting examples of this textual development. This process is also easily explained. One cannot be mistaken that the circle of thought in Eckhart's sermons is quite narrow; in his reluctance to engage with practical life, he was forced to vary the same abstract ideas over and over again. Certainly, this often happened in different places and before different audiences—indeed, several different versions of one and the same sermon may very well go back to records made in different locations. However, when all these pieces came together in the hands of collectors, they realized that much was present in duplicate, and many a sermon contained only one or two new thoughts. Thus, they began to make deletions or cuts—sometimes large, sometimes small, sometimes with more or less care. As a result, it has become difficult in many cases to cleanly and surely separate Eckhart’s original property from the rest of the mass.

How cautious one must be with such judgments is demonstrated by **Preger’s** <note>Wilhelm Preger (1827–1896), a prominent theologian and historian of German mysticism.</note> verdict on **Johannes Franko** ¹. The sermon on **“Let it be”** <note>original Latin: "Fiat." Taken from the biblical "Fiat mihi secundum verbum tuum" (Let it be done to me according to your word).</note> shared below on page 42, number 43, had become known to Preger from a Klosterneuburg and an Oxford manuscript; he had it printed in the <term>aforementioned work</term> <note>original: "a. a. O." (am angeführten Ort), the German equivalent of the scholarly citation "Op. cit."</note> on page 457 following the latter manuscript. Now, everyone can see for themselves that what we have there is only a very poor fragment from the first part of the sermon (though Preger sought to prove the contrary), and that Preger's...

---

¹ *History of German Mysticism* II 173.

<summary>The editor explains that Meister Eckhart's recorded sermons are often heavily abridged summaries or "sayings" created by note-takers and copyists who removed repetitive content. He warns that previous scholars, like Wilhelm Preger, sometimes mistook these poor fragments for complete works.</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, sermons, manuscript transmission, abridgment, Wilhelm Preger, Johannes Franko, German Mysticism, Fiat, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s introduction, moving from the general problem of textual corruption and shortening in Eckhart's sermons to the specific challenges of identifying individual authors like Franko and the provenance of the manuscripts used in this edition.</meta>

XIII

...judgment of **Franko** <note>Likely referring to Franko of Cologne or a similar contemporary figure whose identity remains obscured by the fragmentary nature of the texts.</note> is completely unfounded. It is simply impossible to characterize a philosophical preacher based on such meager fragments.

Regarding the **numbering** of the individual pieces, I have pursued a purely practical goal: the numbering has inherent value only for the more or less complete sermons. Any individual sayings and so forth that stood between these sermons I have combined under a single number, without wishing to imply that I consider them remains of the same sermon or even the work of the same author.

I have provided **Sermon No. 4** of Appendix I from another source, as the text in this manuscript is very poor. **Sievers** <note>Eduard Sievers (1850–1932), a prominent German philologist and linguist.</note> also shared it according to the **Oxford manuscript**, and thus it should now be possible to establish a passable text for it first; it is therefore more suitable than any other for inclusion in textbooks.

The first three sermons included in the appendix originate from a manuscript that **Friedrich von Amberg**¹ brought to the local <term>Minorite monastery</term> <note>The Minorites, or Friars Minor, are a branch of the Franciscan Order. This monastery was a center of learning where many such manuscripts were preserved.</note> around 1400, along with many others. (**Manuscript No. 95**.) He had several old pieces bound into one volume, renumbered the 211 leaves (made partly of **parchment**, partly of **paper**), and preceded them with a general index. The first part consists exclusively of sermons or sermon drafts, while the second part also contains many interesting—partly historical—treatises and reports.

---

¹ Originally a Minorite in **Regensburg**, he appears in 1384 as Provincial and Professor of Theology in **Freiburg im Breisgau**; in 1393 he was Provincial in **Freiburg im Üchtland** <note>The city of Fribourg in Switzerland, distinguished here from the German Freiburg.</note>, where he died in 1432—according to the order's chronicle at the age of 103. He was a supporter of **Clement VII** <note>The first antipope of the Great Western Schism, who reigned from Avignon.</note> and is said to have been appointed General of the Minorite Order by him. See also my treatise on his copy of the vocabulary of **Fritsche Closener** in the *Journal for the History of the Upper Rhine* 1895, Issue 3.

<summary>The editor details his editorial methods, specifically the practical numbering of sermon fragments, and provides a historical account of the manuscript's preservation by the 14th-century theologian Friedrich von Amberg.</summary>
<keywords>Franko, sermon numbering, manuscript fragments, Friedrich von Amberg, Minorite monastery, Freiburg, Clement VII, Fritsche Closener, manuscript provenance</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s introduction, specifically discussing the provenance and stylistic characteristics of the sermons found in the collection brought to the Minorite monastery by Friedrich von Amberg.</meta>

XIV

The sermons have “come from many sources,” as the catalog of **St. Catherine’s Convent** <note>Likely the Dominican convent in Nuremberg or St. Gallen, famous for its large collection of mystical and devotional literature.</note> would put it; **Friedrich von Amberg** was a collector in the truest sense of the word and, on his long journeys, gathered whatever was within his reach. Only three clearly recognizable groups of sermons are of interest to us here.

The smallest in scope is represented by **No. 1**. It is written purely in German; the author is not influenced by the <term>mystical movement</term>, or at least only slightly. He presents his thoughts in a simple, popular language, adorned with proverbial expressions, and not without skill. The fact that he belonged not only to an old but an outdated school of thought is proven by the circumstance that his sermons are **crossed out through and through**. If Friedrich von Amberg was the one who did this—and that is indeed the most likely case—it must be quite surprising; after all, it was he who preserved for posterity the (most likely authentic) collection of sermons of the most popular of all German preachers¹!

If **No. 2**, along with the other sermons of this type, does not originate from **Suso** <note>Heinrich Suso (c. 1295–1366), a student of Meister Eckhart and a central figure in German mysticism, often called the "Minnesinger of Divine Love" for his lyrical style.</note>—since we have too few sermons of his to make a definitive judgment—then there must have been other “**minnesingers in prose**” besides him.

The **mixed Latin-German sermons**, of which I offer a sample in **No. 3**, are interesting for the very reason that they show how common the use of the German language was in treating essentially philosophical questions even back then. People were already thinking entirely in German regarding these matters; otherwise, it would not have been possible for the author to repeatedly slip from the official **language of the schools** <note>Referring to Scholastic Latin, the technical language used in medieval universities.</note> into his mother tongue during his drafts. This is not unusual in practical, popular sermons, but in...

---

¹ See my reports on this manuscript in the *Historical Yearbook* 1891, page 359 ff., where its age is, however, stated too low. According to **Father Denifle** <note>original: "P. Denifle." Heinrich Seuse Denifle (1844–1905) was a renowned Dominican scholar who revolutionized the study of medieval mysticism and Meister Eckhart.</note>, who has since examined it, it still belongs to the 13th century.

<summary>This page analyzes different groups of sermons in the collection, noting their varying levels of mystical influence and the significant transition from academic Latin to the German vernacular for philosophical expression.</summary>
<keywords>Friedrich von Amberg, St. Catherine’s Convent, Heinrich Suso, mixed-language sermons, Scholastic Latin, Heinrich Denifle, 13th-century manuscripts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction’s discussion on the linguistic and theological characteristics of the sermon collection, moving from the use of German to the specific discovery of the Nuremberg Eckhart manuscript.</meta>

XV

in the field of speculative mysticism, I have not known of any such case until now ¹.

However, this group of sermons is interesting in another respect as well: as my colleague, Father Michel, assures me, their author does not adhere to <term>Thomistic-mystical</term> <note>Referring to the mystical tradition based on the teachings of St. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), which emphasized a clear distinction between the Creator and the created.</note> views, but rather to thoroughly **pantheistic** views. This would be all the more significant as we are dealing here with Latin formulations of thoughts originally delivered in German; therefore, any doubt is excluded as to whether this might merely be an inaccurate or clumsy rendering of the Latin terminology.

In the drafts printed here, as well as in the others, the original text is occasionally modified, though apparently not with the intent of removing the questionable character of certain statements from an <term>orthodox</term> <note>In this context, "orthodox" refers to views that align with official Catholic Church doctrine of the 14th century.</note> standpoint. Where the original word was no longer recognizable, I have marked the overwritten word, as well as the additions, using brackets.

Since this entire work serves only as a supplement to previous publications, it seemed to me a fitting place to draw attention to the manuscript.

The fact that the **Nuremberg Eckhart Manuscript** could remain unknown until now is explained by the fact that users of the **City Library** relied on the catalog, according to which it supposedly contained <term>Epistles and Gospels</term> <note>The standard readings from the New Testament used in the liturgy of the Mass.</note> of the 14th century. This false entry, which was bound to lead other library users away from the manuscript, is exactly what led me to it; and even if I found something different than what I was looking for,

---

¹ Compare, for example, Schönbach, *Regarding a Graz Manuscript of Latin-German Sermons*. Graz 1890.

<summary>The editor discusses the pantheistic nature of the sermons and explains how a cataloging error in the Nuremberg City Library led to the discovery of this previously unknown Meister Eckhart manuscript.</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, Nuremberg Manuscript, pantheism, Thomism, City Library, manuscript discovery, Father Michel, Schönbach</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's account of how the Nuremberg Eckhart manuscript remained hidden, explaining that the city library’s catalog was based on outdated 15th-century inventories rather than modern scholarly examination.</meta>

XVI

I did not experience any unpleasant surprises. At that time, however, I saw in that catalog entry only an isolated error and otherwise considered it fairly reliable and the contents of the library as a whole to be well-known—especially as it had been used by all those who dealt with <term>mysticism</term> <note>A spiritual movement focused on the direct, personal experience of God, which flourished in the late Middle Ages.</note>, such as Preger, Denifle, Wagner, and others. It was only during the last autumn holidays, when the printing of these texts was already nearly finished, that I became convinced during another visit to the library that the entire catalog is completely unusable and frequently misleading. The reason for this is that the compiler, when dealing with the <term>manuscripts</term> <note>Handwritten books produced before the widespread use of the printing press.</note> of the former **St. Catherine’s Convent**, relied too heavily on the tables of contents pasted into them in the 15th century; as a result, we essentially have a catalog from that era before us. Everyone must be convinced by the information I provide below—if they do not admit it from the outset—that under these circumstances, a systematic investigation of this library (which is so extremely important for the mystical movement) was an indispensable prerequisite for any historian of **German mysticism**.

I note this, however, less to level a reproach against Preger <note>Wilhelm Preger (1827–1896), a prominent theologian and historian of German mysticism.</note>, but rather simply to establish the facts and to protect others from an error in which I myself was unfortunately caught for too long: namely, holding the contents of the library to be better known than they actually are. Had I realized the truth sooner, this work would also have taken on a somewhat different form.

---

The intellectual and spiritual life of individual nuns in the **Dominican convents** of the 14th and 15th centuries is sufficiently known to us. Much, to be sure, could still be added, including from the **Nuremberg manuscripts**, but for the

<keywords>Nuremberg City Library, St. Catherine's Convent, manuscript catalogs, German mysticism, Wilhelm Preger, Dominican convents, 15th century history</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of late medieval mystical literature, arguing that as the movement spread, it became repetitive and less original. He transitions from discussing the general decline of these "Sisterbooks" to a specific study of the library at St. Catherine’s Convent in Nuremberg.</meta>

XVII

Little of significance for either literary or cultural history can likely still be gained from this material. The more the mystical-ecstatic life spread, the more it decreased in depth, originality, and interest. The accounts of these experiences become flatter, more general, more monotonous, and more formulaic <note>The author is referring to "Sisterbooks," which recorded the lives and visions of nuns, often following predictable patterns.</note>, even if individual poetic features are not entirely lacking here and there. In the *Lives of the Nuns of Weiler*, exactly the same things are told about **Elisabeth von Esslingen** as are reported from **Töss** <note>Töss was a famous Dominican convent in Switzerland, a major center for mysticism in the 14th century.</note> regarding **Beli von Sar**. It is not the similarity of the two women's conditions, but rather the circumstance that two different doctors arrive at the same remarkable diagnosis, which shows clearly enough that legend played a shaping hand in these stories.

However, it may be more interesting than the publication of further evidence of literary production in the women’s convents of that time to learn something more precise about the extent and nature of their education through a specific example. In this regard, the **St. Catherine’s Convent in Nuremberg**, which remained exclusively receptive to the mystical movement, offers an excellent opportunity with its catalog, which is interesting for more than just its richness. But before we examine this monument more closely, a few remarks about this convent should be provided for orientation.

The convent, confirmed in the year 1295, was not favorably inclined toward the **Reform** <note>The "Observant Reform," a movement in the 14th and 15th centuries aimed at returning religious orders to a stricter, original observance of their rules.</note> undertaken toward the end of the 14th century; on the other hand, this reform was earnestly desired by the local **Dominican monastery** as well as by the **city magistrate**. There are no signs at all that the nuns were morally corrupt; and when **Johannes Nider** <note>A prominent German Dominican theologian (c. 1380–1438) known for his work on church reform and his treatise on witches.</note> writes to the nuns of **Schönensteinbach** that they had previously led a life "in which you would be loath to die," one must remember that the women of Schönensteinbach had already adopted the strict rule for which Nider was an enthusiast in 1397, and thus had entirely different concepts of monastic life. If the intellectual life in Nuremberg...

<sig>II</sig>

<summary>The author explains that late medieval mystical accounts became formulaic and legendary, then pivots to using the St. Catherine’s Convent in Nuremberg as a case study for monastic education and reform.</summary>

<keywords>mysticism, Sisterbooks, St. Catherine’s Convent, Nuremberg, monastic reform, Johannes Nider, Töss, education, hagiography</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the history of St. Catherine’s Convent in Nuremberg, moving from its intellectual life to the social and economic reasons behind its 15th-century reform.</meta>

XVIII

religious life was not as active as in many other convents—including those that had not yet been reformed—and that the introduction of reform seemed particularly necessary here, this is easily explained by the convent’s location. The nuns were, without exception, daughters of the city's burghers <note>Nuremberg's merchant and professional class.</note> and thus, even after entering the convent, they remained with at least one foot in the life of the **Imperial City**, as the house practiced no strict enclosure <term>strict enclosure: the "clausura," a rule preventing nuns from leaving their convent or receiving outsiders, intended to ensure spiritual focus.</term>. Not only were family relationships maintained, but all the feuds of the individual clans within the city naturally influenced life inside the convent walls. It is therefore quite understandable why the **Dominicans** pursued reform here with much more zeal than in secluded convents, such as **Engelthal** or elsewhere, and why they withdrew from it entirely in 1408 ^1 when they were unable to carry out their plan.

The failure of this reform also carried a material disadvantage for the city; for wealthy daughters of the citizenry who felt a calling to a stricter life went to foreign convents—likely on the advice of the Dominicans and other clergy—particularly to **Schönensteinbach** in **Alsace**, with which the Nuremberg Dominicans maintained active relations. In this way, "great wealth" <note>original: "gros gut"</note> was led out of the city. **Endres Tucher**, in his *Memorial* for the year 1428, states with all desirable clarity that it was primarily this grievance which gradually inclined the City Magistrate toward the reformation of **St. Catherine’s** ^2. Thus, the reform finally took place in the year 1428 through the Master General **Bartholomäus Texerius** and the Provincial of the German Order...

---

^1 Schieler, *Master Johannes Nider of the Order of Preachers*. Mainz 1885, p. 157, note 2.

^2 <note>original: "Item man hat die frawen vermauret zu sant Katharina..."</note> Also, they have walled in <note>Enforced strict enclosure.</note> the women at Saint Catherine’s, and foreign nuns had to come here and they had to keep the order as the monks of the Preachers <note>The Dominicans.</note> do; for it happened that very wealthy young women and widows carried great wealth into other convents outside the city, where there was unity in those other convents. *Chronicles of the German Cities II 18*.

<summary>This page explains that St. Catherine’s Convent initially resisted reform because its nuns remained deeply connected to Nuremberg's social and family politics. The city ultimately forced the reform in 1428 to prevent wealthy women from moving to distant convents and taking their inheritances out of the local economy.</summary>
<keywords>St. Catherine’s Nuremberg, Dominican Order, monastic reform, strict enclosure, Endres Tucher, economic history, Schönensteinbach, Johannes Nider</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the 1428 reform of St. Catherine’s Convent, detailing the conflicting historical accounts of the event and the eventual installation of new leadership from the reformed convent of Schönensteinbach.</meta>

XIX

...place under the leadership of **Nikolaus Notel** with the assistance of the Nuremberg Prior **Johannes Nider**.

The latter described the events in great detail nine years later in the third book of his *Formicarius* <note>Latin: "The Ant-Hill," a famous work by Nider using the behavior of ants as a moral allegory for human society.</note> (Chapter 3). However, his account—which is also repeated by his biographer **Schieler**—does not quite align with other sources that were unknown to him. The city chronicle, for instance, records that the reform took place between October 16 and 28 ^1. Another report states that of the 35 sisters, eight rejected the reformation and moved to the convents of **Engelthal** (five sisters) and **Frauenaurach** (three sisters). Nider, however, places the event during Advent <note>The four-week period of preparation before Christmas.</note> and claims that all the sisters eventually accepted the reform after a long period of refusal ^2. This contradiction may only be an apparent one: it is possible that Nider is referring only to the 27 sisters who remained; though they initially agreed to stay, they may have resisted when the "strict rule" <term>strict rule: the Observant reform, which required absolute poverty, silence, and enclosure.</term> was actually implemented, even attempting to win over members of the City Council to their side ^3.

Regardless, the success of the reform seemed to have little prospect without the transfer of nuns from already reformed convents to Nuremberg. Its survival was secured by bringing in ten nuns from **Schönensteinbach** <note>A famous reformed Dominican convent in Alsace that served as a model for many others.</note>. In doing so, the authorities were careful to consider Nuremberg women where possible, and by appointing **Gertrud Gewichtmacherin**—a local woman—as the Prioress, they ensured that...

---

^1 "Also, in the year of our Lord 1428, between the feasts of St. Gall and St. Simon and Jude (October 16–28), the Dominican friars changed the rule of the nuns at St. Catherine’s; they walled up their light-openings and windows everywhere, and they are never again permitted to eat meat. Also, in the same year, on the feasts of St. Lucy, St. Ottilia, and St. Job <note>Mid-December feast days.</note>, ten nuns were brought in from Colmarberg (=Schönensteinbach) and they shall govern the order and the convent; and several were children of citizens from here." *Chronicle of the German Cities*, Vol. I, 375.

^2 Published under the title *The Reformation of the St. Catherine’s Convent in Nuremberg in the Year 1428* by **Theodor von Kern** in the 31st Annual Report of the Historical Society in Middle Franconia (Ansbach 1863).

^3 The incident related in *Formicarius*, Book V—which one may interpret as they wish—exerted, according to Nider’s account, a considerable influence on this shift in sentiment.

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the reformed St. Catherine’s Convent in Nuremberg, following the 1428 implementation of the "strict rule." It discusses the stability of the new leadership and the shift toward higher intellectual standards within the cloister.</meta>

...sufficiently justified by the circumstances. Among the "daughters of the city" <note>This refers to women born in Nuremberg who joined the local convent, highlighting the connection between the town's patrician families and the cloister.</note> besides her, there were **Gretlin Vornan** (who entered in 1419 and died in 1477 in Nuremberg) and **Greta Karthüserin** (who entered in 1422 and died in 1472 as a Prioress in Freiburg); the names of these three will encounter us several times on the following pages. The new Prioress, who had entered **Schönensteinbach** in 1418, remained in her office for 40 years and 2 months (died 1469), ensuring that there was no lack of stability in the convent's leadership. The spirit that prevailed in **St. Catherine’s Convent** under her direction was, of course, quite different from the earlier period. She was able to admit no fewer than 104 sisters, which put her in a position to assist in the reformation of a series of other convents—a process overseen by **Aegidius Schwertman**—in the same way that the reform of Nuremberg had been supported by the mother-house at Schönensteinbach.

However, a turn had taken place not only regarding monastic life; the entirety of intellectual striving received fresh encouragement.

**Denifle** <note>Heinrich Denifle (1844–1905) was a famous Dominican scholar who wrote extensively on the history of the order and German mysticism.</note> may be right when he considers the assumption of spiritual care <term>spiritual care: also known as the *cura monialium*, this refers to the duty of Dominican friars to provide confession, preaching, and guidance to the sisters.</term> in the nunneries as highly detrimental to the **Dominican Order**, in that so many of its most capable talents were withdrawn from their actual vocation <note>Denifle argued that friars spent too much time as chaplains to nuns instead of preaching to the public or studying.</note>; but the spirit of these scholarly confessors to the nuns was by no means lost without a trace. It is not merely individual Dominican nuns of that time who command our respect; rather, the mystical sermons <note>Deeply philosophical and spiritual teachings intended to lead the listener toward a direct experience of God.</note> presuppose a level of intellectual cultivation for the convents in general that is difficult for us to clearly imagine today. To be admitted into those convents, a pious mind and aptitude for practical work were by no means enough: a specific and, it seems, quite significant degree of intelligence and education was also required. To give just one example: In the year 1429, a wealthy Nuremberg woman, **Margaretha Riglerin**, had been admitted; the convent was well-satisfied with her and hoped to overcome her only fault, "that she [the] learning..." <note>The text breaks off here; the original phrase "das sie die lernunge" refers to her education or studies.</note>

<summary>This page details the growth and stability of St. Catherine’s Convent under its new Prioress, noting that the reform brought not only stricter discipline but also a high standard of intellectual and spiritual education for the nuns.</summary>
<keywords>St. Catherine’s Convent, Gretlin Vornan, Greta Karthüserin, Prioress, Schönensteinbach, Aegidius Schwertman, Heinrich Denifle, Dominican Order, spiritual care, Margaretha Riglerin, mysticism, Nuremberg</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the intellectual and spiritual standards at St. Catherine’s Convent in Nuremberg following its 1428 reform. It highlights the rigorous expectations for the sisters' education and the convent's growth as a center for book production.</meta>

...being able to overcome that which "comes hard" <note>original: "hart ankumet"</note>. However, after two years, the community found itself disappointed and dismissed them "on account of a lack of learning" <note>original: "propter defectum discendi"</note>. From the letters exchanged with **Johannes Nider** <note>A prominent Dominican reformer and author (c. 1380–1438) who was instrumental in the Observant movement.</note> and others regarding their departure from the **convent**, one can see that the stated reason was indeed the actual and only one ¹. Such a high regard not only for spiritual devotion but also for intellectual ability surely could only have developed under the influence of capable male advisors.

The spirit of **Schönensteinbach** <note>The influential reformed Dominican convent in Alsace that served as a model for Nuremberg.</note> dominated **St. Catherine’s Convent** from the reform onward. However, a difference can be perceived in one point: while the Alsatian community still exchanged letters with distant clergymen ², one notices little or nothing of that in **Nuremberg**; whatever was needed in the way of intellectual and spiritual encouragement was found right there on the spot. In contrast, they sought to copy every piece of spiritual German literature that could be obtained from anywhere and to incorporate it into their own **collection of books**. Some items were certainly brought into the cloister by the entering women, and some were donated by pious souls, but how many copies also went back out into the world from the convent's own **scriptorium** <note>A writing room where nuns meticulously hand-copied manuscripts.</note>! In the first thirty years after the **reform**—what was present before is explicitly stated in the book inventory—the mass of books and small volumes increased to such an extent that one

---

¹ The reason for the correspondence, which **Johannes Meyer** <note>A Dominican chronicler known for documenting the lives of the sisters and the progress of the reforms.</note> has preserved for us, was that the community wanted to withhold the assets already brought in—money, clothing, and books—to cover the costs of board and instruction. They certainly did not do so without some justification; however, all the men consulted decided in favor of a full refund, while nonetheless thoroughly approving of the reason for the dismissal.

<meta>This page continues the description of the library and literary culture at St. Catherine’s Convent in Nuremberg. It focuses on the practical organizational efforts made by the sisters to manage their growing collection of manuscripts for use in their daily communal life.</meta>

...evidently no longer knew how to find their way through the collection. They sought to remedy this drawback—in the 1450s or 1460s, in any case after 1456 and before the death of **Prioress Gertrud** (1469)—by uniting a number of small pieces of the same format and more or less similar content into larger volumes. They then compiled a catalog of the entire treasure with detailed information on the contents of the individual numbers, while simultaneously pasting shorter tables of contents into the volumes themselves. Their primary goal was the regulation of **mealtime reading** <note>original: "Tischlektüre," the practice of listening to spiritual texts while eating in silence.</note>, aiming to make it easy for the lector <note>The sister assigned to read aloud to the community.</note> to find a suitable text. The manuscript <note>abbreviated as "Hs." in the original German.</note> is now located at Cent. VII 79 <note>A shelfmark for the Nuremberg City Library.</note> and contains 168 paper leaves measuring 21 x 14.5 cm. It is bound in plain pigskin, upon which a later hand has written: "An old notebook <note>original: "notel"</note> of what was read during the year." On folio 2b is the instruction manual (see below, p. 114); on folios 3a to 87 follows the calendar with reading assignments for the individual days. So that the reader can form an idea of the arrangement, a sample is provided here.

<meta>This page continues the description of manuscript Cent. VII 79, moving from the calendar of readings to the structure and authorship of the catalog itself, noting the physical condition of the handwriting and the scope of the collection.</meta>

XXIII

Folio 86 then begins the catalog itself and extends to Folio 159. Folios 159–167a are blank; on Folio 167b, a note in the most recent hand can be found regarding books given away as gifts. (See below, page 160.)

The original catalog was written by one and the same hand, as were the additions (bracketed here). Both are somewhat negligent in their recording of phonetic sounds, which is particularly unpleasant in the case of names. The additions, which may have been entered 15 to 20 years later, come from an elderly person whose hand was unsteady and trembling, so that in some cases the script is barely legible.

The document does not present itself as an actual and complete library catalog, but only as a list of those books that were suitable for **table reading** <note>The practice of reading aloud to the community during silent meals.</note>. For this reason, one finds only scattered, smaller Latin pieces, while the monastery certainly did not entirely lack larger books in that language. Indeed, given the purpose of this list, it is not even out of the question that they possessed other writings in the German language as well¹.

But however that may be: the approximately 370 entries nevertheless constitute a most respectable collection of German books for that time, even when one takes into account that a considerable number of duplicates are among them. That the content is entirely religious in nature is not surprising; only a few entries, such as *The Runner* <note>A popular didactic-satirical poem about social classes and morality.</note> by <term>Hugo von Trimberg</term> [O, XLV] and two apparently polemical writings against the Jews [O, XXX] and against the <term>Waldenser</term> <note>A medieval Christian movement often declared heretical by the Catholic Church.</note> [O, V], form something of an exception. But what [exists] in this field, apart from Nether-

---

¹ That they continued to write busily in <term>St. Katharinen</term> even after the invention of the printing press is proven by a slip of paper still lying in the register today, which begins with the words: "Also, a spiritual man, named Brother Hans, lent 11 small books <note>original: 'puchlein'</note> here on the Monday nearest before [the feast of] the holy evangelist and apostle <note>original: 'zwelfpotentag,' literally 'twelve-apostle-day.'</note> Saint Matthew in the year [14]74." Something else had been written on the slip earlier, of which the words (Johan)nes Herolt are still fully legible. This suggests the suspicion that he was "Brother Hans." But how then can it be explained that in Sermon 85 of the *<term>Discipulus</term>* <note>The pen name of Johannes Herolt (c. 1390–1468), a Dominican theologian.</note> by Herolt, 1416 is given as the current year? Is the passage borrowed literally from somewhere else?

<meta>This page continues the detailed overview of the monastery's library collection, specifically focusing on the types of German-language religious texts the nuns collected and copied during the late Middle Ages.</meta>

XXIV

The nuns were essentially able to gather together what was produced in Germany during the 14th and 15th centuries. Thus, they possessed the following biblical writings:

A complete Bible [A 1-6], one Bible with the exception of the Prophets¹, five Gospel Harmonies (D 5, 8, 10, 15, 16), one Song of Songs (A 13), eight Psalters (C 1-5, 7-10), two Acts of the Apostles [A 15, D 5], two Apocalypses (A 15, 16), three History Bibles <note>A popular medieval genre that mixed biblical translation with historical commentary and legendary material.</note> (A 11, 12, 17), and eleven <term>Lectionaries</term> <note>Books containing specific passages of Scripture to be read on particular days of the liturgical year.</note> (Prophets, Epistles, Gospels, Passion) (D 1-4, 6-9, 11 (including the Gospel of Nicodemus) 12-14).

One can see that the biblical reading material was very extensive, and the translators certainly did not need to wait for the <term>Waldensians</term> <note>A pre-Reformation Christian movement often associated with early vernacular Bible translations, sometimes viewed as heretical by the Church.</note> to find a market for their wares!

In addition to the Lives of the Saints, the mystical literature of the 14th century is particularly well represented; they possessed at least the main works of **Eckhart**², **Tauler**, **Suso**, the book—

---

¹ This second Bible is merely a copy of the first; why the Prophets specifically were omitted, I am unable to discern. Perhaps this section interested the women the least, or perhaps it is merely a coincidence, as the sequence in which individual parts were copied appears somewhat strange. Specifically, three volumes of the younger copy, written by <term>Kunigund Niklasin</term> <note>A known scribe and nun at the St. Katherine’s convent in Nuremberg.</note>, are still extant: Cent. III 40 "the second book," 41 "the third book," and 43 containing the New Testament. All three bear the initials K. N. Number 40 was finished on **All Souls' Day** 1445, number 41 on the eve of **Corpus Christi** 1437, and number 43 on the Tuesday before **Michaelmas** 1443. Cent. III 42 belongs to the older Bible copy and is identical to A, V. (See page 115.) Accordingly, the information provided by Walther in *The German Bible Translation of the Middle Ages*, p. 310 f., which mentions only three volumes, should be corrected.

² One of Eckhart's sermons is explicitly recommended for **table reading** (see below, page 107, note 2). Around the middle of the 15th century, people were still making new copies not only of Tauler and Suso but also of Eckhart. For instance, the codex Cent. VI 46^t—the contents of which were partially identified by Denifle on a pasted-in leaf—contains a series of works by Eckhart in addition to pieces by Hane the Carmelite, those from the Sterngassen, etc. However, the scribe was clearly no longer entirely comfortable with it, for at the beginning she calls the book "difficult and unknown" <note>original: "swer und unbekant"</note> to many people. "Therefore one should not make it common, I pray for God's sake, because it was also forbidden to me. If there were anyone who wished to punish it, they should truly blame their own blindness. But if there is anything herein that one might punish in truth, then let it be known that it is not the fault of my unbelief, but rather the fault of my ignorance; therefore, interpret it for the best." <note>This note reflects the controversial status of Meister Eckhart's mystical writings, which faced ecclesiastical condemnation in the 14th century.</note>

<summary>This page catalogs the extensive biblical and mystical German-language manuscripts held by the convent, highlighting the nuns' active role in preserving and copying works by famous mystics like Meister Eckhart despite religious controversies.</summary>
<keywords>Bible translations, Gospel Harmonies, Lectionaries, History Bibles, Meister Eckhart, Johannes Tauler, Henry Suso, Kunigund Niklasin, Nuremberg, monastic libraries, women scribes, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the monastery library's holdings, transitioning from high medieval mystical works like those of Suso and Eckhart to the more practical, instructional literature favored in the 15th century.</meta>

XXV

...of the *Nine Rocks* <note>Referring to "The Book of the Nine Rocks," a major mystical work of the 14th-century "Friends of God" movement, often attributed to Rulman Merswin.</note> and so on. Most well-represented, however, was the work of the **Monk of Heilsbronn** <note>A 14th-century Cistercian author known for his didactic spiritual poems; Heilsbronn is located near Nuremberg.</note> titled *Of the Six Names of the Body of Christ* <note>original: "von den sechs Namen des Fronleichnams"</note>, which was also produced very close to Nuremberg.

This older collection was then continually supplemented by the works of the present day. Naturally, preference was given to the sermons and writings of those men who were personally close to the nuns in Nuremberg or Schönensteinbach <note>A prominent reformed Dominican convent in Alsace that served as a model for the Nuremberg community.</note>, such as **Nider**, **Herolt**, **Mülberg**, etc.

When one compares the intellectual works of these later men with those of their own fellow friars from the 14th century, it is almost difficult to understand how a single generation of the Order could find both equally excellent and edifying. **Nider** <note>Johannes Nider (c. 1380–1438), a Dominican theologian and reformer.</note>, for example, was certainly a prominent man in his circles, and in terms of their value to cultural history, his writings perhaps surpass the entire mystical literature of the 14th century. However, regarding idealism, he is surpassed by even the least significant mystic of the preceding century: the spirit has departed, the urge toward the heights has vanished, and one remains on the ground, contenting oneself with what is practically achievable in the <term>guidance of souls</term> <note>original: "Seelenleitung"; the practice of spiritual direction and moral counseling.</note>. Consequently, the literature becomes more sober, but also more understandable and useful for the average Christian; it takes on a strongly **catechetical** <note>Instructional, focusing on teaching the basic principles of Christian faith, duties, and morality.</note> character. Instructions on the duties and sins of man, on the institutions and customs of the Church, rules for living, and so forth, appear in previously unimagined numbers and reach the widest circles of the citizenry. Given the detail of our catalog, one can form a fair picture of the character of this instructional, prayer, and devotional literature of the 15th century with its help alone.

In the main, the sisters have [written/copied] the books themselves—

---

¹ From Section I, No. 8 (p. 133), it appears that Nider's *Twenty-four Golden Harps* grew out of sermons. Since people in Nuremberg were likely well-informed on this matter, and the Strasbourg edition of 1493 contains the same information, its accuracy can hardly be doubted.

<summary>This page describes a shift in the Nuremberg convent's library from 14th-century high mysticism to 15th-century "sober" instructional and catechetical literature. It highlights how the nuns prioritized works by contemporary spiritual advisors like Johannes Nider over more abstract mystical texts.</summary>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the monastery library’s growth, moving from the specific authors mentioned previously to the practical ways books were acquired—through gifts, dowries, and purchases—and how they were systematically organized.</meta>

XXVI

written; apart from the printed editions, only a few pieces were bought with money. More items were gifted to the monastery, and the individual nuns brought quite a lot with them; indeed, from ancient times, the "Psalter" <note>A book containing the Psalms, which was a standard prayer book for the laity</note> was part of a woman’s personal dowry. For some nuns, the number of books belonging to their personal collection was very significant for that time. For example, Katharina Tucher brought about 30 items with her into the monastery, including a history Bible <note>A popular medieval genre that summarized biblical history in the common tongue, often mixing it with secular history and legends</note> and a lectionary <note>A book containing specific scripture portions to be read during church services</note>. It does not appear—as there is no system at all in the selection—that these were written specifically for her <note>original: "ad hoc"</note>, but rather were handed down to her from the family library; for there can be no doubt that wealthy families like the Tuchers already possessed a considerable number of books of both spiritual and secular content at that time. Other works were gifted by male and female citizens, seemingly when they themselves could likely no longer use them due to their old age, such as a New Testament from "old Steffan Tetzel." (A 5.) ^1

Just as the book inventory sheds a welcome light on religious life in the 15th century, it is also of significant interest in its own right for the history of <term>librarianship|the professional organization and management of book collections</term>. The foresight in the planning and care in the execution are surprising for that period. First, the entire treasure of books was divided into 13 or 14 groups, designated with letters of the alphabet. The principle that governed this was, of course, not purely based on subject matter, but was a mixture of subject and form: while the nature of the content was generally decisive, it seems that special consideration was also given to the size of the books. Since the physical arrangement of the books undoubtedly corresponded to the order of the catalog,

---

^1 In the 15th century, New Testaments in the hands of educated citizens were not at all rare; however, a "Gospel Book" <note>A book containing the four Gospels, often translated into the local language</note> was more common. "A written German gospel book" <note>original: "Ein geschreven duitsch evangeliumboich"</note> was also found in the estate of the Cologne citizen Thonis Bertholt, who died in 1519. *Annals of the Historical Society for the Lower Rhine* <note>original: "Annalen des histor. Vereins für den Niederrhein"</note> 1844, page 120.

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the library's organization, moving from how books were acquired to how they were physically and thematically categorized within the collection.</meta>

<page-num>XXVII</page-num>

...naturally, one could make do with less space in this way. When producing <term>miscellany volumes|books containing multiple different works bound together</term>, extensive consideration for the physical format was required anyway, and for that reason alone, it was not possible to organize the grouping solely according to content.

In general, however, content was the deciding factor. **A** contains <term>Biblica|books of the Bible and related scriptural texts</term>, **B** catechetical writings <note>Instructional texts on the principles of Christian doctrine</note>, **C** Psalters, **D** <term>pericopes|specific passages of Scripture assigned to be read on particular days</term>, partly with interpretations, **E** sermons and religious tracts, **F** liturgy, **G** and **H** the rules and regulations of the **Dominican nunneries**, **J** lives of the saints, as well as writings and sermons by famous Dominicans, **K** law and medicine, **L** prayer and devotional books, **M** and **N** the same; the supplements to **N**, as well as **O**, contain miscellaneous items.

As stated, the principle was not carried out with absolute strictness—and, at least in the case of the miscellany volumes, it could not be carried out at all—but in general, it is clearly recognizable.

The list is particularly interesting because, wherever possible, it indicates the origin of the manuscripts or the source materials, the donors, and the female scribes; for printed works, the prices are also given. Thus, this list likely stands as quite unique among medieval catalogs due to the foresight in its design and the care in its execution.

A good number of the manuscripts are still held by the **Nuremberg City Library**, but many have also been lost. (See above, page 13, note 2.) To note this in each individual case, as well as to define the contents of each entry more precisely throughout and, where applicable, provide bibliographic references, would certainly have been very desirable; however, when one considers that not everything is still extant, and that what remains is shelved mixed with manuscripts of other origins, that the current catalog is of little use, and that the present list, despite all its detail, is often quite imprecise, one can easily see that such a task—if it were to be truly reliable—would essentially...

## No. 3. ¹

Folio 5. One should seek God with wandering <note>original: "irretume" — in this mystical context, it suggests a "straying" from the self or the ordinary path to find the divine</note> and with forgetfulness and with a state beyond the senses <note>original: "unsinnen" — referring to a mystical ecstasy where ordinary sensory perception is suspended</note>. For the <term>Godhead (gotheit): The fundamental essence of God</term> has the power of all things within itself and has nothing equal to it. Therefore, the highest clarity of the simple <term>being (wesen): The core essence or nature of a thing</term> must enlighten all
<margin>10</margin>
things. On this, Saint Dionysius <note>Saint Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century theologian whose mystical writings were foundational for medieval thought</note> says that beauty is that which is well-ordered with a withdrawn clarity. Therefore, the Godhead is a pull <note>original: "zok" — a drawing or attraction</note> of the three persons. The soul should be ordered with its lower powers under the highest, and with the highest under God, and with the external
senses <insert>under the internal</insert> and with the internal senses
<margin>15</margin>
under <term>reason (redelicheit)</term> and with thought under knowledge and with knowledge under the will into unity, so that the soul may be alone and that nothing may flow into it but the bare Godhead, which has flowed out into itself. On this, Saint Dionysius says that it has revealed its powers with a bare being, which the highest powers alone worked.
<margin>20</margin>
A wise master <note>Likely a reference to a Church authority or a philosopher like Aristotle</note> says this: As the highest power gains the upper hand in its works, so all others enter into it and lose their own work. There the soul stands in its order and in its bare being; that is its withdrawn clarity, which has the power of all things within it. A pagan master <note>A term used by medieval writers for Greek or Roman philosophers, often referring to Proclus or Plato in this context</note> says this: If the soul knows itself, then it knows
<margin>25</margin>
all things. So the Godhead has flowed into the Father and into the Son and into the Holy Spirit, and in eternity into itself, and in time into the creatures—into each as much as it is able to receive: to the stone its being, to the tree its growth, to the beast its feeling, to the angel its reason, and to humanity all four natures. Because of this, God became human, so that He [might reach] all things
<margin>30</margin>

¹ Repeats on Folio 79 b f.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly transcription of a Middle High German mystical treatise. <margin>5</margin>
...nature took in time through grace (5 b), as He had it in eternity by nature. On this Saint Paul says: "Christ is all things to me." <note>A paraphrase of Colossians 3:11: "Christ is all, and in all."</note> Then there occurs a shining and a reflecting of His own nature. God's <term>being (wesen): The fundamental essence or core existence of a thing</term> is a primary being, a flowing being, a firm and
<margin>10</margin>
primordial being, and is a perfect being. From all being, power flows into the work. Therefore, the three persons [of the Trinity] are a sustainer of the <term>Godhead (gotheit): The divine nature or essence considered as the source of the three persons</term>, and the three persons pour themselves into the being of the soul through grace. For God’s being follows the persons into the being of the soul,
<margin>15</margin>
and one being penetrates the other entirely. The highest power flows out of the being of the soul, just as the three persons flow out of the Godhead. And as God pours His grace into the soul, He pours it into the soul’s being. For no stain can fall into the being of the soul, regardless of what the [lower] powers do.
<margin>20</margin>
The highest power of the soul draws its virtue from the being of grace, which is there in the soul’s being, and the highest power goes out of the lowest into its being. The growing spirit and the following <insert>perceiving</insert> <note>original: "enphindend" — an editorial correction from Folio 80 of the manuscript</note> spirit of God’s nature is a following of Christ’s person and human nature.
<margin>25</margin>
Thus as the soul is drawn into the divine nature, everything she is in terms of flaws and imperfection is taken away from her, and she is "killed" <note>The mystical concept of "mortification," where the soul dies to its earthly self to be reborn in the divine.</note> in the divine nature, so that she takes into herself the divine nature, as the Father had within Himself. She does not take it from her own nature; she takes it from the divine nature into her nature; she takes
<margin>30</margin>
perfection and power, as Saint Paul says: "I can do all things through him who strengthens me." <note>Philippians 4:13</note> The wisdom that springs forth in the understanding, which is a path in the understanding and is fulfilled in the desire, has neither heart nor thought. Regarding this, Saint Dionysius says: As the soul takes an outflow and escapes into eternity and into time and its own understanding, it should then return
<margin>35</margin>
in the inflow, just as God flows back into the soul, and should not flow [away/out]. God flows back into Himself, so that He regards Himself as little as when they [created things] did not exist. Thus the soul should do. She should grasp the person of the Son with humanity, [and with the person of the Son] grasp the Father and the Holy Spirit in both of them, and both of them in the Holy Spirit, and with the person of the Father grasp the simple being, and with the being grasp the <term>abyss (abgrunt): The infinite, "bottomless" depth of God's essence</term> and should vanish into the abyss without matter and without form. Matter and form and understanding and the being she has lost in the unity, for she has become nothing in herself. For God works all her works (6 a) and holds her in His being and leads her in His power into the bare Godhead; there she flows with the bare Godhead into all that God flows into. And she is the place of all things.

and she <note>The soul is traditionally referred to as "she" (feminine) in German and Latin theological texts.</note> has no place of her own. This is the essence <note>original: "iest" — a Middle High German term for "being" or "existence"</note> of wisdom, which has neither heart nor thought. The soul flows into the <term>Godhead (gothait): The divine essence, often distinguished by mystics from the personhood of God</term> so closely that many people are deceived by it; for whatever she is, she is by <term>grace (gnaden): A free gift from God, as opposed to something earned or part of one's natural state</term>, and where she is, she is by the power of another; yet she flows into the Godhead so

<margin>10</margin> and God’s will shall be one: wherever God may be, there we should be with God. No one can come to this while in this body, except as God gives the final gift that He gives to the soul, which is the vision <note>original: "anplich" — a sight or direct gaze</note> of the Godhead; then the soul is strengthened in the Trinity.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul’s mystical union with the Godhead. The previous page discussed how the Trinity pours into the soul's essence; here, the author describes the indistinguishable closeness of this union and cites Saint Paul to explain the transformation into the divine image. ...takes in every physical sense, then he is not a human, but is <insert>he</insert> **animalistic**. As long as a human is [merely] human, he is not spirit. When the <insert>spirit</insert> works in all his powers and in all his dwelling <note>original: "wenuge" — referring to the state of being or the inner "room" of the soul</note>, then for the first time is the human truly spiritual. As is said of humanity, there are two senses to be noted: a spiritual sense and a physical sense. A human who is spiritual follows the spiritual sense in every way and "clothes" every creature—he does not see them [in] their own [limited] form, but rather he brings them always through his **reason** <note>original: "redlicheit" — rational or moral integrity</note> into a divine form. From this it happens that people so easily become greedy for a word they hear or for other small things: that comes from them receiving every thing through the raw sense. Such people [never] become spiritual. When a human is a true spiritual human, he is still as far from God as "nothing" is from "something." Nothing stands far [from God] by nature. Thus, if a human is to become one with God, that can happen in no other way than through the **heat of love** <note>original: "heiz der minne" — "Minne" is the Middle High German term for mystical, courtly, or divine love</note>.

This word spoke Master Eckhart and said: "If we were to say what a true human were, we would find few humans. That would be a just human who was burned through with divine love, so that he had **divine knowledge** <note>original: "wekantnůz"</note>. As long as you are not that, as long as you [are] no human, you are like a painted human <note>i.e., a mere representation or facade of a person, lacking the inner divine life</note>."

This word spoke Master Eckhart: "If you take God in His goodness or His mercy, then you take God in the 'fall' <note>original: "valle" — referring to God as He is manifested in the world of variety and attributes, rather than in His absolute essence</note>. He who takes God in the outflowing, where He is in the soul, he takes Him quite rightly."

This word he also spoke: "Where the Father has many **childbeds** <note>original: "chindelbette" — a metaphor for the many places or moments within the soul where the Divine Word is born</note>, there the space is narrow and the covering is small; otherwise it must always fall away. Everything that is not God must fall away."

This he also spoke: "Wherever anything foreign is, there God is not born." Master Eckhart spoke this sermon.
