## No. 9.

### Folio 14. I saw the holy city Jerusalem <note>Latin original: "Vidi civitatem sanctam Jherusalem," from the Book of Revelation 21:2</note>.

Saint John saw in the spirit a city that was holy and called Jerusalem; the city was new, it came down here from heaven and was made of gold and was adorned like a bride for her husband.

I want to apply this to the soul. The Son has been eternally in the Father, and He begets His Son without ceasing, and the birth is always new. Whatever is at its beginning is new. A house that was made yesterday is new today, for it is near to its beginning.

<summary>This page captures several aphorisms and sermon fragments by Meister Eckhart focusing on the "spiritual sense" and the necessity of stripping away non-divine attachments to allow the "birth of the Son" in the soul. It concludes by introducing a sermon on the New Jerusalem, symbolizing the soul's eternal renewal.</summary>

<keywords>Meister Eckhart, spiritual sense, divine love, birth of the Son, New Jerusalem, mysticism, reason, divine knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the soul's relationship to the Divine. While the previous page discussed the soul's transformation through the "heat of love" and the role of human reason, this page shifts focus to the role of grace and the "natural light" within the soul, explicitly citing the teachings of Meister Eckhart.</meta>

God created the soul in his only-begotten Son and formed it in him and saw it in him, how it pleased him: and it pleased him well. The soul, which ought to be new, should hold itself as a medium <note>original: "mittel" — meaning both the center point and the means of connection</note> in God and re-form itself in his only-begotten Son and be ready to receive the inflow from God without ceasing.

<margin>5</margin> Our Lord was asked who Saint John was, and whether he was a prophet. He is more than a prophet: everything that the prophets ever prophesied happened in a natural course. Saint John was drawn so far above nature that all creatures were too coarse for it, such that they could not receive his works.

<margin>10</margin> Johannes <note>The name John</note> is interpreted as "grace." Now a small question was asked in our school: that grace became manifold. I answered to this and said: it does not flow out as a tiny drop, but rather a spark falls from grace into the soul, which has so much power within it that from it spring the powers of the soul—knowledge and faith and love—which are
<margin>15</margin> moved. What is grace? Grace, as grace is in itself, does not work outward; rather, it works inward. Were there a person who had but one tiny drop of this grace, he would have more good and [be] more inwardly joined into the **essential being** <term>redlich wesen: the soul's rational and true essence</term> without works; thus, even if he never performed any work and slept all the time, he would still be nearer to God and more inward in God. I say: were there a person who
<margin>20</margin> let himself be burned a hundred times a day purely for God, all his works could not help him to be joined into the **unborn being** <note>original: "ungeborn wesen" — a central Eckhartian concept referring to the uncreated part of the soul that is one with God</note> as much as this person without works. What is grace? Grace works within the ground of the soul; where no birth was ever thought of, there grace works in and works so far in that the three are one being. God and
<margin>25</margin> grace are so alike: wherever God goes forth, he carries grace on his back. This is what **Master Eckhart** says.

The prophet says: "Rejoice, you who do not bear fruit; those [of you] are many, they are well a thousand times more than those who are bearing fruit in the world; they are many beyond number." <note>original: refers to Isaiah 54:1 or Galatians 4:27; the author interprets the "barren" soul as the one most filled with divine potential.</note> The soul has a **natural light** within it. In this natural light, God has more delight and more satisfaction than
<margin>30</margin> in all creatures he ever created; he adorns all his power in the natural light. Take a piece of black coal: as unlike as it would be against the heavens, so are all creatures compared to the natural light that the soul carries within it. When the soul is carried into that light, it bears itself and its self within itself, and bears itself against itself into itself. It
<margin>35</margin> loses all that "birth" <note>original: "gebûrt" — likely referring to physical or worldly becoming</note> so entirely and becomes so completely raised above itself and becomes so completely inclined, one into one. It comes to the point that it bears God, as...

<summary>This page explores the nature of grace and the soul's inner "natural light," arguing that a single spark of divine grace is more powerful than any outward religious works. It features a specific attribution to Master Eckhart regarding the oneness of God and grace within the ground of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, grace, ground of the soul, natural light, divine birth, John the Baptist, essential being, inwardness</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul’s mystical union with God. The previous page discussed the "natural light" of the soul and the inward work of grace, which draws the soul closer to the essential being of God regardless of outward works. This page delves into the "birth" of God within the soul and the superiority of the intellect over the will in the process of union.</meta>

God births himself; and there a true union occurs across all creatures, across the angels, across God himself, so that he finds a unique distinction there.

Some masters seek **blessedness** <term>selicheit: a state of supreme spiritual happiness or beatitude</term> in knowledge or in the will:
<margin>5</margin> I say that blessedness lies neither in knowledge nor in the will. Blessedness is this: that the soul leaves all blessedness behind—that is, all its own "selfhood." The heavens perform all their works for this reason: that they wish to be like God; not that they wish to be like Him in their outward works, but rather they seek rest, just as **being** <term>wesen: the pure, essential existence of a thing</term>
<margin>10</margin> is without work. The heavens seek the same thing: to come into a silent standing. If the heavens and other creatures, which are lesser, seek this, what then should we do? There God remains God, there blessedness remains blessedness, grace remains grace, and the soul remains soul.

Meister Eckhart said: God is a speech without speech and a word without word, and in that becoming, all creatures become alive and
<margin>15</margin> growing. Who has spoken the word and spoken the speech? The heavenly Father has spoken it in his only-begotten Son. Can no one else speak that word [and that speech]? No, no one can speak it but the heavenly Father, and yet it is spoken. When is it spoken and where is it spoken? When the soul finds no
<margin>20</margin> satisfaction in any creature and has carried itself entirely into God with all its works and has forgotten itself and intends God purely; there God gives more than the soul could ever imagine. Since it has carried itself so purely into God, God gives Himself to it in such a way that He performs the soul's work in it without effort, so that the soul is a co-worker with God.

<margin>25</margin> And where is it spoken? When the very highest part of the [soul], bare and free, is joined at once with God, there the word and the speech are spoken, and there mouth has come to mouth and there kiss has come to kiss <note>A common mystical metaphor for the most intimate union between the soul and the Divine</note>, and the soul understands the word in the word and no one else; and the soul could also speak something in response. Here the soul has come to its
<margin>30</margin> very highest. That this may happen to us, may God help us.

### No. 10.

<margin>Fol. 14 b.</margin> Meister Eckhart said: Some masters ask whether **understanding** <term>verstantnuzze: the intellect or cognitive faculty</term> is nobler, or love. Some masters say that love is nobler than understanding. I say that this is not so.
<margin>35</margin> Our best masters <note>Eckhart likely refers to Thomas Aquinas and the Dominican tradition here</note> say that understanding is nobler than love. Love and will take God insofar as He is good. If God were not good, the will would not take Him; if God were not lovable, love would not take Him. Understanding does not do this. Understanding turns neither to...

<summary>Meister Eckhart describes the soul's union with God as a "silent standing" where God speaks His Word directly into the highest part of the soul. He argues that the intellect is superior to the will because it seeks God's pure being rather than just His goodness or lovability.</summary>
<keywords>Meister Eckhart, blessedness, mystical union, the Word, intellect vs. will, selfhood, divine birth</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the sermon's exploration of the soul's relationship with God, moving from the rejection of outward works to the inner "ground" of the soul. Having established that blessedness is found in "being" rather than "doing," the text now describes how the intellect strips away all external attributes of God to reach His bare essence.</meta>

— 7 —

goodness, nor love, nor wisdom, nor dominion. Everything that the soul attributes to God by name (15ᵃ), God pulls a skin <note>original: "haut"; meaning a veil or a covering that obscures the truth</note> over it, and it veils God; the intellect <term>verstantnuzze: the faculty of understanding or reason, often viewed by Eckhart as the highest power of the soul</term> does not do this. Even if God were neither good nor wise, the intellect would still take Him; it strips everything away. <margin>5</margin> It turns neither to wisdom, nor goodness, nor dominion, nor power. It strives toward the bare essence <term>wesen: pure, essential existence</term> and takes God purely, before He is clothed in thoughts of wisdom and goodness. Where the Son receives his blessedness <term>selicheit: supreme spiritual happiness</term> in the Father, there the intellect receives its blessedness.

Now I wish to speak something that has never been read and never yet been preached! <margin>10</margin>

For yesterday it was read in the school: there is a ground <term>bodem: the innermost, uncreated essence of the soul</term> in the soul that is like the Fatherhood. Just as the Father is bringing forth the Son in the Holy Spirit, and the three are one God, so this ground is bringing forth intellect and will, and yet is one power, just as God is one Word. And the ground is so pure <margin>15</margin> that no harm from any creature can enter into it. Everything that one can say of the soul is merely an appendage of the ground, and in that ground, God looks at the soul, and the soul looks at God. Now St. Paul speaks: "When we look upon and see the glory of God, we shall be transformed from clarity to clarity and be as one with the image." <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 3:18: "And we all, who with unveiled faces contemplate the Lord’s glory, are being transformed into his image with ever-increasing glory."</note> This image becomes so united with God that no creature can <margin>20</margin> separate them, nor does God Himself wish to separate them. And alas! That we regard that image so lightly, that we do not maintain it in such great purity <term>lautercheit: absolute spiritual clarity and simplicity</term> as God has given it to us! For in all that we can do, God is not satisfied unless we stand there in the bareness <term>blozheit: a state of total detachment from all things</term> of all things, so that God may at all times and without ceasing work His only-begotten Son in this ground. <margin>25</margin> This birth happens not once a year, nor at one hour, nor on one day, but at all times beyond time, in the "while" where there is neither "here" nor "now." This is the ground of which I have spoken. That we may so live that God must work in us, may God help us.

I have sometimes spoken saying that the soul is the after-image <margin>30</margin> <term>nachbilde: a reflection or secondary copy</term> of God; now I say that she is the image itself, and from the same image that the Father has eternally formed, and from the same from which Christ is formed.

Meister Eckhart says this: All creatures have a contradiction <term>widerslac: literally a 'counter-blow'; the logical distinction or negation that separates one created thing from another</term> within themselves: one denies that it is the other; the highest angel denies that he is the lowest. God is a "negation of negation"; <margin>35</margin> the One denies all otherness. When we name a tree as a tree, that names nothing; for all the senses are deceived. When we name it as it is in its first outburst <term>usbruch: the primary emanation or flowing out of a thing from its source in God</term> (15ᵇ) in its purity, then we name...

<summary>This page describes the "ground" of the soul where God eternally births His Son, asserting that the soul's intellect seeks God's bare essence beyond all names or attributes. Eckhart emphasizes that the soul is not merely a reflection of God but shares the same "image" as Christ, existing in a state beyond time and space.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul’s ascent to God. Having previously discussed how the intellect strips away attributes to reach God’s "bare essence," the text now moves into a discussion of the "Pharisee" as a figure of separation and the inherent potential within the soul to "become God." It introduces authorities like Master Eckhart, Brother Johannes, and Bishop Albertus Magnus to support these mystical claims.</meta>

— 8 —

...not merely as a tree; we call Him the bare divine nature. Another sense, which is a little better, is above that. The divine nature sets itself apart purely. Master Eckhart says this: "The Pharisee asked our Lord to eat with him." What is the Pharisee? It is as much as to say "a separation" <note>The word Pharisee is derived from the Hebrew *parash*, meaning to separate or set apart; here used to mean one who is separated from the world and joined to the One.</note>; that is a Pharisee: that which is separated from all things and is joined to the One. The prophet says: "Lord, have mercy on the work that is in You." The highest work that God ever wrought happened in mercy <term>barmherzicheit: literally "warm-heartedness for the poor," the MHG term for divine compassion or mercy</term>. Knowledge carries one so far in that it takes the essence <term>wesen: the pure, essential being of a thing</term> as the essence in itself; but where the essence is losing itself and sinking into itself, there knowledge falls away.

Brother Johannes says this: "There is a question among the masters whether the 'power' <note>original: "mugen"; the capacity or potentiality</note> to become God exists in the soul or in God. I say that this power is in the soul. For it to happen that the soul can become God, God must work within that power and must draw that power into Himself: therefore, the work is in God and the power is in the soul. If I were all that [I?] am, then I would be God, for then there would be neither time nor place nor any changeability <term>wandelhafticheit: the state of being subject to change or decay</term> in me. Nothing is as easy or as possible for me as being God; so I remain in the work, and I require no external work for it; rather, if I were to become something else, I would have to become nothing: now I remain that which I am."

I say that no creature is so lowly <note>original: "snôd"; wretched or insignificant</note> that it cannot fulfill a "being"; as far as it can fulfill being, it is possible for it to be God, for everything that is "being" is God.

<margin>24 Silscholf Albrecht.</margin>
Bishop Albrecht <note>referring to Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a prominent scholar and teacher of Thomas Aquinas</note> says: "Only then is a person a divine person, when they receive the divine influence without hindrance." A saint says: "The light of my soul was never extinguished, and my desire stood without ceasing in the contemplation of God."

John. He came as a witness to the light. He was not the light, but rather he held the witness of the light. That was the true light, which enlightens all people who come into this world. He was in the world, and the world was made through him, and the world knew him not. ### Number 15.

Folio 22, verso. Jesus lifted up his eyes and looked into heaven and spoke: Father, the time has come; glorify your son, so that your son may glorify you in return. <margin>20</margin>

*On wisdom: how the soul is carried into God with divine wisdom.*

Saint Augustine also spoke: that all the words and the teachings of the humanity of God <note>The "humanity of God" refers to the Incarnate Christ</note> are an image and a figure of our Lord’s life and of great dignity before God. <margin>25</margin>

The soul must be purified and made refined <term>chleinlich: literally "small," but here meaning simplified or reduced to its essential, spiritual point</term> in [the] light of grace, and everything that is foreign to the soul must be separated and stripped away <term>geschelt: literally "peeled," like removing the rind from fruit to reach the core</term>, including even a part of what the soul is itself. I have spoken of it before: The soul must be so completely stripped <term>geplozzet: made naked or bared</term> of everything that has been added to it <margin>30</margin> and thus be carried up in purity and flow back into the Son, just as it flowed out in him. For the Father created the soul in the Son; therefore it must flow back into him just as it flowed out from him. Now he says: "He lifted up his eyes." In that little word lie two senses: the one is a demonstration of the pure Trinity. If we are ever to come...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Gospel of John, specifically the passage where Jesus lifts his eyes to heaven (John 17:1). It explores the mystical concept of the "ground" of the soul and the analogy of celestial influence on the natural world, moving from the previous page's introduction of the sermon.</meta>

->— 10 —<-

into the ground <term>grunt: a central mystical term referring to the deepest, uncreated part of the soul where it is one with God</term> of God and into his innermost being; so we must first come into our own ground and our innermost being in pure Trinity. The masters say that the stars pour all their power into the ground of the earth and into nature and the elements of the earth and work the purest 
<margin>5</margin> gold. 
As far as the soul comes into the ground and into the innermost part of its being, so far does the divine power pour itself entirely into it and works quite hiddenly and openly very great and very high things in the love of God, which is likened to pure gold. That is the first sense. 
<margin>10</margin> The second sense is that the soul should carry itself with all its flaws and its sins in humility and should seat itself and bow down under the gates of the dignity of God, where God melts out in mercy, and should also carry all the virtue and good work that is in it, and should seat itself therewith (23 a) under the gates where God 
<margin>15</margin> melts out in a good way. Thus the soul should follow and order itself according to the image. That is: "He lifted up his eyes." <note>A reference to John 17:1, "Jesus lifted up his eyes to heaven."</note>
A master says, he who is skillful and well-versed in it, could order water over wine in such a way that the power of the wine can work within it. In this way, the power of the wine turns water into wine, and if it were well- 
<margin>20</margin> ordered over the wine, it would become better than the wine; yet at the very least it becomes as good as the wine. Thus it is in the soul, which is well-ordered in the ground of humility and thus comes up and is drawn up into the divine power, which never rests until it comes directly upon God and touches him "nakedly" <note>original: "blozz" — meaning unmediated or without the interference of images and concepts</note> and remains thus within (this is also the soul's essence, 
<margin>25</margin> for God is a pure essence). A master says: In God, who is a pure essence, nothing at all enters unless it be pure essence. Therein is also the soul's essence, which has come directly upon God and into God. Therefore he says: He lifted up his eyes from below and looked into heaven. 
A Greek master <note>Likely a reference to Aristotle or a Neoplatonic commentator</note> said: The heaven signifies as much as a 
<margin>30</margin> skin <note>original: "hût" — can mean skin, hide, or a protective covering</note> of the sun, for the heaven pours its power into the sun and into the stars, and the stars pour their power in the midst upon the earth and work gold and stones so that the stone has power to work wondrous works. Some have the power that they draw to themselves bone and iron. <note>A reference to the magnetic properties of loadstones</note> Every stone and herb <term>chůrtel [krůtel]: Middle High German for "Kräutel," meaning small herbs or plants</term> is a little house of the sun, which 
<margin>35</margin> has enclosed within itself a heavenly power. Just as the heaven pours its power into the stars, so the stars pour it further into the herbs and into the animals. The herb is nobler than the stone, for it has a growing <note>original: "gewaschendez" — related to the word for waxing or growing</note> life. It could not grow under the physical heaven unless there were a rational power within it, from which it receives its life. Just as the...

<meta>This page continues the mystical commentary on the Gospel of John, specifically Chapter 17. It transitions from the physical influence of celestial bodies to the soul’s intellectual ascent into the "naked sight" of God. The text explores the relationship between the Father and the Son and the nature of "eternal life" as the knowledge of God's unity.</meta>

The lowest angel pours his power into the heavens and sets them in rotation and works through them; just so, the heavens pour their power quite hiddenly into every herb and into the animals. Because of this, every thing has a quality of the heavens and works all around itself in a circle <note>original: "sinibel", meaning spherical or circular, reflecting the medieval view of the cosmos</note> just as the heavens do.

<margin>5</margin>Animals walk more firmly upon the earth and have sensory life, yet they remain within time and within space. However, the soul steps beyond in her highest natural light, beyond time and beyond space, into the likeness of the light of the angels, and works with them intellectually <term>vernunfticlich: referring to the power of the "intellect" or "reason," which mystics viewed as the highest faculty that can perceive God</term> in the heavens. In this way, the soul should climb upward in all things through the working of the intellect. Wherever she finds divine light <margin>10</margin>and divine likeness, there she should keep watch and not turn back until she has climbed even higher. And thus she should raise herself further into the divine light and so come beyond every veil into the pure, naked sight of God with the angels in heaven. Therefore He says: He lifted up his eyes and looked into heaven and spoke: Father, the hour has come, glorify <note>original: "clarweiz," from the Latin "clarifica," meaning to make bright, clear, or glorious</note> your son, <margin>15</margin>that your son may glorify you.

<margin>20</margin>How the Son glorifies the Father and how the Father glorifies the Son—of this it is better to be silent than to speak; they would need to be angels who should speak of it. But concerning the little word (23 ᵇ) I wish to speak a bit, where He says: "All those whom you have given to me." To the one who looks at this according to its true sense, it means this much: "All that you have given me, I give to them: eternal life." That is the same life that the Son has in the first outflowing <term>auzburch: a technical term for the emanation or "outbursting" of the Son from the Father</term> and in the same ground <note>referring to the "grunt" or the shared essence of the Trinity</note> and in the same purity and in the same taste <note>original: "gesmak," implying experiential or "tasted" knowledge</note> in which he has his own blessedness and in which he possesses his own being. I give them eternal life, and no other. This sense I have sometimes spoken of <margin>25</margin>generally, but tonight I leave it, and it lies specifically in the Latin <note>The author refers to the original Vulgate text of John 17</note>, as I have always said: you are in Him yourself and speak clearly to my body!

This is eternal life: that they recognize you as the only true God. If two recognized one God, and one recognized a thousand, and the other <margin>30</margin>recognized God alone—no matter how small that recognition might be—he recognized God more than those thousand did.

The more God is recognized as One, the more He is recognized. If my soul were sensory and were noble and pure, she would recognize that it was nothing other than recognizing an angel; and if there were ten such souls and they recognized another angel who was nobler, it would be the same thing. Therefore says Saint Augustine: "If I recognized <margin>35</margin>all things and recognized not God, I would have recognized nothing; but if I recognize God and no other thing, then I have recognized all things." The closer and deeper one recognizes the One, the more one recognizes the little roots from which all things have sprouted. The more one recognizes the root and the kernel and the...

---

It defines eternal life as the experiential knowledge of God's absolute unity, citing St. Augustine to emphasize that knowing God is equivalent to knowing the root of all existence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the nature of God and the soul's union with the Divine, likely following a discussion on the angelic choirs and the soul's interior stillness.</meta>

The ground of the Godhead is better known as one, the more one knows all things. Therefore He says: "That they might know Thee, the only true God," <note>original: "daz man dich erchenne allein einen waren got"; a reference to John 17:3</note> and he means that the soul should strip away everything that one attributes to God in thought or in understanding, and take Him purely as He is, a pure being: thus He is true. Therefore our Lord says: "This is eternal life, that they know Thee, the only true God." That we may come to the truth, may God help us.

### No. 16.

->Stand in the gate of the Lord’s house and preach the word. <note>original Latin: "Sta in porta domus domini et prædica verbum." Reference to Jeremiah 7:2.</note> Pfeiffer 120 ff.<-

### No. 17.

<margin>Folio 24a.</margin> Lord, it is well-fitting for the house that it be holy, wherein one praises Thee, that it be a house of prayer for the length of days.

I mean the "days" here, when I say: a length without length, that is a length without breadth; when I say: "all time," I mean beyond time; rather, everything beyond what I just spoke of, where there is neither [here] nor now. A woman asked our Lord where one should pray. Then our Lord said: "The time shall come, and is now, that the true worshippers shall worship God in spirit and in truth." <note>John 4:23</note> Because God [is] a spirit, therefore one should worship Him in spirit and in truth. He who is Truth itself—that we are not; rather, we are "true," and alongside that there is something untrue. It is not so in God. Rather, in the first breaking-forth, where the truth breaks out and springs forth in the gates of God’s house, there the soul shall stand and shall speak out and bring forth the Word, as it is within her, [and] shall speak and praise; and no one shall hear the voice in the stillness and in the repentance. As I just spoke of the angels who sit there in the choirs of truth, they are [the] throne of God, in which God rests. In this stillness and in this rest the soul shall stand, and in the gate, there God speaks into the soul and speaks Himself entirely into her. There the Father brings forth His only-begotten Son and has such great delight in the Word, and it is so very dear to Him, that He never ceases; He speaks the Word at all times, that is, beyond time. It fits well with our words when we say: Holiness becomes Thy house, and that one praises Thee therein, and that nothing be therein except that which praises Thee.

Our masters speak and ask: what praises God? That is done by <margin>(24b)</margin> likeness. Just as everything that is "like" [God] in the soul praises God; whatever is in any way unlike God does not praise God. Just as a picture praises its master who has impressed it into itself and made it so very much like him: the likeness of the picture praises its master without words. That one can praise God with words, that is—

<summary>This page explores the mystical concept of "likeness" to God, arguing that the soul praises God not through external words but by becoming a "house of prayer" where the Father eternally speaks the Word (the Son) into the soul's stillness.</summary>
<keywords>Godhead, eternal life, house of God, spirit and truth, only-begotten Son, divine likeness, stillness, Meister Eckhart</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on prayer and the soul's union with God, likely following a discussion on the internal versus external nature of devotion.</meta>

small, or pray only with the mouth. Our Lord said: "You pray, but you do not know what you pray." <note>original: "Ir betet, aber ir enwizzet niht, waz ir betet." A paraphrase of John 4:22.</note> What is prayer? Dionysius <note>Refers to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential Christian mystic.</note> says: A rational climbing up into God, that is prayer. A pagan <note>Likely referring to a philosopher like Aristotle or Plato.</note> says: Where there is spirit and unity and eternity, there God works; where there is flesh against <margin>5</margin> spirit, where there is distraction against unity, where there is time against eternity, there God does not work—rather, He can do nothing with it.

I say: All [joy?] and all sufficiency and bliss and all the world that one may have here, that must all pass away! He who wishes to praise God must be holy and gathered <term>gesammet: a state of inner focus and stillness</term> and one spirit, and nowhere outside himself, but rather everything equally raised up into the eternal eternity. Up above everything—I mean <margin>10</margin> not only all creatures that are created, but everything that He could create if He wished—above that the soul shall come. As long as anything is before the soul, and as long as anything is before God that is not God, she does not come into the ground <term>grunt: the deepest, essential core of the soul where it meets God</term> of the length of days. Augustine says: When the light of the soul shines upon <margin>15</margin> the creatures, from which they take their being, he calls that a "morning"; and as the light of the angel shines upon the light of the soul, he calls that a "mid-morning." David says: The path of the righteous man is wisdom and increases into a full midday. The path is beautiful and pleasing and joyful and secret; but as the divine light shines upon the light <margin>20</margin> of the angel, and the light of the soul and the light of the angel flow into the divine light like a river, he calls that the "midday." Then the day is at its highest, and the sun pours her radiance into the stars, and the stars pour their radiance into the moon, so that it is all ordered under the sun. So has the divine light enclosed the light of the angel and the light of the soul within itself, so that it <margin>25</margin> is all ordered and stands upright; and there it praises God at all times. There is nothing more that does not praise God, and everything stands divine; the more equal [to God], the more full of God, and it praises God altogether. Our Lord said: "I shall dwell with you in your house." We ask our Lord for this, that He dwell with us here, so that we may dwell with Him eternally in His house and be eternally <margin>30</margin> blessed with Him. May God help us to this.

Folio 24b. One reads in the Holy Scripture of four ways of coming to God. The first is flying, as Ezekiel the prophet saw the Seraphim flying to God. The second is climbing, as Jacob the patriarch [saw] on the ladder. The third is driving, as Elijah the prophet in the fiery chariot. <margin>35</margin> The fourth is running on the feet like Moses.

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the life of Moses, specifically focusing on his encounter with the burning bush and the nature of the soul's ascent to God.</meta>

taken from the waters. The second, that he was a thoroughly sensible <note>original: "vernunftig" - meaning wise or possessing spiritual discernment</note> man. The third, that he drove and pastured his little sheep in the inner pasture of the wilderness. The fourth, that he took off his shoes before the bush where God was.

<margin>5</margin> The first—that Moses was taken from the water—[signifies] that the person has taken themselves away from the false world and from everything within it that is evil and unblessed. The second—that Moses was gentle—means that a person should be of such a gentle spirit that they overcome all adversity. The third is that Moses drove his little sheep to the <margin>10</margin> inner pasture of the wilderness; this means that a person, with all their senses and all the powers of their soul, should turn upward toward the mountains and the wilderness of the high Godhead and there feed their little sheep. The fourth is that Moses loosened his shoes before the bush. The shoe is made of dead leather and signifies that the soul should loosen itself from all mortal <margin>15</margin> and perishing things, and soar with the higher part of the soul above all things that the heavens cover—for they all carry death within them and are vanity—and should observe a nakedness <note>spiritual poverty or detachment</note> from all things to hear what God wishes to speak with her. Therefore our Lord spoke to Moses: "Take off your shoes!" That is the feet of your soul; that is to say: your understanding <margin>20</margin> and your desire should not be wrapped up in any mortal thing if God is to speak with them at all.

->What path does the Godhead have, or where can it travel, since it<-
has no feet nor anything that is physical? The path of the Godhead is unity, where the three Persons <note>The Trinity</note> walk within one another in one essence. <margin>25</margin> The "walking" of the Persons is that they know and love one another. Each one recognizes and loves itself in the other; thus the Persons walk in unity among one another.

The feet with which the Godhead goes or walks into the Persons, as the Persons [walk] into the essence: one foot of the Godhead is the providence <term>providence: God's foreknowledge and care for the world</term> of all things; the <margin>30</margin> other foot is the preservation of the foreseen things.

Now one might ask: What pleasure could fall upon God [from his creation], since all things must please him from nothingness? For that which he saw there was God. To this, Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential Christian mystic</note> says: God saw himself in all things; therefore God is pleased with himself, for God is in himself a single good. <margin>35</margin> Therefore the soul should behold its simple image in God, which never came out [of Him], thus stripped of all things. For the perfection of the spirit lies in this: that the "something" which He has created comes to meet its "nothingness," which is the image. Just as God is nothing but spirit, so too is the image "nothing" <note>In mystical theology, "nothingness" refers to being stripped of worldly qualities to match God's transcendent nature</note> in him, in which...

Dyonisius"; likely Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-6th century theologian influential in medieval mysticism</note> says: The greatest power that the spirit possesses is that it flows away into the "nothingness" of its own image and dissolves therein; there the spirit loses its works, its form, and its senses, but not its being. Yet the essence of the Godhead has drawn the bare being of the spirit out of itself into Itself and made it like unto Itself, so that nothing appears but one single being. Thus the spirit loses its works and its form and its senses. Thus the bare essence of the Godhead has swallowed the spirit into itself, so that 
<margin>10</margin> nothing remains but the bare spark, which is called <term>mind</term> <note>original: "mens"; the highest, most intuitive part of the soul in medieval philosophy</note>. Regarding this, Saint Dionysius says that the Godhead has become as nothing to all the powers of the soul. Dionysius means by this that the bare essence of the Godhead has drawn the bare spark of the spirit into itself, and yet the spirit finds no bottom <note>or "ground"</note> in that essence. Paul acknowledged this when he was drawn into the third heaven and 
<margin>15</margin> saw such things and heard such words that one cannot easily speak, and he cried out with a loud voice: "O the depth of the riches of the wisdom and the knowledge of God! How unsearchable are His judgments and how inscrutable are His ways!" <note>A translation of Romans 11:33</note>

## Number 19.

**Folio 25 b. O the depth of the riches of wisdom.**
<margin>20</margin> The richness of God is and is not, and possesses nothing that can be expressed in words. The wisdom of God is the knowing of all things before they happen. The coming of God is the inclining of Himself in a hovering light. Regarding this, Saint Dionysius says: The light in which God dwells is His own essence, which is known to no one but Himself. That is the high way of the Godhead, in which no creature has ever walked. Regarding this, God says: "My 
<margin>25</margin> ways shall be above yours as the heavens are above the earth." <note>A translation of Isaiah 55:9</note> A saint says regarding this that nothing is more useful, nor more spiritual, nor more blessed for the soul than to walk in a knowledge of the Holy Trinity. 
Now note with diligence the distinction between the Persons and the Essence! What is a Person? That which brings forth <note>begets</note> and gives out and gives all things. What is the Essence? 
<margin>30</margin> That which neither gives nor brings forth. What is the power of the Essence? The power of the Essence is that it is without personality (not that it separates itself from the Persons). Rather, that same Essence of the Persons is also the essence of all things; it is the being of those that have being, and the life of those that live, and also the light of the lights. Thus it is not so with the Persons, for 
<margin>35</margin> they are not the Persons of all things, in the way that the Essence is the essence of all things. Therefore the Father cannot make anyone else's person into His own Person. He brought forth...

<meta>This page continues the metaphysical exploration of the Trinity, specifically the relationship between the Divine Essence (Wesen) and the Persons (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit), following the discussion of the soul's union with God.</meta>

in the Person out of His Person, not out of the Essence, but rather with the Essence into the Essence. Thus has the Essence of essences <note>original: "wesen der wesen"</note> flowed through the essence of Fatherhood powerfully to make one, to bear one like unto Himself. Thus is the Essence revealed by the proceeding of the Person. That
<margin>5</margin> is the power <term>vermuglichet: the inherent capability or potentiality</term> of the Person: to reveal the Essence, which cannot reveal itself of itself, for it neither gives back nor brings forth. The power of the Essence is its highest power, yet it is also manifest in itself. The Persons know and comprehend the Essence entirely.
<margin>10</margin> Now there is a question among the masters <note>referring to Scholastic theologians</note>, whether the Persons know and comprehend the Essence to its foundation with the Essence and not with the Persons. For Essence is comprehended by nothing except by Essence: for this reason the Persons of God are God. But that is also so small, what they have comprehended, as a drop of water against the sea. Yet where anything is of God, that is God entirely;
<margin>15</margin> but the indwelling Good, which shall remain theirs eternally, that is the "dazzling nothingness" <note>a reference to the 'divine dark' or 'nothingness' of Pseudo-Dionysius</note>, which causes them to sink away from themselves.
Now one may ask: why is there not one Person, just as there is one Essence? You may note this in all things that exist: they are not of themselves, but they are caused by a Cause that is of itself, which is the
<margin>20</margin> Father; and He has the image of all things in Him, which is the Son; and love toward that same image, which is the Holy Spirit. For this reason, if the (26ᵃ) image of all things had not hovered eternally in the Father, then the Father could not have given birth. This is spoken of the causative power of the Father. For this reason there must now be more than one Person, because in the
<margin>25</margin> eternal outflowing from the Father all things have flowed out, and not in themselves. Thus the eternal outflow is a cause of all things in eternity; but in time they are created from nothing: therefore they are creatures. But in the eternal outflow, in which they have flowed out apart from themselves, there they are God with God. Regarding this, Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-6th century mystical theologian</note> says that the First Cause causes
<margin>30</margin> all things according to the likeness of itself.
Now mark the distinction of the outflow in eternity and in time! What is an outflow? That is a delight of His will with a slight distinction. Thus we have gone out in time in the delight of His love. The eternal outflow is a revelation of Himself in Himself: thus
<margin>35</margin> the revelation flows into a naked knowing of Himself. There, knowing is the same thing as that which is known. This is the eternal outflow, of which never a tear <note>original: "zaher," likely meaning a tiny drop or fragment</note> came out into the mingling of all creatures. This is the Son. From the Father alone all things flow out temporally with measure; but in the

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Trinity, moving from the potentiality of the Divine Persons to the specific mechanics of the "eternal flow" and the soul's intellectual union with God. It builds on the previous page's distinction between the Essence and the Persons.</meta>

of the eternal flow, they are images without measure. Thus is the flow overflowed into itself. Upon this Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century philosopher whose "Mystical Theology" deeply influenced medieval thought</note> says: God is a wellspring who has overflowed into himself. The Father is a cause of the Son, that is in his eternal begetting; the Father and the Son, from whom the Holy Spirit has flowed, <margin>5</margin> (flowed) that is an eternal inflowing <term>eingizzung: the mystical process of divine infusion or "pouring in" of grace and being</term>.

Ah, now one might ask how it stands with Fatherhood: whether it be the origin of the Essence, or whether the Essence be the origin of Fatherhood. Now understand with an enlightened spirit that the Essence neither loves nor brings forth. If the Essence were the origin of the Father, <margin>10</margin> then the Essence would be the one bearing: but it is not so. If Fatherhood were the origin of the Essence, then the Essence would be the receiver: but it is also not so. Since the Essence has itself purely in itself, there it is nothing but pure Essence; rather, the nature of the Essence—which itself is nevertheless the Essence—creates its own hidden nature within "natured nature" <term>genaturten natur: likely referring to "natura naturata," the reality brought forth or "natured" by God's creative activity</term>. This <margin>15</margin> hiddenness is not from the nothing; from the nothing it is one; from the one it is all; from the all it is everything at once. Therefore it is not a nothingness of nothing; therefore it is a uniquely one one, and is also all at once.

This is the primacy of the Father, and the Father is the primacy of the Son, and they both are the primacy of their Spirit, which is one nature with <margin>20</margin> them both.

Oh, how quickly the Spirit, who is common to them, recognizes from Himself into that pure realm which is yet unknown to those who are not stripped bare of their own being! When the bare soul receives anything of God with her understanding, then she knows herself. When she knows how she belongs to Him and how they both are in union—and could she then be free from <margin>25</margin> the heaviness of her body—she would remain there constantly. That high knowing which the soul has from the hidden unknownness is what Job speaks of: "The depth of the mighty spirit comes and whispers to the ears of men" <note>A mystical paraphrase of Job 4:12: "Now a thing was secretly brought to me, and mine ear received a little thereof."</note>. What does he mean by "depth"? That is the diligence <term>sorchsamicheit: a state of intensive spiritual care or focused attention</term> in this knowing of which we speak here: the revelation of the bare, hidden truth. The whispering is the <margin>30</margin> overflowed union, where the knowledge and the knower are one. For this knowledge is unknown to many people.

### No. 24.

->Folio 29 <sup>b</sup>. «In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with<-
->God, and God was the Word».<-

St. Augustine (says) and interprets this word, saying that it is difficult to per-
<margin>10</margin>ceive. Whoever cannot understand it should know that it is the fault
of his unpracticed senses in the light and in the grace. He who does not
understand this, let him pray to God that He grant him to understand it. He who writes of this
does not write of it so that one should sing about it, although one
could say little about it, for it is hidden <note>original: "veste," which can mean firm, fastened, or a stronghold; here implying the topic is beyond human grasp</note> from all people. «In
<margin>15</margin>the beginning was the Word», that is that the Son is born of the
Father; «and the Word was with God», that is, that the person of the Son was
eternally with the person of the Father. «And God was the Word», that is, that
the Son is the same as the Father is in the Essence <term>wesen: the fundamental being or "is-ness" of God</term>.
Now observe with diligence and with an uplifted spirit! This Word was
<margin>20</margin>a beginning, a firstness <term>ersticheit: primacy or the quality of being first</term>, although one cannot establish any [temporal] firstness therein; that is to be understood regarding the [Divine] Persons, for there none is before the
other. Therefore it is called a firstness because it has no beginning. For
all things have their start in Him; before Him there is nothing, and He is before anything [else],
and also one cannot live otherwise, for all things point to the first
<margin>24</margin>cause <note>The footnote suggests "cannot praise" (lobten) instead of "live" (leben)</note> from which they were created. There the firstness does not need the
other as the other needs the firstness; there the other proves that it
cannot deny the firstness. Upon this St. Augustine says of all
creatures that they are not the beginning <term>angeng: origin or inception</term>. This is spoken of
the beginning of which Saint John speaks.
<margin>30</margin>Now note what that means, "the Word in itself"; that means as much
as that there is no distinction, as indeed there is none [in the Essence]. For the Word
has eternally soared in the beginning without distinction of the Essence. Upon this
Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite</note> says: God is flowed-back into himself. The Word, in
what it is, is not physical or material; therefore it is incompre-
<margin>35</margin>hensible to all creatures. Why does he say: «the Word was»? By this he means,

<meta>This page continues the exposition on the "Word" (the Son) from the Prologue of John, using an analogy from St. Augustine to explain the relationship between the Father's essence and the Son's manifestation. It then transitions into a mystical psychological explanation of how the soul's faculties reflect the Trinity and how the human spirit can return to its "origin" in God.</meta>

that it is nothing else than a pure understanding <term>vestantnûz: an intellectual grasping or consciousness</term>. Whatever else is said about it, that is not this Word, for it is an understanding that understands itself. Thus it speaks to the Father, because it is manifest to him that he is. On this St. Augustine says: As a master who had all skill within him, who seeks nothing
<margin>5</margin>outside of himself but had a disciple into whom he poured all his skill, so the disciple makes manifest his master. Thus the Father has poured into his Son everything that he is; thus the Father is revealed in his Son, because it has brought forth in the eternal birth the nature and the same essence <term>wesen: fundamental being</term> that the Father is, and yet he is another in person.
<margin>10</margin>For whatever the Godhead holds up, the humanity (which this Word has taken upon itself) also holds up with it. Whatever else is said—whether it be wisdom or the radiance or an image—that is all spoken too poorly. How it is that he is the nature and the same essence and yet another person is beyond the understanding of all men.

<margin>15</margin>Now one might say: What use is there in this? Whoever wishes to notice it correctly: the soul has a likeness of the same God, and that lies in the highest powers <term>creften: the faculties of the soul, such as memory, intellect, and will</term>. The first power works itself outward and hears and perceives (30 ᵃ) and draws into itself all bodily things spiritually.

The second power gazes upon it and makes it like itself and carries it
<margin>20</margin>up to the first cause. The third power, in which it has perfect likeness of the first essence; just as the essence is its own understanding within itself, so there is an origin <term>ursprunk: the source or "ground" of the soul</term> in the soul in which it understands all truth. Regarding this, a high master says: Whoever should understand the truth should not understand it just as it is truth, but rather he should also understand it within a truth.
<margin>25</margin>It is certain that the origin of the first truth is perceivable within us. When the spirit comes to the point that it is taken up from the contemplation of spiritual images into the unformed essence of the firstness <term>ersticheit: primacy or the state of being first</term>, there the spirit comes back into the origin of the first cause. Therefore the prophet says: Whoever wishes to come to the mountain
<margin>30</margin>of the height of the essence of the spirit should be without sin and should be separated from all bodily things and should go beyond all the powers of the soul. For he should dwell in the highest power, in which he is joined into the first essence.

### No. 27.

Fol. 31 b. <note>original Latin: "Circumfulsit Paulum lux"</note> **A light shone around Paul.** Saint Paul was surrounded by a light and it struck him down; there he heard a voice that spoke: "Saul, Saul, why do you persecute <note>original: "echtest"</note> me?" Then he spoke: "Lord, what will you have me do?" He was caught up into the third heaven. This "pulling up" <term>zuk: a rapture or being forcibly drawn; the root of "entzuct" (raptured)</term> is as much as to say that he is above nature, or drawn above the things that are natural, and thus fell away from his own nature. **Damascene** <note>John of Damascus, an 8th-century monk and theologian</note> says: Man is an image of God, therefore it is fitting for him to know God, and yet not by
<margin>5</margin>nature. It happens—if you will believe me—above nature.

How did he know God? Was he raptured into the third heaven, into the 
<margin>10</margin>heaven where God is, not so that a form might be shown to him? A saint says: **God is a light to which there is no approach.** <note>original: "zeganges"</note> **Saint Augustine** says: The Son comes from the Father as light from light. For God is an incomprehensible light; it is so transcendent <term>uberswenk: overflowing or surpassing all measure</term> that one cannot grasp him with the understanding <term>verstantnůz: the faculty of reason or intellect</term>,
<margin>15</margin>for he is a light above nature. This light is not a likeness <term>glichnůz: a reflected image or symbol</term> that one sees through a likeness. Therefore he did not remain there. This light is of two kinds: one is formal light, which remained in his knowledge <term>bechantnůz: cognition or awareness</term>; the other did not remain in his knowledge. While he was raptured, he had neither faith nor hope <note>original: "geding"</note>. He knew without image <term>pild: a mental representation or sensory form</term>, without mediation, God in his essence, as 
<margin>20</margin>he is.

Now there is a question: whether Saul was dead or alive. Now he says himself: "I know a man in Christ who was raptured into the third heaven, whether it were in the body or not, I do not know, but God knows it well." **Saint Augustine** says: No one should take it upon himself 
<margin>25</margin>to say this, since he himself did not say it. He saw without image, nothing other than within himself. No power remained in him that was of the senses; no power remained in him except the understanding. Whatever gives life was not in Saul; rather, much less remained in him than in one who sleeps, for that person still has images in a dream.

<margin>30</margin>Now there is a question: how much did he know God? He knew God as He was, and did not remain as he knew Him, lest some image be imprinted in him. Therefore he did not remain blessed like the saints. It happened to him as to a person who becomes red from looking <note>the text suggests a temporary flushing or blushing</note>: when that passes, he is pale again. But the saints are constantly red, like a person who is red by nature.

<margin>35</margin>A teacher says: A person might have such great desire that he would be so raptured that he thought he was raptured and yet was not raptured, but he simply comes into a forgetting of himself. Our desire is not a perfect thing, but rather the understanding alone was raptured.

So it was with Saint Paul: if the lower faculties had shared in the rapture <term>zuckes: a pulling or snatching away; the mystical state of ecstasy</term>, he would have been incapable of 
<margin>5</margin> suffering, and for that reason, he was active <note>original: "wurchend" (working/acting)</note>. 
It is best that one strengthens the heart with grace. <note>A reference to Hebrews 13:9: "It is good that the heart be established with grace."</note> Nothing is higher than grace. The perfection of a thing lies in its end, says the wise man <note>Aristotle</note>. A human never reaches the best until he grasps his end. Everything that exists cannot grasp Him by its natural manner. Therefore, 
<margin>10</margin> because He is transcendent <term>uberswenket: overflowing, exceeding all bounds</term>, it is impossible for the soul to grasp God unless it is drawn above nature, and that is grace. Grace is a beginning. The least thing <gloss>the innermost</gloss> that is called grace works more than the nature of every creature. Grace draws nature into God. The least work that grace performs is more transcendent than everything that creation performs. With the least grace, a 
<margin>15</margin> creature is drawn above the nature of all angels without grace. The least thing that is grace is nobler and higher than everything that creatures are able to perform. The least human with grace is better than all humans without grace. The work of grace is above the work of all creatures. God works in every person through grace. In grace, God draws the spirit above itself. All 
<margin>20</margin> of nature’s work is measured; God’s work in grace is immeasurable. Where God remains in the soul, and the soul rests, there He works in grace. As grace enters, He draws the soul above itself. Therefore he says: It is best that one strengthens the heart with grace. Whatever is not steady, flows away. Grace works according to divine nature. Grace is nowhere but in the 
<margin>25</margin> essence of the soul, not in the heart, for that is bodily; and yet [he] says: It is best that one strengthens the heart with grace. Grace is nowhere but in the ground <term>grund: the deepest, most essential part of the soul</term> of the soul, where it is drawn into the "over-being" <term>uberwesen: transcendence or divine existence beyond being</term>. The heart lives from the soul, thus grace is in the heart. But strictly speaking, grace is only in the being of the soul. 
<margin>30</margin> The heart has the nature that it never receives anything except when it dies. I say: it can suffer no pain without death, and yet people say: "my heart hurts." It is not in the heart; it is around the heart. The heart is always working and yet never grows tired. 
That we may be strengthened with grace, may He help us, etc.

Whoever wishes to be **Christ’s disciple** must ascend and lift his reason above bodily things and must travel beyond the fury of the instability of perishable things. As long as there is any changeability there—whether it be worldly knowledge <note>original: "chundicheit," meaning familiarity with the world or social ties</note>, or anger, or sadness—it covers the reason so that it cannot hear the Word.
<margin>5</margin> A master <note>Likely a reference to Aristotle or a scholastic philosopher</note> says: Whoever is to understand natural things and also material things must strip his understanding of all other things. I have also said this before: as the sun pours out its radiance upon physical things, whatever it can grasp there, it makes small and draws it upward with its power.
<margin>10</margin> The radiance of the sun, where it has flowed out—when it draws [moisture] up into the air and it is large in itself and warm from the sun—when it then climbs up toward the cold, it finds a resistance from the cold and is struck down and becomes rain or snow. So it is with the Holy Spirit. He lifts the soul up (33 a) <note>A reference to the manuscript folio number</note> and raises it and draws it up, and if she were prepared, he would pull 
<margin>15</margin> her into the **ground** <term>grunt: the innermost essence or "divine spark" of the soul where God dwells</term> from which he has flowed out. Thus does the Holy Spirit: if he is left in the soul, she climbs upward; as the Holy Spirit falls away from the soul, so she sinks down. For what is of the earth sinks; fire and what is of fire is [drawn] upwards. Therefore, a person 
<margin>20</margin> must have trodden under foot all things that are earthly and everything that can cover this understanding, so that nothing remains there except that which is like the understanding. If the soul still works in understanding, then it is like it. The soul that has thus overcome all things, the Spirit lifts up and supports her with him into the ground from which it has flowed out. 
<margin>25</margin> Yes, he [lifts] her into her **eternal image** <note>original: "ewig pild," the divine archetype or "idea" of the soul as it exists eternally in God</note> from which she flowed out—into that image where all things are one to her, into the width and into the depth where all things end again. Whoever wishes to come here must have trodden under foot all things that are unlike it, and [whoever] wishes to hear the Word and wishes to be Jesus's disciple of salvation.
Now take note! **Saint Paul** says: >As we with unveiled face behold the splendor and the **clarity** <term>clarheit: radiance, brightness, or divine glory</term> of God, so we are 
<margin>30</margin> transformed and formed into the image,< <note>A translation of 2 Corinthians 3:18</note> just as the Godhead gave itself into Our Lady’s reason all at once, because she was bare and pure when she received God into herself. From the overflow of the Godhead, it broke out and flowed over into the body of Our Lady, and a light was formed by the Holy Spirit in 
<margin>35</margin> Our Lady's body. And had she not carried the Godhead in her reason, she would never have received him bodily. A master says that it is a special grace and a great gift that one flies up with the feather of understanding and lifts oneself reasonably toward God and becomes joined from clarity with clarity. If he were not <insert>good</insert>, then <insert>love</insert> would not love him and would not take him as God. Without goodness, she would have nothing. The reason <note>original: "Vernunft"</note> of the soul loves God as he is a pure being, a transcending being. For being and goodness and truth 
<margin>15</margin> are equally broad, and as far as being extends, so it is good and it is true. Yes, for truth consists in being, as he said to Moses: "He who is, he has sent me." <note>original: "Der da ist, der hat mich gesant" — a reference to Exodus 3:14, *Ego sum qui sum*</note> 
<margin>20</margin> Saint Augustine says: The truth is the Son in the Father; for truth consists in being. Is being truth? If one asked many a master this, he would say: yes! If one had asked me myself, I would have said: yes! For truth is also "added on." Now they take him as he is **One**, for "One" is more properly "one" than that which unites. When all else is laid 
<margin>25</margin> aside, yet that very thing which is laid aside is the same as that which is added, in that it changes <note>original: "andert," meaning to become 'other' or to differentiate</note>.

And if he is neither goodness nor being nor truth nor one, what is he then? He is also **nothing**; he is neither this nor that. If you still think of anything [specific], then he is not that. Where then shall the soul take truth? If she finds no truth there, where 
<margin>30</margin> she is formed into a unity, into the first purity, into the impression of pure essentiality? (Does she find no truth?) No, she finds no grasp of any truth, but truth follows afterward, truth comes from it. Saint Paul (says) he was caught up into the third heaven. What these heavens are, note this: the one is above all 
<margin>35</margin> physicality; the second is an estrangement from all imagery; the third is a bare understanding without mediation in God. <meta>This page continues the theological discussion regarding the nature of the soul and the mystical experiences of Saint Paul, specifically addressing his rapture or ecstasy.</meta>

the point of a needle, he would have been aware of it. For Saint Augustine <note>original: "sanctus Augutinus"</note> says in the book *On the Soul and the Spirit*: The soul is created as if at a point between time and eternity; with the lower senses it practices temporal things according to time, but according to the highest power it grasps <margin>5</margin> and perceives eternal things without time. Therefore he says, had one touched Saint Paul with the point of a needle during the time of his rapture <note>original: "enzuckung"; referring to a mystical state of being "caught up" to heaven</note>, he would have been aware of it, because his soul remained in his body, just as the form remains in its matter. And just as the sun illuminates the air, and the air the earth, so his spirit received pure light from God, and [the] soul from the spirit, and the <margin>10</margin> body from the soul. Thus it is manifest how Saint Paul was both raptured and yet remained. He was raptured according to his spirituality <note>original: "geistlicheit"</note>, he remained according to his soul-nature <note>original: "selicheit"</note>.

The second question is whether Saint Paul understood outside of time or within time. I say: He understood without doubt, he understood outside of time; for he did not understand by means of the angels, who were created in time, but rather he understood by means of God, <margin>15</margin> who was there before time, whom time never grasped.

The third question is whether he [understood] in God or God in him. I say: God understood in him, and he as nothing in God. Take a parable <note>original: "glichnůz"</note>: the sun shines through the glass; the rose-water comes through the cleverness of the glass's matter from the burning power of the sun: thus the sun gives birth in <margin>20</margin> the glass and not the glass in the sun. So it was concerning Saint Paul: when the clear sun of the Godhead <note>original: "gotheit"</note> shone through his soul, there was pressed out from the holy rose of the sweet spirit the flow of the lovely divine (34 <sup>a</sup>) distinction, of which the prophet speaks: The rush of the river never gladdens my city, which is my soul. And that happened <margin>25</margin> to him through the clarity of his soul, which love penetrated by the touch of the Godhead. The fellowship of the body hinders you, so that your soul cannot understand as purely as the angel; but as much as one recognizes things without material substance, so much is one angelic. The soul recognizes from without; God understands in Himself through Himself, for He is the origin of all things.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of Saint Paul’s mystical experiences to a specific sermon by Master Eckhart (Sermon 31) concerning the nature of God and the "temple" of the soul. It continues the exploration of how the divine interacts with time and being.</meta>

### No. 31. <note>Compare with Pfeiffer, page 267.</note>

Fol. 38 a. Master Eckhart spoke: This word, which I have presented to you in Latin <note>original: "in latein"</note>, is written in the Book of Wisdom <note>The quote actually originates from Ecclesiasticus/Sirach 50:6–7, but was often attributed to the Book of Wisdom in medieval texts.</note> and is read in the works of Saint Augustine, and it says in German thus: "Like a morning star in the midst of the mist, and like the full moon in its days, or <margin>5</margin> like a reflection or a radiance of the sun, this lord has shone forth in the temple of God." I take the last phrase: "the temple of God." What is God, or what is God’s temple? Twenty-four masters came together and all wanted to say what God might be. They came and each one brought his word; of these, I shall take five <note>Though the text says "five" here, Eckhart only lists three in the following sentences; medieval editors noted this as a likely transcription error.</note>. One said: "God is something compared <margin>10</margin> to which all temporal and changeable things are nothing." The second said: "God is something above being <term>wesen; the essence or fundamental nature of a thing</term>." The third said: "God is an intellect <note>original: "vernunfticheit"</note> who lives by his own knowledge alone."

I leave the first and the last two and speak of the second: that God is something above being. For just as being has time or place, these do <margin>15</margin> not touch God, for he is up above. God is in things insofar as they have being, and yet he is above them; for that which is one in many things must of necessity be above those things. The very fact that he is in all things means he is also above all things. Some masters claim that the soul is entirely in the heart. This is not so; the soul is entirely undivided—undivided in the eyes, <margin>20</margin> undivided in the feet, undivided in every single limb. I take a piece of time, which is the day today or the day tomorrow; but then I take the "Now" <note>original: "nu"; referring to the "Eternal Now," a moment where time and eternity meet</note>, which takes all time into itself. The "Now" in which God made the world is as near to the day that was yesterday as the "Now" in which I speak at this moment; the Last Judgment is as near in eternity as the "Now" in which I <margin>25</margin> speak at this moment.

Great masters say that God is a pure being. This is not so! God is above being as high as the highest angel is above a gnat. Where being was not, there God worked <note>original: "wurht" or "wart"</note> being in non-being <note>original: "nichtwesen"</note>. Lesser masters read in the schools that all kinds of being are divided into ten modes; <margin>30</margin> these they strip away from God entirely, for none of them touches God or hides him. The first and also the coarsest mode, which carries the most being, is equal to you, who carry the least being, for they both have an image in God. In God, the image of all things is equal, yet they are the images of unequal things, such as the angel, the soul, and the gnat. As I have said: God is not being; by saying this I have not <margin>35</margin> stripped being away from him, rather I have exalted it in him. If I find copper in gold, it is there, but in a higher way than it is in itself, and...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of God initiated on the previous page, moving from the definitions of the "masters" to specific theological paradoxes and the comparison between the intellect and the will.</meta>

yet copper is not lacking in the gold. A master speaks, and that is
Saint Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century theologian whose "apophatic" or "negative" theology—describing God by what He is not—deeply influenced Eckhart.</note>: God is neither this nor that. He who thinks he has
known God, if he knew anything at all, it was not God. Saint Augustine says:
God is powerful without power and wise without wisdom and is good without goodness. A
<margin>5</margin> master speaks: God is something that works in eternity, undivided in Himself,
who needs no one's help or witness, remaining in Himself,
needing nothing, and all things need Him; all things strive toward Him
as toward their final end, where the end is without a mode <note>original: "an weise"</note> and the mode and God vanish
into joy.
<margin>10</margin> Saint Bernard says: To love God is a mode beyond mode <note>original: "weis an weis," meaning a way without a way, or a measure without measure.</note>. A
physician who wants to make a sick person healthy has no limit <note>original: "weis"</note> to how healthy
he wants to make him; he certainly has a limit to the means by which he wants to make him healthy, but as healthy
<gloss>(28 b)</gloss> as he can possibly be, and that is without a limit. How dearly should we
hold God? As dearly as we possibly can, and that is without a limit. No thing
<margin>15</margin> works above its being <term>wesen; essence or existence</term>. Fire works only in the wood. God
works above being in the "way" <note>original: "weise"</note> where He can touch Himself. God is
good, He is better, He is the very best. Goodness lives in being and is not broader
than being; if there were no being, there would also be no goodness; being is even purer
than goodness. Now our Lord says: "No one is good, except God alone" <note>A reference to Luke 18:19.</note>.
<margin>20</margin> We call a man good when he is useful and common <note>original: "gemein"; in this context, "common" means communal, shared, or universally available to others.</note>. Upon this
a pagan master speaks: A hermit is neither good nor bad in that sense,
since he is not useful and common. No things share themselves out of their own
being, for they are not of themselves. That very thing that they are,
they have from another. God is the most common of all, for He is of
<margin>25</margin> Himself what He is. In all His gifts, God gives Himself entirely as God, as He
is, to whoever could receive Him as He is. The soul that loves God, loves Him under
the veil of goodness.

Yet these are all the words of pagans, who knew nothing except through natural
knowledge <note>original: "wechantnûz"</note>. We have not yet come to the words of the holy masters,
<margin>30</margin> who knew in a much higher light.

I read in the school that intellect <term>vernunfticheit; the faculty of understanding or reason</term> was nobler than will. Another
master taught in another school that will is nobler than intellect.
This is not so. Will takes things as they are in themselves; intellect takes
things as they are in it [the intellect]. This is true. An eye is nobler in itself than an
<margin>35</margin> eye painted on the wall. Now let us take this in the soul: it also has a small drop
of intellect; it has powers <note>original: "craft"</note> that work in the body. One power functions
and works more in the night than in the day, through which a human being
grows. The soul has another power with which I think. I think of a
rose in the winter, when there is no rose. With that power, the soul works in
<margin>40</margin> non-being and follows God, who works in being.

<keywords>Dionysius, Augustine, Bernard, intellect vs will, being, essence, goodness, soul's powers, nature of God</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of God's nature, shifting from the definitions of "goodness" and "being" to the superiority of the intellect over the will and the relationship between the soul and the Divine using the metaphors of the mirror and the celestial bodies.</meta>

Another power the soul has in the eyes is so subtle that it does not take things as they are in themselves; they must first be purified in the air and the light. St. James says: "Every great, perfect gift comes down from the Father of Lights." <note>James 1:17. The author uses this to explain how perception requires a medium of light.</note> (Meaning all falls into fading [unclear]...) <margin>5</margin> If we take God in His essence, we take Him in His vestibule. Essence <term>wesen; being or existence</term> is God’s vestibule, where He dwells. Now, where does God dwell in His temple? Intellect <term>vernunfticheit; the power of reason or the higher mind</term> is the temple of God, where He dwells and shines holily within. God dwells nowhere more properly than in His temple of the intellect, for there He is in His stillness, where nothing touches Him. God, in His <margin>10</margin> unique knowledge, knows Himself in Himself, as the other master says: God is an intellect who lives in His own unique knowledge.

One who would clothe a king in grave clothes on the day he was made king would not have clothed him well. "Good" is a garment under which God <margin>15</margin> is hidden. If God were not good in God, God would not want it for a moment. I am not blessed because God is good; I am also not blessed because God is intellectual and I recognize that; but I say that intellect is better than will, because intellect pulls the skin of "goodness" off God and takes Him naked, where He is stripped of goodness, of essence, and <margin>20</margin> of all names. The intellect of God is that upon which the angel’s essence and his life entirely depend.

One asks me whether the image exists more properly in the mirror or in that from which it originates. I say: It is more properly in that from which it originates. When the mirror stands before me, my image is in it; (39 a) <margin>25</margin> but when the mirror falls away, my image is no longer there. My image is in me, from me, and toward me. The intellect of God is that upon which the angel’s essence and life entirely depend, where he recognizes himself as a morning star in the midst of the mist.

The most essential thing that one can say of God is "Word" and <margin>30</margin> "Truth." For our Lord spoke: "I am the truth," <note>John 14:6</note> and Saint John spoke: "In the beginning was the Word." <note>John 1:1</note> By this he meant that we should be an "adjunct" <note>original: "peywort," literally a 'by-word' or 'adverb', implying we are dependent on the primary 'Word'.</note> to this Word. Just like the "Friday-star" <note>original: "vreistern," referring to the planet Venus</note>, which is called "free" on Friday; when it rises before the sun, it is called a morning star; when it follows the sun, it is called an evening star; <margin>35</margin> sometimes it is above the sun, sometimes below the sun. Among all stars, it is the one closest to the sun.

He also says: "Like a full moon <note>original: "man," likely a variant of "mâne" (moon) rather than "man" (person), given the context of celestial cycles and tides.</note> in its days." The moon has dominion over all moist nature. Never does the moon become so full as when it stands facing the sun and receives its light from the sun; the higher it <margin>40</margin> stands in the heavens, the more powerful it is, and the more it is able to cast out the sea <note>A reference to the moon's influence on the tides.</note>.

<summary>This page argues that the intellect is the highest faculty because it perceives God "naked," stripped of attributes like "goodness," and uses the morning star and moon as metaphors for the soul's proximity to God.</summary>
<keywords>Intellect, Essence, Vestibule of God, Mirror Analogy, Morning Star, Moon and Tides, Truth, The Word</keywords>
