<meta>This page continues the discussion on the intellect's superiority and the soul's perception, using the metaphor of celestial bodies to explain how proximity to the material world weakens spiritual power.</meta>

the closer he stands to the earth, the weaker he becomes. From this he suffers two harms: he becomes pale and flaky, so that he loses his light. <note>The author likely refers to a planet or star, using medieval cosmology to argue that earthly attachment diminishes spiritual "light."</note>

The more the soul is raised above the earthly, the stronger she becomes. He who knows nothing but the creature <term>creatur; any created thing, as opposed to the Creator</term> would never need to think of any sermon,
<margin>5</margin>for every creature <gloss>is</gloss> full of God and is a book.

Whoever wishes to come to this state must be like a morning star, always near to God and equally near, so that fortune and misfortune cannot separate him from God. Beside this word, an adverb. <note>The original term "beiwort" literally means "adverb" or "adjective." Eckhart uses this linguistically to show how the soul is an attribute or "adjunct" to God’s "Verb."</note> It is a brought-forth word, which is the angel and the soul and all creatures; there is another word,
<margin>10</margin>thought and un-brought-forth, to which one may come, which I form within myself; there is another word, un-thought and un-brought-forth, which is eternally in the Father who speaks it, which never came out. <note>This describes the three levels of the "Word": the physical world, the human intellect, and the eternal Divine Logos.</note>

The intellect <term>vernunfticheit; the higher mind or power of reason</term> of the soul, the more it is working inward, the more subtle and spiritual it is in its working toward God; the intellect of the soul,
<margin>15</margin>the stronger it is, the more it knows and becomes united with Him. It is not so with bodily things: the stronger they are, the more they work outward into time, far from God. God’s blessedness <term>selicheit; happiness or salvation</term> lies in the inward working of the intellect, where the eternal Word remains within. Beside this eternal Word, the noble soul shall be an adverb <note>original: "beiwort"</note> and shall work one work with God and there
<margin>20</margin>take her blessedness. Where God is blessed in the hovering knowledge <note>original: "swebenten bechantnúz"; a dynamic, non-static state of divine awareness</note>, there the noble soul shall create and receive her blessedness in the same way that God is blessed. Beside this word, an adverb; may the Father help us to this.

<meta>This page continues the theological discourse on the "birth of the Son" within the soul, a central theme in Rhineland mysticism, specifically associated with the school of Meister Eckhart. The text explores the relationship between God, the soul, and the eternal "now" of creation.</meta>

The masters ask whether the Son has been born. One answers and says: No! The masters say, should the Son be born? One says: No! The masters answer this and say that the Son has been born perfectly and is being born perfectly, now green and fresh without ceasing <note>The author emphasizes that the "birth of God in the soul" is not a past event, but an eternal, ongoing process.</note>. <margin>5</margin> Christ is [divine power] and divine wisdom. In this power, God has created all things, for His wisdom is there and His power.

   Christ is a man. Now Saint Paul says: I have betrothed and wedded this man to you. <note>original: "Disen man han ich eu getrewet und gemahelt." A reference to 2 Corinthians 11:2.</note> Just as a marriage exists outwardly between a woman and a man, so there is an inward marriage <margin>10</margin> between God and the soul. Therefore, one gives a virgin a man for the sake of the truth of the birth. Therefore, God created the soul so that He might bear His only-begotten Son in her without ceasing. When this birth happened spiritually in our Lady Mary, that was more pleasing to God than when He was born of her physically. When this birth <margin>15</margin> happens today in a person’s soul, that is more pleasing to God than when He created heaven and earth. The masters and the saints say, and it is also true, that the soul is worthier than the heavens. Saint John wrote in the Book of Secrets <note>original: "taugen puch," referring to the Book of Revelation</note>: One who sits upon the throne spoke: I make all things new. Saint Augustine says: God’s <margin>20</margin> speaking is His bearing <note>or "giving birth"</note>, and His bearing is His speaking. And if [He] ceased to speak this word for as long as a blink of an eye, heaven and earth would have to perish. Saint Augustine says: In the same way as there is a marriage between woman and man, so there is a marriage in your soul.

   The highest part of the soul is the man. The man in the soul <margin>25</margin> should stand naked at all times, and the woman should be covered, so that the lower is drawn to the highest and the loftiest. <note>In medieval mystical psychology, the "man" often represents the higher intellect/reason, while the "woman" represents the lower faculties or the will.</note> Nature has it in her that she always wants to work in like manner; she always wants to bear like the father. Were she not hindered, a son would be born every time and never a woman. Now God does this through His own goodness and hinders nature, from which <margin>30</margin> a woman is made; otherwise, no woman would ever be born. <note>This reflects the medieval Aristotelian view that nature always aims for the "most perfect" (male), and the female is a result of specific conditions directed by providence.</note> If there were no human but one, there would be neither time nor place nor matter. Man renews himself so that the Son becomes like the Father in every way.

   Now God says: If I have made all things fruitful <term>berhaft: capable of bearing fruit or offspring</term>, why [am] I then not fruitful myself? First, God bears His likeness in the soul and thereafter Himself, and no less than Himself. <margin>35</margin> Thus He who is God’s Son in eternity is also the soul’s son and her child, so that God and the soul have one child together.

If she then wishes to reach the point where she needs nothing at all and becomes more immovable <note>original: *unbewegenlicher*; in this context, it refers to a state of being completely unaffected by worldly changes, mirroring God’s eternal stability.</note> than nothingness, then she should gather all her powers into her free will, so that she remains unhindered by herself and by all things, and that she unites herself in the **immovable [God]**, who was never moved by any works that the **Holy Trinity** ever wrought, and who never needed anything of all that the **Holy Trinity** ever wrought.

If the soul is to reach the point where she becomes more immovable than nothingness, she should be so entirely sunken into the **bottomless ground** <term>grundlozen grunt</term> <note>A central concept in German mysticism, referring to the deepest part of the soul where it meets the essence of God beyond all understanding.</note> of the **divine nothingness** that she can draw nothing out of it, nor ever incline herself toward lesser things than **God**; so that she remains there as steadily as the **heavenly Father** remains eternally in His nature, and shall remain eternally without hindrance, as far as is possible for a creature. 

Ah, good **God**, why is it impossible for the soul, since she was created for this? This is the fault: she inclines herself toward lesser things and lets herself be satisfied by them; therefore, those things that are above her are impossible for her. For **God** has nothing so incomprehensible in all His being that it would be impossible for the soul, who is so noble that she is able to seek it. 

Ah, noble soul, go out from yourself, so that you never return again, and restrain yourself so that you never look back; and do not burden yourself with anything that is revealed to you, and do not let anything that is present to you disturb or hinder you through any service or any agitation. Follow your **bare nature** <term>blozzer natur</term> <note>The soul in its essential state, stripped of all worldly attachments and images.</note> closely, toward the **self-sufficient nothing** <term>undurftigen nicht</term> <note>Literally the "un-needy" or "lack-less" nothing. This describes God as a void that is paradoxically full because it requires nothing external to exist.</note>, and seek no other place than that self-sufficient nothing. He who created you from nothing shall Himself be your place in His self-sufficient nothing, and in His immovability you shall become more immovable than nothingness.

**Lord**, You say You have made me like You; that transcends all senses; for there is no master so wise that he can make an image (44 b) that is exactly like himself. **Lord**, since I am like You, grant me, **Lord**, that I may see You as You have seen me, and that I may take hold of You as You have taken hold of me. And grant me, **Lord**, that I may be united by Your grace into Your nature, as the **Son** is eternally one in Your nature, and that Your grace may become my nature. For, **Lord**, Your grace becomes nature, and by Your grace we become God, just as the **heavenly Father** is.

Here **Christ** admonishes us that we should be perfect as the **heavenly Father** is in His nature; He says: **God** is closer to you than you are to yourselves. This is also what **Saint Augustine** says: The soul has a secret entrance into the divine nature, where all things become nothing; there, through knowing she becomes **knowledge-less**, and through loving **love-less**, and through willing **will-less**, and through light **dark**. If she knew herself, that would be a flaw in her; if she knew **God** in her, that would be a...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the relationship between God the Father and the soul, specifically focusing on how the soul is drawn upward through grace toward a mystical union and divine vision, as exemplified by Saint Paul.</meta>

Father. In this way the Father draws the soul, so that He runs with the soul. The best gift that the Father has given us is the gift of nature; the most perfect is the gift of grace, which has drawn us from these lowest things up to the highest. Regarding this, Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, an influential mystical theologian</note> says: This power from 
<margin>5</margin>above is drawn upon us and has united us and drawn us to a God-made unity and to the simplicity where God unites the soul with Himself; there the soul becomes a partaker of the divine nature—not that it becomes God by nature along with Him, but rather it becomes a partaker of His nature in some measure. There are two transformations: one is a transformation from nature
<margin>10</margin>to grace, the other is a transformation of the divine vision <note>The 'beatific vision,' or the direct experience of seeing God</note>. Now we should examine whether we can attain this while in this body. When God made us, He could not have made us better, such that we would be better humans than we are. Had He made us any better, we would be angels and not humans. Mankind is created for
<margin>15</margin>eternal life; therefore, so that we may worthily attain this, a light enters the soul and gives it power and makes it perfect in its nature; then the soul is drawn above itself into a divine vision—that is an ecstasy <term>enzucken: a state of being "carried away" or "raptured" into a spiritual trance</term> or a grasping, where God is seen with God. Thus one can arrive in the blink of an eye, as Saint Paul did; he was
<margin>20</margin>caught up into the third heaven. Some say he saw a reflection of the Godhead, just as the sun casts its light upon a wall; but that is not so—he saw the divine nature itself. There he heard a word, and in that word he heard many words. That hearing was a vision; there he heard things that one cannot and must not speak, for they are beyond the power of human nature.
<margin>25</margin>That which is beyond human power is God. What did he see in that vision? He saw the divine nature. One finds three kinds of heaven: the first is a crystalline heaven, which is when the soul gazes upon the good that God has done through Himself for it and for all creatures.
The second heaven is a starry one, where the power of many arts
<margin>30</margin><gloss>(47<sup>b</sup>)</gloss> and the stars of many arts shine; this is when the soul gazes upon the manifold good that God has wrought in it and accomplished by His power.
The third heaven is a divine heaven, into which Saint Paul was caught up. One finds three kinds of ecstasy: the first are those caught up 
<margin>35</margin>by the sickness of their bodies, which comes from themselves; the others are caught up by the enemy <note>The "enemy" here refers to the devil or demonic possession</note> when they are possessed; the third is a divine ecstasy, as Saint Paul was caught up. This is what Saint Augustine says: Divine nature is seen in this life.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual perception and the soul's relationship with God, following themes of clear understanding and the obstacles posed by worldly attachments.</meta>

...pure, clear understanding of nature. If the pagan masters and priests <note>original: "heidnischen meister und phaffen"; likely referring to ancient philosophers like Aristotle and Plato who were respected but considered limited by their lack of Christian revelation.</note> were still alive, they would gladly hear the word of God, for they turned their understanding toward the highest even without faith. And from this I also conclude that it is natural to hear God's word, provided that the 
<margin>5</margin>person aligns themselves with it. Those people who hear God in such a way <insert>are dead</insert>. <note>Meaning they hear the words physically but are spiritually unresponsive because they do not act on them.</note>

There are also people who see God; they are of three types. The first are those who see God from afar in a darkness: these are the people who serve God, yet they possess such disordered love for created things <term>creatures; any worldly thing, person, or object that is not God</term>. When they should join themselves to God, they instead practice this disordered love. For the 
<margin>10</margin>created thing places itself between them and God, not in its physical essence but through its mental image. Nevertheless, they see God with their spirit, but this is a small thing, for their life is heavily inclined toward created things—I mean not only people but other created things as well. The spirit would gladly be away from them, yet the soul wants to remain there: thus there is a struggle between the soul and the spirit in the 
<margin>15</margin>simple essence. These people are also dead. The second type who see God are those who see Him clearly and yet through a medium <note>original: "mit eim mittel"; an intermediary or something standing in between.</note>: these are they who love God above all things; if they knew of anything that was against God, they would cast it aside. Yet they still love those things that do not separate them from God: that is the medium in 
<margin>20</margin>which they see God. These people are alive but are weak; it is with them as it is with the sick. A sick person has three traits: he cannot perform any strong work; whatever he does, he does without joy. The second is that sour things taste good to him. The third is that no good food tastes right to him according to its value. So it is with 
<margin>25</margin>these people. Although they are alive, every good work is difficult for them; as they should unite themselves with God to some degree, as much as they are able, that is difficult for them; for the medium they hold onto is a hindrance to them. These people have the most precarious life. Although <insert>folio 48b</insert> 
<margin>30</margin>discretion <term>bescheidenheit; the ability to discern or use reason</term> has taken the upper hand, their soul is still inclined to love things that are not God. Therefore, the soul is drawn to take up that love, but discretion holds it back; for this reason, they are in a state of trial <note>original: "becherung [sic!]"; likely a scribal error for 'Anfechtung' (temptation) or 'Bekümmerung' (affliction).</note>. Whatever their many distractions perceive, it entices them toward that love. They must stand 
<margin>35</margin>in great watchfulness, or they will quickly fall back into death. These people see God clearly through a medium.

The third type see God clearly without a medium; these are they who have abandoned all love, except for the natural necessity of the body. That which one sees has a medium; that which touches has no medium. If you wish to touch God, then 
<margin>40</margin>you must step beyond your nature.

One touches God in two ways—not the divine essence itself. The first touching is simple, just as one hand touches the other. The heat that one has, it shares with the other. Thus God does to those who touch Him simply, and through this they 
<margin>45</margin>attain the freedom of their spirit, which he [man] had in the first Paradise.

<summary>This page categorizes three ways of perceiving God, moving from those hindered by worldly images to those who see God clearly but still struggle with attachments, and finally to those who achieve direct contact through total detachment.</summary>

they are not God, except insofar as they resemble God in the essence that they are.

The second "let it be done" <note>original: *fiat*</note> (49), which was spoken in that moment of time <insert>this happened at the Word</insert>, when our Lady spoke to the angel, as he said: "Greetings to you, full of all grace, the Lord is with you." <margin>5</margin> Then our Lady spoke: "According to your word, let it be done to me."

That she was full of grace, she did not heed; rather, she desired much more that God should be with her. Therefore, Saint Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian often cited by medieval mystics</note> says of our Lady’s virtues that they are so incomprehensible that he had to remain silent about her. There the Word was made flesh <note>original: *ingeflischet*, literally 'infleshed'</note>, which flows eternally from the Father like <margin>10</margin> a radiance, as Master Plato <note>The Greek philosopher; medieval writers often attributed such metaphors to him</note> said: that He is a radiance of His Father. Were there a man so great that his head reached to heaven and his arms as wide as the firmament, and if one held a small mirror before him: one would see him entirely. In this way, the eternal Word was made flesh. He took human nature upon himself and not a human person, and from the <margin>15</margin> work of the Holy Spirit, a body was made, and a soul created from nothing; and the person of the Son united with human nature, and the union happened in a single moment of [time] all at once, and not before or after, so that a perfect human and perfect God was there. <margin>20</margin>

Now there is a question among the masters, whether the works of the Holy Trinity <note>original: *drivaldicheit*</note> are divided, or whether the Holy Spirit worked alone when he made the body, and whether the Son worked alone when he took human nature upon himself. The works of the Holy Trinity are undivided, yet according to the order of things, one gives to the Son one part, and to the Holy Spirit the other. <margin>25</margin>

Now there is a question: was the eternal Word received in Mary personally or essentially, and was he also in the bosom of the Father personally and essentially? You may understand it thus: in the constant flow, the Word flows from the Father as if from an intellect <note>original: *verstantnucz*</note>, in which the Word is born to itself. In that constant flow, Mary received the eternal Word in <margin>30</margin> time, in that moment, personally and essentially, within the indwelling <insert>flow</insert>, where the Word flows as from an intellect in the Father; thus he remained in the bosom of the Father according to the indwelling intellect personally and essentially. (Thus no one [can grasp] the eternal flow, as he [came] from the Father into Mary personally and essentially); thus he came, coming according to the <margin>35</margin> flow, and remained as the indwelling intellect.

Oh, what light and what grace should rightly come to every enlightened soul from this clear distinction! Now let us speak further of Christ and of his supernatural light. Now there is a question: whether the light of Christ’s soul was supernatural, even though it was a creature, and <margin>40</margin>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the relationship between God and the soul, specifically focusing on the moment of Christ’s Incarnation and the nature of "supernatural light."</meta>

Christ’s soul, though it is a creature <note>meaning a created thing, rather than the Creator</note>, is the subject here. Now the masters ask whether His created being is nobler and higher than that light. I asked a high-ranking master about this, and he said: In one respect the light is nobler, in the other, the soul; he did not inform me further. Then I entreated God that He might give me to understand how this <margin>5</margin> was. 

Now observe what the supernatural light is! When Christ's soul was created, it came to know itself and was brought above itself into the unity of time; there it was unified. That was not natural to it; it was all above nature, what happened to Christ's soul. This occurrence is called a supernatural light. Through this, Christ's soul can do all that it is capable of in this <margin>10</margin> vision <note>original: *beschenheit*, referring to the state of beholding or contemplation</note>. In this respect, this supernatural [light] is nobler than the soul of Christ, as you yourselves may notice: any thing that adorns another, that by which the one is adorned, is for that reason called nobler [than] that which it adorns. Just as matter is adorned by color, so is the color revealed by the matter, for color has no subsistence in itself. Likewise, Christ's soul is <margin>15</margin> adorned by the supernatural light, and Christ's soul reveals the supernatural light. 

Now observe how the soul is nobler than the light, which is supernatural. When the supernatural light performed its work (49 b) on Christ's soul, that happened in a blink <note>original: *plik*, a common mystical term for an instantaneous moment of divine action</note>; then Christ's soul had nothing more to do with the supernatural light, for the union of divine and human <margin>20</margin> nature happened in a blink all at once in one person. For that reason, the soul of Christ is nobler than the supernatural light. 

Now observe who they are who ever came so far above themselves that they took themselves to be God. That was from nothing more than a supernatural light, which has drawn them so far into it that they saw themselves in it as a light. Look, you may <margin>25</margin> notice this! It happens sometimes that a person strikes their eye so hard that sparks <note>original: *ganster*</note> spring up in their eyes; from the sparks, he sees himself in his eyes. So it happens [that] the supernatural light so completely strips the soul of itself, and the bare spark of the soul, which is called the <term>mind</term> <note>original: *mens*, referring to the highest power of the soul</note>, shines <note>original: *lauchet*</note> against the supernatural light, so that the <margin>30</margin> bare essence of the spirit sees itself in the supernatural light and imagines that it is God. So know that it is nothing else than that the spirit dwells there in a supernatural essence, and that it is yet very perfect. 

There is a question about the worker and the work: whether the work is as noble and as perfect as the master craftsman. This is <margin>35</margin> spoken of the persons in the Trinity <note>original: *drivalticheit*</note>. Understand this: the Father is an origin <note>original: *ursprunk*</note>, having the power to originate an origin like Himself. In that way the Son is not an origin [independently], for He is an origin together with the Father. The Spirit of them both—how is He the master craftsman and the work? That all they work happens equally and perfectly. Therefore Saint Dionysius says that the <margin>40</margin> first Cause <note>original: *sach*</note> causes all things like Himself. A master says this:

<vocab>Cristi sel, ubernaturlich liht, drivalticheit, sanctus Dionisius, werchmeister, creature, ursprunk, ganster, mens</vocab>

It concludes with a Trinitarian discussion on the Father as the "origin" and the equality of the "work" of the Godhead.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's relationship with God, transitioning from the specific nature of Christ’s soul to the general "work" God performs in any soul that has achieved a state of spiritual detachment.</meta>

The work that God works in a <term>detached soul</term> <note>original: *ledigen sel*; "ledig" implies being free, empty, or unencumbered by worldly attachments</note>, bare of all things, is nobler than all the works He ever worked in time, in heaven or on earth. Ah, you should take note of this, how one may understand this! The works that God has done and worked upon the angels in heaven occurred out of the almighty power of God’s might, where He <margin>5</margin> created them from nothing. In that work, He faced no resistance, and it was the same with the soul, which He also created from nothing and to which He also gave free will; for God would never perform any work within the soul against the choice of its free will. Whenever the soul becomes detached and stripped of everything that might hinder God’s most beloved will within her, and she turns with freedom <margin>10</margin> to say "yes" to that noble will—as if she had received it through her own free choice—then God can work as freely within her as He did when He created all things from nothing.

This work maintains two points above all other works: the first is that the free will is no hindrance to God, who nonetheless does not wish to act against that free will. God works as freely as He wills, what He wills, <margin>15</margin> and when and how He wills, as if the soul had no will of its own.

The second point is: just as God is free in His own being, so is this work freely worked. Because it could have faced opposition from the free will, and yet finds no hindrance there, it is for this reason the noblest work that God works in heaven or on earth among <margin>20</margin> His creatures. Ah, now one might ask: what is this work? It is nothing other than a revelation of God Himself, within Himself, in the soul. As essentially as He exists in Himself, so is He Himself present in this work; thus, that in which the work is done is brought into the one who works, in a likeness of the "Worker" who has produced His own <margin>25</margin> likeness there. See, that is the work that is worked: it is as noble as the Worker Himself, since His own likeness is within the work. The <term>image-rich form</term> <note>original: *bildreich form*; referring to the divine ideas or archetypes</note> of God, which has the images of all things enclosed within it in simplicity, therein shines the image of all things, unformed, in simplicity. This same form shines simply as a light in all spirits— <margin>30</margin> distinctly to the higher spirits according to their stability without resistance, and to souls according to this living essence, according to how they are prepared for it within the flux of time. But how richly that image—which the soul has received from this likeness—raises itself above the point of time and this flux, like the highest spirits in eternity! Take note of this: when <margin>35</margin> this happens, the human has more of an attachment, a clinging indwelling with joy in their "image" (which is God), than they have a remaining within themselves; thus the image shines richly to the spirit in its eternal image. In this way, the...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mystical union and the nature of the soul. It transitions into a series of numbered entries (55-57), featuring teachings attributed to "Bishop Albert" (Albertus Magnus) regarding the divine light and the Incarnation.</meta>

If we wish now to become like the naked <note>The Middle High German "bloz" translates to naked or bare, but in a mystical context, it refers to a soul stripped of all worldly attachments and distractions to reach a state of pure spiritual essence.</note> spirit of God, then we must become naked of all mental distractions, just like God's spirit. This nakedness is not known to all spirits: this is no one's fault but our own untrained senses.

Another master says: The soul is a power lifted up into the authority of the Father, a reflecting radiance in the wisdom of the Son <margin>5</margin> and a resting circuit in the sweetness of the Holy Spirit. The soul is as great as the extent to which it grasps eternity; it is as good as the extent to which it loves good things; it is as blessed as the extent to which it has communion with the Godhead.

## No. 57.

Fol. 69<sup>b</sup>. Bishop Albert <note>Likely Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), the influential Dominican scholar and teacher of Thomas Aquinas.</note> said: From the Father flowed a light <margin>15</margin> and it pours itself upon all hearts. In this light, all those have spoken who have preached the Christian faith. That you are ignorant now does not excuse you hereafter. And if you desire to turn toward the light, it would be as ready for you as it was for those before; for just as the spirit falls upon the creatures with an inclination, so he finds his image in them. For this reason, he desires nothing else <margin>20</margin> than that he should be transformed into them. And this could never happen unless he draws his likeness into himself. For just as far as the creatures form themselves in the soul, so far does the soul shine through with the radiance of their form. And so must God also return with his light and with his work into the soul. For the Word that has hovered eternally in your <margin>25</margin> Father, that has taken a bright cloud <note>The "bright cloud" is a common mystical metaphor for the humanity of Christ, which veils yet reveals the divine light of the Word.</note> upon itself; in that bright cloud, all creatures have been made perfect.

A master says: Like unites itself with its like. The Son of the Father has equality <note>A different scribe's handwriting begins here in the manuscript.</note> with his Father, because he is an image of his Father. Therefore it was fitting that he should take on human nature, because <margin>30</margin> in man all creatures are gathered. Therefore it was possible that he took on humanity from his natural mercy for their lost state. For in human nature there was a likeness of the middle person <note>The "middle person" refers to the Son, the second person of the Trinity, positioned between the Father and the Holy Spirit.</note>. That one nature brings forth the other through the intellect,

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse from the previous page, moving from the general influence of divine light to the specific nature of the Trinity and the example of St. John the Evangelist as a model for the soul's union with God.</meta>

...no person does this in any nature except the person of the Father in the divine nature. This is an image <note>original: "gleichnûzz" – often meaning a parable, likeness, or analogy used to explain spiritual truths</note> of the birth <note>original: "geberung"</note> of the persons within eternity.

Out of the Father’s heart a light has shone, which has illuminated all the earth. The shining nature of God has taken upon itself human nature. If human nature then wished [to ascend] onto the mountain of the divine nature <gloss>70 a</gloss> [...] For blessed is the good spirit to whom this happens, that he becomes unified <unclear>shared/communal</unclear> within the divine [nature], for he has all delight with her in herself. The blessed spirits in the kingdom of heaven all reflect themselves in God, for they see God in all creatures, and they see God in themselves and themselves in God, and with all creatures in all creatures, and with God all creatures in God. 

No one may behold themselves in the mirror of God’s wisdom unless they can recognize it in their heart and speak the Word <note>The "Word" (Logos) refers to the second person of the Trinity, Jesus Christ, through whom all things were created.</note> from which all things are created. And no one can reflect themselves in the mirror of God’s wisdom, nor recognize nor speak the Word from which all creatures are created, unless they be without blemish. Whoever recognizes the Word in their heart has great delight in their heart, which no eye has ever fully seen, nor ear ever fully heard, nor ever entered into the heart of man. <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 2:9, describing the joys of heaven.</note> 

Just as it was with John, when John received the Word not as a man alone, but as God within the man, with the outpouring of the grace of His grace, when he said: "In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and God was the Word." <note>The famous opening of the Gospel of John (John 1:1).</note> Whoever does not recognize this as it is, let them love God so that it may be given to them by Him from whom it was given to John. For the grace of God inclined itself toward John and illuminated his light for him, for he did not receive the Word from his humanity; he received it from the Godhead, where he had become one with God.

The soul cannot come to God nor enter into God unless she first comes before God and by God and in God, and free of herself; only then is she united with God, just as John did when he was divested of himself <note>original: "ent-ward" – a mystical term for "un-becoming" or the loss of self-identity during union with the Divine</note> and leaned upon the breast of Jesus Christ. <note>A reference to the "beloved disciple" leaning against Jesus during the Last Supper (John 13:23).</note> In this way, God also cannot come to the soul unless He first comes to the soul and around the soul, and embraces the soul, and permeates the soul with Himself. Then He is completely enclosed within her, and the fountain <note>original: "prunne" – wellspring or source</note> of the Godhead is revealed, and the soul is clothed with God. That this may happen to us, may God help us. Amen.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul's union with God, specifically addressing whether the soul can experience God directly while still bound to the physical body. It transitions from the general mirror of God's wisdom discussed on the previous page to the technical question of "means" or mediation in spiritual experience.</meta>

# No. 59.

Fol. 71 b. Some ask whether the soul may reach the point in this earthly life where it receives [God] without means <note>original: "sunder mittel" — without any intervening instrument, mediator, or secondary cause</note>. The answer that belongs to this is twofold: "No" and "Yes." "No" is to be understood in this way: for whatever the soul is to receive, it must receive either without means or through the light and <margin>5</margin> grace. Therefore, grace itself is the means, for it is a created thing <note>original: "creatûr" — in this context, grace is seen as a gift created for the soul, rather than God’s own uncreated essence</note>. The soul cannot easily bypass this means while in the body; however, after the life of the body, it no longer has anything to do with this means. For the masters say that the divine nature then becomes a naked object <note>original: "bloz furwûrf" — a direct, unmediated presence presented to the soul's vision</note> to the soul. From this, the soul receives full power specifically and grace, which is God Himself. Yet even then, you <margin>10</margin> may note a new kind of "means" in unity. The unification of the soul with God in all sufficiency—that very "mid-point" <note>the author puns on the word "mittel," which can mean both a "means/instrument" and a "middle/center"</note> is the means, for God is uniquely One without any "with" <note>original: "on mit" — without attachment to anything else</note>. Thus the soul is one with God. This "unity" is the means that the soul can never lose, neither here nor hereafter.

The second part, which says that the soul receives without means <margin>15</margin> in this body, you may understand in this way: because the soul has within itself a likeness <note>original: "geleicheit"</note> of the highest God. Through this likeness, it receives the Like; there, like is received by like without means. See, those who wish to receive without means should remain constantly in this likeness. <margin>20</margin>

Now one might ask: Wherein does this likeness lie? See, you may note this: the likeness of the highest God lies in the "other-worldliness" <note>original: "uberirlicheit" — the state of being above earthly or material concerns</note> of the inner and outer person—that is, in an unchangeability <term>unwandelhaftikeit: the quality of being steadfast and unaffected by the shifting world</term> regarding all lower things, so that the outer person is not changed by them, and also [stays free] from all movements of the spirit, so that the inner person is not <margin>25</margin> disturbed by them. Instead, it remains steadily <note>The original OCR note suggests "sterclichen" (strongly) as an alternative to "stetlichen" (steadily)</note> in an eternal, present "Now" <term>ewigen genwurtigen nu: the mystical concept of the "Eternal Now" where time does not exist</term> as one, so that it may be the same in all things. Regarding this, Christ speaks to his disciples: "If you remain steady in my words and my words remain in you, then I shall release the truth," <note>A paraphrase of John 8:31-32</note> that is, the truth that exists before all changeability. Saint Augustine says: "O Lord, what is it that I feel so sweetly within me <margin>30</margin> and that makes me so foreign to myself? If it remained steadily within me, that would be the true eternal life." If you feel this within you, you should know that you do not feel it in this body as anything other than a (72) prelude to eternal life.

Master Richard <margin>35</margin> <note>Likely Richard of Saint Victor, a 12th-century Scottish theologian famous for his works on mystical contemplation</note> also says: "O soul, tell me, what is it that clings to me so blissfully and touches me beyond myself? Tell me, is it God or a creature?" He himself doubts here. Yet you may note the explanation: at first, he took it for God, because it transformed and...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's reception of God, moving from the concept of "means" or mediation to the radical transformation of the soul as it enters the "unnamed essence" of God, leaving behind its identity as a created thing.</meta>

— 60 —

...power makes the supreme good immeasurably lovable in all things, just as it is itself immeasurable. Secondly, the soul is anxious that it might be a creature <note>original: "creatur" — any created thing, as opposed to the uncreated God</note>, for whatever the soul has grasped within itself must be a creaturely thing. You
<margin>5</margin>should note this: All physical things love themselves spiritually according to their spiritual form. Since their spiritual form is a creature, then everything the soul has grasped within itself must be a creature. Regarding this, a master, Manducator <note>referring to Peter Comestor (d. 1178), a famous theologian whose nickname means "the Eater" because he "consumed" the scriptures</note>, said: "You do nothing else but ask whether you will not become spiritual, whether it be God or a creature." Mark this: it is truly
<margin>10</margin>God! You may note this in things: that you become a stranger to yourself, and that which is within you—it takes you out of yourself into itself. In this state of being "taken up," the spirit becomes "de-spirited" <note>original: "entgeistet" — a mystical term for the soul being stripped of its own separate, created identity to unite with God's essence</note>. Regarding this, Saint Dionysius says that the soul is then not called "soul"; it is called the unnamed essence. This is the essence outside of its own spirit. Everything that the soul receives is so small
<margin>15</margin>compared to being in God outside of its own spirit; there, she is more God than she is a creature. There, she has left behind her "something" and stands upon her "nothing." There, she has neither work, nor form, nor possession; she has it instead in essence.

Now one might ask whether to this de-spirited spirit God is a spirit or not. Saint Augustine says God is a spirit; yet Dionysius says
<margin>20</margin>God is not a spirit. Now note how God is a spirit. The most nimble and most subtle word that a creature can manage is "spirit." Therefore, with the closest word she can manage, the creature names God and says He is a spirit. But because creatures fail in understanding and in words, therefore Dionysius says He is
<margin>25</margin>not a spirit; He is more inward to all spirits than the spirits themselves.

Oh, what wealth and what bliss floats in that likeness, where the de-spirited spirit and (de-spiritedness) God are both one de-spiritedness!

The person who is set in rest and peace regarding all things is like Jerusalem. In Jerusalem there was a mountain called Zion, upon which stood the temple of God. The soul that is like Zion shall be raised above herself 
<margin>25</margin>and shall be a dwelling of God in her very highest part. That we may arrive at this, etc.

### Nr. 64.

Fol. 75 a. Our Lady <note>the Virgin Mary</note> gazed up into the mirror of the Godhead. In this gazing, the soul has seven manners of gazing. First, she enters into a wonder; in this wonder she enters into a forgetting of all creatures and transcends all forms. Now she has come beyond the 
<margin>35</margin>very highest form of the gazing power. Oh, fair soul, now remain steady and look and do [not] turn back to that which you have surpassed!

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s ascent, moving from the definition of the "Israelite" soul as one who beholds God to the specific stages of spiritual gazing (contemplation). It utilizes the common medieval mystical trope of Mary and Martha to distinguish between active and contemplative life.</meta>

For the second time, she <note>The "she" refers to the soul (Middle High German: *si/sele*).</note> enters into a light-filled beholding. In this light-filled beholding, she goes outside of herself and speaks like **Abraham** to his servant and to his donkey when he said: "Stay here below, I shall return." <note>Abraham’s sacrifice of Isaac was often interpreted as the soul leaving behind "beastly" nature (the donkey) to encounter God. Original Latin: *Exodus 24:14* or *Genesis 22:5*.</note> Upon this <insert>speaks</insert> **Saint Bernard** of the soul, saying that she speaks: "I shall return poor as soon [as I can]." **Mary Magdalene** sat at our Lord's feet and heard his word. This **Martha** blamed, and she spoke: "Lord, why do you let my sister let me work alone?" Now it speaks, as it seems to me, that Mary said: "Lord, speak further; let the ignorant chatter!" This belongs to the light-filled beholding.

For the third time, she comes into a secure and into a free beholding, wherein she forgets all pictorial things <note>Images or mental representations used in prayer.</note>. Regarding this, **Saint Bernard** speaks: Fear and anxiety and worry (leave her) and all intention, desire, will, and understanding gather themselves into a simplicity.

For the fourth time, she sees <insert>t</insert> without looking back; for then she has come over the hindrance of the lowest powers <note>The "lowest powers" typically refer to the physical senses and basic emotions.</note>. Regarding this, **Saint Bernard** speaks: When the soul gathers into herself and rises up and gazes, it would be much easier for her to suffer death than to return back here.

For the fifth time, she sees without any worry.

For the sixth time, she is confirmed and see<insert>t</insert>s steadily remaining without any hindrance.

For the seventh time, she sees without time and without measure.

If it is then the case that she comes beyond (75 b) time and beyond measure, she reaches the point where she knows neither God nor creature. For that in which she [previously] knew is a **creature** <term>creature: in mystical texts, this refers to anything created (people, objects, thoughts), as opposed to the uncreated God</term>; therefore, she cannot know God [in that way], nor does she know the creature, for she has come beyond the time and measure of creatures. Then she is drawn into a **groundless ground** <note>original: "gruntlosen grunt" — a central concept in Rhineland mysticism describing the infinite depth of God where the soul loses its individual identity</note>: there she is understood, there she is grasped, there she has a sense without sense, etc.

**Our Lord** spoke to Martha: "Martha, you are far too anxious; one thing is necessary, and that is that you leave all things and fall purely into one thing." How purely? Not so that <insert>you</insert> might purely see God as he is in his nature, but rather that you go with your understanding into God and see that he is a God whom no one can understand with any senses, but rather that he understands himself. There you should increase, and in this increasing, all mental images vanish from you. There one knows of God that one knows nothing (and this knowing in your unknowing you are creatures). Now I place this into the senses, of which our Lady has spoken.

<meta>This page continues the instructional sequence on the soul's spiritual progress. Having previously discussed the four stages of "beholding" or contemplation, the text now shifts to practical requirements for maintaining a "free heart" and details the mystical experience of Saint Paul as a model for the soul's vision of God.</meta>

- 63 -

Whoever now wishes to have a free heart must possess nine qualities within himself. The first is love <note>original: *minne*; in this context, a mystical, divine love</note>, for within it the soul unites herself with God. The second is divine rest. The third is that your will and the divine will be one single will; in this way, all your anxiety will be taken away. Whether friends die and depart from you, <margin>5</margin> or wherever they may go, you shall stand free of it all and remain unmoved; to put it briefly: the very fear of hell will be taken from you. The fourth is a pure and a clear conscience <note>original: *consciencia*</note>, so that you seek nothing else than what belongs to your conscience; thus you should test yourself at all times: whatever causes you to falter even once, you should guard yourself against it forever. The fifth is that you <margin>10</margin> be free from all capital sins <note>The "seven deadly sins" or primary vices.</note>. The sixth is that you should guard your senses against all entanglement. The seventh is that whatever you feel arising or struggling within you, you should piously let it be without any kind of contradiction. The eighth is that you should order all your works with discernment <note>original: *bescheidenheit*; spiritual discretion or moderation</note>. The ninth is that you should acknowledge the truth and constantly <margin>15</margin> cling to it with your entire being. For as much as you possess truth, so much do you possess wisdom; and as much as you possess wisdom, so much do you possess truth. This is to be understood regarding every loving soul, etc.

->Nr. 65.<-

<margin>Fol. 75 b.</margin> Saint Paul <note>original: *Sanctus Paulus*</note> was snatched up into the third heaven.

Saint Paul (76) says that if it were for God’s glory, he would forgo God <note>This likely refers to Romans 9:3, where Paul wishes to be cut off from Christ for the sake of his people.</note> for the sake of the Word, so that God would be praised all the more through him. Saint Paul saw God just as the highest angels see him; they see God in his "garment-house" <note>original: *kleidhaus*; a mystical term referring to the manifestations or "clothing" of the divine essence</note>. <margin>25</margin> Regarding this, Saint Paul says: Whoever wishes to see God in his garment-house <note>original: *kleidechuz*</note> must possess seven qualities within himself. The first is that the person have a sweet heart and a gentle mind toward all people. The second is that whatever may happen to the person, he should remain entirely patient within it; moreover, he should also remain in his own essence just as he was before. The <margin>30</margin> third is that whatever good fortune <note>original: *gelůk*</note> happens to the person, he is not so moved by it that it becomes harmful to him. The fourth is that whatever misfortune happens to a person, regardless of the cause, he nonetheless remains unmoved in his essence. The fifth is that the person should go into his soul and toward his soul and above his soul <margin>35</margin> and should see if there is any darkness <note>original: *vinsternůzzes*</note> in his soul, so that he...

<meta>This page continues a sequence of spiritual instructions. ...piously cast out, or he cannot see God. The sixth is that the person should go above all created things. For God is unmeasured <note>original: *ungemezzen*; meaning infinite or beyond limit</note>, and therefore the person must recognize truth beyond all measured things. The seventh is that everything that the person was, he should [no longer] be.

<margin>5</margin> A master says: All those who are in the cold do not freeze, but rather those who feel the cold are the ones who freeze. If someone steps on my foot, my mouth says: "Who is stepping on me?" If someone steps on another, my mouth says nothing. This is because my spirit is not his spirit. In the same way, a person should have become so much one spirit with God 

<margin>10</margin> that whatever insult was offered to God would be more painful to him than if he himself were killed.

When our Lord struck Saint Paul <note>original: *sanctum Paulum*</note> at his conversion, our Lord said to him: 
> Saul, Saul, why do you persecute me? <note>original: *Saule, Saule, wez durchiagst du mich?*; Acts 9:4</note> 

In this way, God has spiritualized Himself into the good soul; whatever one does to the person, that troubles 

<margin>15</margin> God. This is because of the great union <note>original: *einunge*; a central term in German mysticism referring to the soul's merging with God</note> that God has with the soul. Thus, nothing at all should trouble the person—whatever might be done to them—because of the great union that the soul should have with God; except only this: whatever he sees that is against God, that alone should trouble him; and that also comes from the right union of the spirit. King David says: 

<margin>20</margin> > Blessed are they, Lord, who dwell in your house; for they shall live and praise you eternally. <note>Psalm 84:4</note> 

God’s house is nothing other than eternity. Before the gate of this house, we should go for [our] bread. A Greek pagan master <note>Likely a reference to Aristotle or Plato, often cited as "the Master" or "the Heathen" in medieval texts.</note> says: When natural virtue is not directed toward eternal things, it loses its name, so that it cannot be called virtue. Saint 

<margin>25</margin> Augustine says: When we contemplate eternal things, we are no longer in the world. The reason we do not find God is our own fault: we look for Him in time and in measure. Now King David says: 

> Who are they, Lord, who dwell in your house? <note>Psalm 15:1 / 24:3</note> 

These are those who are innocent, and those who are of pure heart, and those who deceive no one 

<margin>30</margin> and those who cannot be deceived by anyone, and those who are unencumbered in their soul, etc.

### Nr. 66.

Fol. 76 b. Our Lord says in the Gospel of John <note>original: *ewangelio Johannis*</note>: 
> Abide in me! <note>John 15:4</note> 

For whenever one abides, one does not move. Movement 

<margin>35</margin> occurs in the powers <note>The "faculties" of the soul, such as memory, intellect, and will.</note> and in thoughts; and when one remains unmoved in the powers and in thoughts, that is called a state and a clinging and an image of this. As long as a cloth can become whiter, it is moved; however, when it can become no whiter, it remains...

It emphasizes that a soul in union with God is troubled only by what offends God and seeks to dwell in the "house" of eternity rather than the world of time.</summary>

— 65 —

...it, so it is. Thus it is concerning the will: when the will clings to God, whatever then falls into the thoughts, however evil it is, it does not matter at all. For the will is so free that no one can bind it; and what you do not want, that also is not [attributed to you]. And if the contemplative faculty <note>original: *beschawerinne*; likely referring to the power of contemplation or the soul in its role as a "beholder"</note> were as free—as free as the will is—then she would be without ceasing in the naked Godhead. <margin>5</margin> This is not the case, because she has work to perform; the will does not have this, rather it commands and forbids. For the understanding <note>original: *verstantnůzz*</note> understands what is true and passes through it, from which it receives delight, and in that delight it becomes its own, because the will desires it, etc.

<margin>10</margin> In seven things one can notice whether a person loves their spiritual friend in God. The first is that he does not often desire to see [him]. The second: when he sees him, that his desire and his speech are such as if he did not care whether he saw him or not. The third is that he does not care to speak with him except about God. The fourth is, when he thinks of his friend in his <margin>15</margin> prayer, that he is all the more stirred to prayer and to devotion <note>original: *andaht*; a state of focused, inward spiritual attention</note>. The fifth is that he can well endure with a peaceful heart if [the friend] loves another person as much as him, or even more. The sixth is that he never seeks to increase the friendship with any kind of small gifts <note>original: *kleinet*; small items of jewelry or trinkets used as tokens of affection</note>. The seventh is whether he can rebuke <note>original: *gerůgen*; to reprimand or correct a fault</note> his friend as well as another, or even better, in proportion to how <margin>20</margin> much dearer he is to him and how much he loves him.

Particular love <note>original: *Sunderlich lieb*; refers to "particular friendships" or exclusive emotional attachments that were discouraged in monastic life</note> without guard is a false lover and is an interloper against the true spouse <note>original: *gemaheln*; Christ as the bridegroom of the soul</note> and is a destroyer of peace and is a desirer of vice and is a joiner of sin and is a lair of the devil and is a voice in confession and is a <margin>25</margin> liar in questioning and is a corrupter of secrets and is a noise-maker among spiritual people and is a bastard child <note>original: *kebeskint*; literally the child of a concubine</note> in herself and is blind in the true light and is dead in divine wisdom and is a speaker of the secrets of the eyes and is a murderer of the <margin>30</margin> will and is an opening of great blindness and is a stirrer of eternal death and is a perverter of eternal life.

> Saint Bernard <note>original: *Sanctus Bernhardus*</note> says: God's comfort is sweet and is not given to those who seek other comforts.

> Hilary <note>original: *Ilarius*</note> says: Whoever wants to have the Holy Spirit must be released from himself and be unmixed and have nothing in common with envy. (And is a flatterer in conversion <note>original: *bekerung*</note>.) <margin>35</margin>

<meta>This page continues the collection of mystical treatises and sayings, moving from the ethics of spiritual friendship on page 65 to a structured exploration of "perfection." It specifically examines the dual necessity of self-knowledge and the knowledge of God, drawing on the authority of Saint Augustine and the "negative theology" of Pseudo-Dionysius.</meta>

### Number 67.

Folio 77. The Godhead is purely enclosed, etc. <note>original: *bloz beslozzen*; refers to the divine essence being both "naked" or unmanifest and yet self-contained</note>. Pfeiffer 521. Then this repeats on Folio 5a, see above Number 3.

### Number 68.

Folio 80a. Three things are to be noted in which perfection lies. The first is pure knowing without error. The second is whole loving, inseparably. The third is perceptive enjoyment without weariness <note>original: *entsebelich gebrauchen sunder verdruz*; "entsebelich" suggests a spiritual "tasting" or sensing</note>.

Saint Augustine <note>original: *Sanctus Augustinus*</note> speaks of knowledge in this way: "Lord, grant that I may know You and know myself, then it is enough," <note>original: *Herre, gib mir, daz ich dich und mich bekenne*; a translation of the Latin *Noverim me, noverim Te*</note> for in this lies all the blessedness of rational spirits. This is a common saying, that one should know oneself; but how one truly knows oneself lies in three things. 

The first is that one should recognize that one is a creature of God, so that we, with true humility, [place ourselves] under the powerful hand of our creator. The second is that we should recognize that we are a <term>working-tool</term> <note>original: *werkgezeug*; an instrument or vessel through which God acts</note> of the Holy Trinity, which works in us a constant readiness to hold ourselves for Him, just as every tool is ready for the master craftsman. This also works in us a likeness to Him, for wherever the Holy Trinity works, there she works and transforms <note>original: *verwurket*</note>; she works away unlikeness and works in her own likeness. Thirdly, we should know ourselves as the image of God, in that He has formed us after Him. This works in us true <term>union</term> <note>original: *einung*; the mystical joining of the soul with God</note>. Remain in Him, for it is a great shame and a vice for someone who bears the mark of his Lord to turn to anyone else. This also reminds us that since we naturally bear God's image in our soul, we should constantly remain in the one whose image we bear.

Whoever wishes to know themselves rightly and nobly should know themselves in this way.

The second part is that one should know God, as Saint Augustine preaches. Three things belong here as well. The first is that whatever we recognize, we should recognize that that thing is *not* God, as Saint Dionysius <note>Saint Dionysius refers to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century theologian who taught that God is best known by "unknowing" or by saying what He is not (Apophatic theology)</note> says. The second way to know God is that we should know Him in a "sensing," whereby we are separated from ourselves and from all things and sense Him alone. The third way of knowing God is so very great that no one can possess it perfectly in this body: that is, that one knows Him in His own <term>property</term> <note>original: *eigenschaft*; meaning God's essential nature or "Selfhood"</note>. As long as a person is left in their own self-interest/individuality, they cannot know God in His essence; but if someone is snatched out of their own selfhood for a moment, as—

---

<note>12 that one often.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the components of spiritual perfection, focusing on the three ways to know oneself as a creature and image of God, and the three ways to know God, culminating in the "Negative Theology" that defines God by what He is not.</summary>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "perfection" from the previous page, specifically concluding the section on the first requirement (knowledge) and moving into the second (love) and third (spiritual enjoyment). It follows the structure of a mystical handbook, likely influenced by the teachings of Meister Eckhart or his followers.</meta>

Saint Paul <note>original: "sancto Paulo"</note> experienced this; he recognized God in His true nature, just as Paul was known by Him. This is called true recognition without error.

The second part is whole love, inseparable, which surely makes innocent <note>original: "unschuldik"; in this context, it means both "without guilt" and "free from the debt of sin"</note> everything for which a person was ever guilty. This love is also to be understood
<margin>5</margin> in three points. The first is: whatever a person holds dear in this world, as soon as they perceive that it is a hindrance to the love of Him <note>God</note>, then they should let go of this earthly love for the sake of His love.

The second point of true love is this: that we be so mighty and powerful within ourselves now, that whatever pain or mockery or any kind of
<margin>10</margin> suffering comes to us, we are well able to endure it through love. For whoever possesses love, everything they suffer or carry or leave behind for that love is too small a price to pay.

The third point of true love is that we perceive <gloss>are aware</gloss> that love has so sweetened our hearts that we do not know whether people
<margin>15</margin> hate us or love us. This is to be understood in this way: not that we do not recognize what people do toward us, but rather that we should have lost that distinction in our <term>resentment</term> <note>original: "widersin"; literally a "contrary-sense" or an adverse emotional reaction to being mistreated</note>, so that we are no less kind to the one who hates us than to the one who loves us. We should dwell so deeply in
<margin>20</margin> the essence of love that no one can make us different, so that we now become "love with love." Whatever is done against love, love remains love nonetheless. Love is innocent of all things, for its only "debt" is love itself. And when we are then "loves of love," we are innocent of all guilt, for our only debt shall be love.

Oh, how blessed and perfect would the person be who was like this!

<margin>25</margin> The third requirement is <term>perceptive enjoyment</term> <note>original: "ensebelich gebrauchen"; "gebrauchen" (fruitio) is a technical term for the soul's enjoyment of God</note> without weariness. That also lies in three points. The first is a powerful delight of the spirit, which overcomes all worldly pleasures that occur in transient ways. The second point is a <term>yearning</term> <note>original: "nidung"; usually means "envy," but in mystical texts, it refers to a "zealous longing" or "emulation"</note> that pulls the spirit out of the senses into that which it yearns for.

The third point that belongs to true enjoyment is this: that the
<margin>30</margin> spirit has lost its "own-ness" <note>its self-centered identity</note> in that same thing which it enjoys, and enjoys according to <term>His-ness</term> <note>original: "giensheit"; from "jener" (that/the other), meaning God's perspective</note> and not according to <term>self-ness</term> <note>original: "sinsheit"; from "sein" (one's own), meaning the human perspective</note>, as far as that is possible.

Oh, well is them who are like this! This is to enjoy without weariness. Lord
Jesus Christ, help your poor creatures to achieve it!

### Number 69.

<margin>35</margin> Folio 81a. Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, an influential mystical theologian</note> speaks of three kinds of light that the soul must have if it is to come into a pure knowledge of God. The first is natural, the second is spiritual, the third is divine.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul's ascent toward perfection. Having previously discussed the nature of love and the removal of earthly hindrances, the text now categorizes the three types of "light" or modes of understanding—natural, spiritual, and glorious—through which a person comes to know God.</meta>

Now take note of what the <term>natural light</term> <note>original: "naturlich lieht"; the innate human capacity for reason and logic inherent in the soul from birth</note> is and how far one may come with it into a knowledge of God! By nature, the soul has the capacity to understand all things that exist, and that they do not exist of themselves. Thus, there must
<margin>5</margin>be one thing that exists of itself and from no one else but from its own being: whatever that is, it has created all things.

The soul also understands by nature: all the goodness that is spread throughout all things is contained entirely within the single cause of all things. The soul also has the capacity by nature to love every thing according to how it is good. When it has then come, through natural knowledge,
<margin>10</margin>to the cause of all things, and realizes that all the goodness spread through all things is contained entirely within the cause of all things, from this natural knowledge there springs within it a natural love for that which is the cause of all things. <margin>12 that it [is] the cause.</margin>

All creatures are fragile and changeable—not in their
<margin>15</margin>essence (that it should perish), but rather in the attainment of their perfection. Regarding this, <term>Saint Augustine</term> <note>Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational church father often cited by medieval mystics regarding the nature of the mind and memory</note> says: The soul cannot remain for long upon a single thought; it falls out of one and into another. It also cannot have many thoughts at once; whatever it falls away from, to that it dies, and it lives
(81 ᵇ) in the other. Since God then has no commonality with
<margin>20</margin>creatures, it is proven by this that no fragility exists in Him who has no commonality with creatures: that is [God] alone, and in as much as the soul possesses His likeness, to that extent is it un-fragile. Thus the soul by nature knows and loves God above all things.

<margin>25</margin>The second light is spiritual; it springs forth in faith. For everything that faith has enclosed within itself, the soul cannot reach by its own nature. Faith is this: that three persons exist in one essence, and one essence in three persons. For this, all natural light and understanding is too small, because no natural light can
<margin>30</margin>provide any likeness <note>original: "geleichnuzz"; an analogy or comparison from the physical world</note> for this. For everything that the three persons work or are capable of, that comes out of a single unity; though they are three persons, they do not work as three, but rather they work as one God. This is a spiritual light with which the soul, in faith, perceives as truly that it is so, as if its natural understanding had ever
<margin>35</margin>given it anything.

The third is a light of glory, which is a divine light. The soul receives this in the highest power of the soul. In this light, one recognizes God <term>without a mediator</term> <note>original: "sůnder mittel"; direct, unmediated mystical union or vision of God, bypassing images or concepts</note>. As far as this light sinks into the highest power,

<summary>This page defines the first two "lights" of the soul—natural reason and spiritual faith—explaining how reason recognizes God as the First Cause while faith allows for the understanding of the Trinity. It also highlights the soul's inherent instability compared to God's unchanging nature.</summary>
<keywords>natural light, soul, knowledge of God, Saint Augustine, fragility of creatures, Trinity, spiritual light, light of glory, unmediated vision</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "lights" of understanding. Having previously described how the soul uses natural reason to recognize God as the cause of all goodness, the text now transitions into a deeper, mystical "unmediated" knowledge of God's own fathomless nature.</meta>

In this way, God is known without mediation. In this light, the soul recognizes the nobility of all things in God; for everything that has ever flowed out, or ever flows out, or ever shall flow out, possesses eternal being and life in God. This is not in the fragile way that life exists in <term>creatures</term> <note>referring to all "created things," including animals, plants, and humans, as opposed to the uncreated Creator</note>, but rather as His own essence, <margin>5</margin> because it is His nature. God does not possess His own essence from nothing; He has it from His own nature, which truly is something in itself and does not depend on the perception of any creature.

This nature is <term>fathomless</term> <note>original: "grundloz"; literally "without a ground" or bottomless. In medieval mysticism, this refers to the infinite depth of the Divine that exceeds human reason</note>; therefore, it cannot be plumbed except by a fathomless understanding. The understanding of all creatures is <margin>10</margin> measured, and for that reason, it has a "bottom" or limit; therefore, it cannot grasp the fathomless understanding, nor Christ, nor [the mystery of] His humanity. When God beholds His own nature, which is fathomless, it can be grasped by nothing except by a fathomless understanding. This understanding is nothing other than what His nature itself is: thus God alone grasps Himself in <margin>15</margin> His own nature. This grasping is an understanding in which God is manifest to Himself and considers Himself a light that no one can approach. As <term>Saint Paul</term> says: God dwells in an unapproachable light <note>referencing 1 Timothy 6:16</note>. Saint Paul says: "Everything that was dishonorable, I never did; and everything that was honorable, I never <insert>neglected</insert> <note>the scribe corrected the text from "despised" (versmahet) to "neglected" (versaumet)</note> <margin>20</margin> and I possess all virtue to the highest degree, and I never spoke nor taught (82 a) anything that I myself was not. Beyond all that, I am better than I am willing or able to say to anyone, for I neither wish nor am able <insert>must</insert> to show or reveal myself further with words, because people could not bear or endure it. No one can or should <margin>25</margin> present an image to me, for I cannot be improved by any man’s image or teaching, because I myself am as close to God as this: that I myself can draw and take from within God everything that I want and that I need. I have been with Peter and with John, the highest [apostles], and I departed from them in such a way that I was never improved one bit by them. For this, I thank you, Lord <margin>30</margin> Jesus Christ, that I have kept my heart in a simple state at all times throughout all my conduct, so that I have never yet been offended by anything, since I first began to understand myself."

->No. 69.<-

Folio 82. It was the evening of that day when our Lord came to His <margin>35</margin> disciples and stood in the midst and said: "Peace be with you!" <note>quoting John 20:19</note> Now He says it was the evening of the day. Just as the heat of midday breaks through the air and makes it hot, the heat of the evening then joins it and becomes even hotter: then, in the evening, it is hottest of all from the accumulation of heat.

<summary>This page describes God’s essence as a "fathomless" nature that can only be understood by Himself, featuring a mystical monologue attributed to Saint Paul regarding his direct union with the Divine. It concludes with an allegorical interpretation of the "evening" as a time of peak spiritual heat or intensity during Christ's appearance to the disciples.</summary>
<keywords>divine nature, fathomless (grundloz), Saint Paul, unapproachable light, essence, unmediated knowledge, mystical union, Jesus Christ, heat of the evening</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul's journey into the divine light. While the previous page discussed the fathomless nature of God's understanding, the text now transitions into an allegory of time—morning, midday, and evening—representing the stages of the soul’s progression toward divine love, as well as a philosophical discussion on God as the "place" of all existence.</meta>

Just as the year has its evening—which is August, the time when the year is at its hottest—so too is there an evening in a **God-loving** <term>soul</term> <note>original: "gotminnende sele"; a soul characterized by "Minne," the Middle High German term for intense, mystical love</note>. This evening is a bitter repentance that has been fully broken through and is burning with heat in divine love. Therefore he says: *It was the evening of the day.* In that spiritual day, morning, midday, and evening remain as one together and do not pass away; but in this temporal day of time, <margin>5</margin> morning and midday pass away, and then the evening falls. It is not so in the "day" of the soul, where they remain as one. The natural light of the soul is the morning, when the soul breaks into the highest and purest part of that light and thus enters into the light of the <term>angels</term> <note>In medieval theology, angels were seen as beings of pure intellect and light who mediate between God and humanity</note>. In that light, it is mid-<margin>10</margin>morning; and then the soul ascends with the angel's light into the divine light, which is the midday. The soul then remains in the light of God and in a state of pure repentance; that is the evening, and there the heat of divine love is at its greatest.

Now he says: *It was the evening of the day.* Then the day is within the soul. <margin>15</margin> **Jacob the patriarch** came to a place and wished to rest in the evening, when the sun had set <note>referencing Genesis 28:11, the story of Jacob’s Ladder</note>. He says: "in a place," but he does not name it. That place is God. God has no proper name; He is a "place" and is the <term>foundation</term> <note>original: "setzung"; meaning the establishment, position, or ordering of all things</note> of all things, the natural place of all <term>creatures</term>. The heavens <margin>20</margin> have no place in their highest and purest state; rather, in the descent of their influence, they are the place and foundation for all physical things that are beneath them. And fire is the place where it meets the air, and the air is the place of the water and the earth. A "place" is that which has received me, in which <margin>23</margin> I stand. Thus the air has surrounded the earth and the water. The more <term>refined</term> <note>original: "kleinlicher"; literally "smaller" or "more minute," but in medieval physics, it refers to "subtle" matter that is more powerful and pervasive than "gross" or heavy matter</note> a thing is, the more powerful it is. Because of this, it can work upon those things that are coarser and are beneath it. The earth cannot <margin>25</margin> be an angelic "place," because it is too coarse and is also the lowest of the elements. Water is partially a "place," because it is more subtle, and for that reason, it is more powerful. The more powerful and refined an element is, the better it serves as the foundation and place for another. Thus the heavens are the place of all physical things, and <margin>30</margin> the heavens have no place that is physical; rather, the lowest angel is its "place," its order, and its foundation. And so it goes, up and up: each angel is the place and foundation of the one below it. The highest angel is the place and foundation of all the other angels that are beneath him, and he himself has no place nor <term>matter</term> <note>original: "materie"; the physical substance of which things are made</note>.

<margin>35</margin> God, however, is His own wall and is His own place, and He is pure spirit. God is not "spirit" in the sense of **Saint Gregory’s** words, who says that all our words that we speak of God are but a "stuttering" about God <note>referencing Gregory the Great's idea that human language is fundamentally inadequate to describe the Divine</note>. Therefore he says: *He came into a place.* That place is God, who gives <margin>38</margin> foundation and order to all things.

<margin>40</margin> I have said more: God’s "least" things are all creatures...

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of the "evening of the day" to describe the soul's peak of divine love and repentance, while also explaining a hierarchical cosmology where God is the ultimate "place" and foundation for all existence.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, repentance, Jacob the patriarch, elements, cosmology, Saint Gregory, soul's light, angelic hierarchy</keywords>
