<meta>This page continues the sermon's exploration of the soul's relationship with God, moving from the allegory of time (morning, midday, and evening) into a philosophical discussion on the nature of the "place" where the soul finds rest. The text emphasizes that God is a "nowhere" that provides the "somewhere" for all existence, and it delves into the internal faculties of the soul—the will and the intellect.</meta>

and live and grow and turn green therein, and His "most" <note>The author uses the superlative "meistes" to describe God's supreme or ultimate nature</note> is nowhere. As long as the soul is "somewhere," it is not in God’s "most," which is "nowhere."

Now he says: he wished to rest in the "place" <note>original: "stat"; referring to the site where Jacob rested, interpreted here as the divine essence</note>. All wealth and all poverty and blessedness lie in the **will**. The <term>will</term> <note>Middle High German: "wille"; considered by many medieval theologians as the highest faculty because of its inherent freedom</note> is so free and so noble that it <margin>5</margin> takes from no bodily things; rather, from its own freedom, it performs its work. **Intellect** <note>original: "Vernuftikeit"</note> indeed takes from bodily things: in this regard the will is nobler; but it is part of the intellect [in one] in its beauty and in a descent, where the understanding takes images from bodily things; but in the highest [part], the intellect works without the addition of <margin>10</margin> bodily things. A great master says: Everything that is carried in through the senses does not reach or enter the highest power of the <term>soul</term> <note>original: "sele"</note>. **Saint Augustine** says—and **Plato**, a pagan master, says also—that the soul naturally has all knowledge within itself; therefore it does not need to draw knowledge into itself from the outside; rather, through the revelation of external skill, <margin>15</margin> the knowledge that is naturally hidden in the soul becomes manifest. Like a physician who cleans my eyes well and removes the obstacle that hinders sight, but he does not give me the sight of the eye. The power of the soul, which naturally works in the eyes, alone gives birth to sight in the eyes once the obstacle is gone. Likewise, everything <margin>20</margin> that is carried into the senses by images (83) and forms does not give light to the soul; rather, it only prepares and purifies the soul so that, in its highest part, it may receive purely the light of the angel and, with it, [into] divine light.

Now he says: **Jacob** wished to rest in the place. The "place" is God and divine being, which gives place and life and being and <margin>25</margin> order to all things. In that place the soul shall rest in the highest and in the innermost part of the place. And in that same **ground** <note>original: "grůnt"; a central concept in Eckhart's mysticism referring to the essential, undifferentiated nature of God and the soul</note> where He has His own rest, there we too shall take our rest and possess it with Him.

The place is unnamed, and no one can speak a proper [word] of it. Every word that we can speak of it is more a denial of <margin>30</margin> what God is *not*, than it is an affirmation of what He *is* <note>This refers to "apophatic" or negative theology, the idea that God is so transcendent that we can only describe Him by what He is not</note>. A great master saw this and reflected that whatever he could speak in words about God, he could not speak properly; there lay therein something improper and false. Therefore he became silent and would never speak another word, and yet he was greatly mocked by other masters. For this reason, it is much more a matter of being silent <margin>35</margin> about God than speaking.

Now he also says: *It was the evening of the day* <note>original: "Ez waz abent dez tags"; referencing John 20:19</note>, when our Lord stood in the midst of His disciples and said: *Peace be with you!* <note>original: "Frid sei mit euch!"</note> That we may come to eternal peace and into the unnamed place, which is the divine being, may He help us, etc. ¹

### No. 73.

->Folio 87b. A master says: A good woman has opened her mouth <margin>10</margin> to wisdom and has tasted and seen how good the purchase is <gloss>the business</gloss> of eternal joys. Therefore her light was never extinguished, even in the night of adversity <note>original: "widermûts"; referring to suffering or spiritual trials</note>.<-

I have spoken of two kinds of wisdom: one that is God, the other that is not God and yet is from God, just as the radiance is from the sun.
<margin>15</margin> This is a gift of God and a ... <note>The manuscript contains a gap here.</note> of divine nature. With this wisdom, one gains for oneself the ability to become blessed while still in this body.

A master says that the <term>soul</term> <note>original: "sele"</note> is a place or a corner where both **time** and **eternity** meet; and yet it is made neither of time nor of eternity, but is a nature made from nothing between
<margin>20</margin> both of them. If it were made of time, it would be transitory; if it were made of eternity, it would be unchangeable. Therefore, it is made of neither time nor eternity, because it is [both] changeable and imperishable.

**Saint Augustine** says that the soul is made from the most
<margin>25</margin> noble and secret "nothing," which is so delightful that it is better to search for it all the days of our lives than for us to ever [fully] find it. For this reason, the soul is so noble that both time and eternity touch upon it. If it leans toward temporal things, it becomes darkened, and if it holds to the eternal...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s nature, specifically how it occupies a middle ground between time and eternity. The text completes the thought from the previous page: that by turning toward eternal things, the soul gains the stability it lacks by nature.</meta>

...[eternal] thing, so she becomes strong (88 a) and steady; with this strength and steadiness, she overcomes all changeable things.

God our Lord has given the <term>soul</term> <note>original: "sele"</note> two kinds of <term>powers</term> <note>original: "kreft"; referring to the faculties of the soul like reason and will</note> as a help, so that she may serve God in time with the lower powers and that she may serve God in eternity with the higher powers.
<margin>5</margin>

A master was asked how one should come to wisdom and other virtues; so he describes six things that a person should have. The first is a humble heart; the second is steady diligence; the third is a quiet heart; the fourth is silent inquiry. For no work is so
<margin>10</margin> perfect that it does not hinder <term>inwardness</term> <note>original: "innikeit"; a key term in German mysticism referring to deep, interior devotion</note>. For one can hear Mass with much greater devotion than one can speak it. One might have such great inwardness at Mass that it would be harmful <note>The author suggests that intense internal focus can sometimes interfere with the outward performance of ritual duties</note>. For all the skill that a person possesses is tested in their works. If a person
<margin>15</margin> sings well, that is tested or heard in the song. In the same way, one recognizes a truly wise person by their silence.

The fifth is willing <term>poverty</term> <note>original: "armut"</note>. He is usefully poor who can make himself poor of all things that are not God.

The sixth is a foreign land. If one could be a stranger <note>original: "ellend"; in Middle High German, this specifically meant being an exile or away from one's homeland</note> in one's own house,
<margin>20</margin> that would be true poverty.

With these six things one attains the <term>wisdom</term> <note>original: "weisheit"</note> with which one becomes blessed in this body.

The other wisdom is an inflow of divine clarity and is a fountain of divine nobility and is God himself. No one can grasp this wisdom in these
<margin>25</margin> temporal things. A person would be very foolish who wanted to make God temporal, and would hold God very cheap if he wanted to grasp God with the lowest powers. God remains ungrasped by all <term>creatures</term> <note>original: "creaturen"</note>. Therefore the wise man said: A good woman has opened her mouth to wisdom <note>referencing Proverbs 31:26</note>. This means nothing else than that you should open your
<margin>30</margin> desire to the highest things and should dwell in the very highest power of the soul, which is so akin to God that he cannot deny himself to this power. And this power shall receive so much <term>sweetness</term> <note>original: "suzzikeit"</note> and wisdom from God, and so much comfort and <term>truth</term> <note>original: "worheit"</note>, that it shall henceforth pour out into the whole soul.

The saints say that it is one way regarding temporal life and
<margin>35</margin> another way regarding eternal life, for we must always begin the thing here first, and then it shall be completed with eternal wisdom in the eternal life.

One master said to the other: "Do you know at all what God is?" "No," he said, "I do not know what God is; I know only this much of him: that I know what he is not. For no one can know God except
<margin>40</margin> in God’s nature, nor can one know him in any other nature..."

<summary>This page outlines six requirements for attaining spiritual wisdom, including humility, silence, and "being a stranger in one's own house." It distinguishes between human wisdom and the divine wisdom that flows directly from God into the highest part of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>soul, faculties, six virtues, inwardness, silence, poverty, divine wisdom, apophatic theology, eternity vs time</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the soul's path to spiritual life, concluding a thought from the previous page that one must "die" to their own worldly nature before they can be truly "born" or made alive in God's nature.</meta>

...become living, unless he first be dead in his own nature. Oh, why then is it that <term>Scripture</term> <note>original: "di schrift"; referring here to the Bible and the writings of the Church Fathers</note> gives Him so many names? It says that He is **almighty**, **wise**, and **good**, etc. There are three reasons for this. The first reason is that God has not "flowed" into any specific nature. I stand here now; I am
<margin>5</margin> no lion. Why is that? Because I am a human being. The nature in which (88 b) God has placed me, I am so enclosed within it that I cannot pass further into another nature. All creatures that God has created are like this. God is the life of all creatures and is Himself not a "nature."

The second reason is that one cannot give God any <term>likeness</term> <note>original: "geleichnûzz"; a central concept in German mysticism regarding how humans can or cannot represent God through images or words</note>.
<margin>10</margin> **Saint Augustine** <note>original: "Sanctus Augustinus"</note> gives Him many names: he says that He is **wise**. Now **Saint Dionysius** <note>original: "sanctus Dyonisius"; referring to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century theologian influential for his "negative theology"</note> says: "No, not at all, He is **beyond wisdom**!" He says that He is a light: "No, not at all, He is **beyond light**!" He says that He is a being: "No, not at all, He is **beyond being**!" etc. Everything that one can speak, God is not that. No one can grasp God’s nobility or His dignity with
<margin>15</margin> any things. When I say: "a human," with that I grasp a human nature. When I say: "a count," with that I grasp the lordship of a count. When I say: "an angel," with that I grasp the angelic nature. When I say: "God," with that I can grasp neither divine nobility nor divine lordship. **Saint Augustine** says in one place to
<margin>20</margin> a master: "The reasons for what God is are many. A good man: what is the 'good' man? A good stone: what is the 'good' stone? A good angel: what is the 'good' angel? Take away the angel, take away the stone, take away the man, take away the 'this' and the 'that'—what is then the **bare good** that God is?"

The third reason why Scripture gives Him so many names is
<margin>25</margin> because He is not like any nature, and because one cannot come to a <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "bekantnûzze"</note> of Him through any likeness. The highest creature that God has created in the angelic nature is more unlike God than the smallest speck <note>original: "vlac"; could refer to a spot or the lowliest thing</note> of nature you have ever seen with your eyes. A saint said: "Lord, it well becomes You that one remains **silent** about You." Two
<margin>30</margin> saints were meant to pray. One said: "Almighty, good God, have mercy!" Then the other said: "**Be silent!** You are blaspheming God." God is so far above us and so high that we cannot praise Him with any words. Were God not so <term>humble</term> <note>original: "otmûtik"</note>, and had the saints not spoken it, and had He Himself not named it, I would never dare to praise Him with words. To the degree
<margin>35</margin> that one **denies** <note>original: "lauchent"; to deny or negate attributes of God</note> Him more, one praises Him more; the more "unlikeness" one attributes to Him, the closer one comes to the knowledge of Him. As I wish to tell a likeness: If I wanted to tell a person what a ship was, who had never seen one, whatever that person saw, he would see well that it was 
not a ship. If he saw a stone, he would see well that a stone
<margin>40</margin> was not a ship. The more he saw that things were not created like a ship, the [closer he would come...]

<summary>This page explores "negative theology," arguing that because God is beyond all human concepts and natures, He is best described by what He is NOT rather than what He is. The author uses the example of "bare good" and the ship analogy to show that stripping away specific attributes brings us closer to divine truth.</summary>
<keywords>negative theology, Saint Augustine, Pseudo-Dionysius, divine names, unlikeness, silence, nature of God, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "negative theology"—the idea that we understand God better by saying what He is not than by what He is. It transitions from abstract theology to practical examples of holy living, specifically citing Saint Elizabeth as a model of detachment.</meta>

...the closer he came to the <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "bekantnůzze"; in this context, it refers to an experiential recognition or spiritual understanding rather than just factual data</note> of the ship. So it is with God: the more one attributes unlikeness to Him, the closer one comes to the knowledge of Him **(89 a)**. For everything that Holy Scripture can achieve, it denies it all to the senses at once. We should speak humble words, saying that He is almighty over all. When the soul comes into the knowledge that God is [completely] unlike all natures,
<margin>5</margin> she enters into a state of wonder and is driven back and comes into a silence.

With this silence, God sinks Himself into the soul, and she is drenched with grace, as our Lord has spoken through the prophet: *The tree that is planted by the flowing water brings much fruit* <note>A reference to Psalm 1:3</note>. The
<margin>10</margin> soul must die in the body, as our Lord has spoken: *This is proven by the grain of wheat that falls to the earth; no fruit can come of it unless it dies first* <note>original: "weizzenkorn"; a reference to John 12:24</note>. This dying of the soul should consist of the knowledge of God, so that she flees from herself and that all things which are not God should become tasteless and stinking to her.
<margin>15</margin> She should take root in faith and grow in love.

**Saint Elizabeth** <note>Saint Elizabeth of Hungary (1207–1231), a princess who famously renounced her wealth to serve the poor</note> well proved how precious and noble a trade it is to give all things for eternal wisdom. Therefore, she freely renounced her <term>princely fiefs</term> <note>original: "furstenlehen"; the lands and titles held by a noble</note> and became a poor person. The Scripture speaks
<margin>20</margin> of her, saying that *her light never goes out at night*; that is, she was found righteous in affliction, and therefore her light shall shine in the eternal life.

However perfect a person may be, if they lose any transitory good, their heart wants to change and become troubled. This is a certain thing: whatever a person loses against their will, if they suffer it patiently,
<margin>25</margin> they earn a greater reward <note>original: "grozzen lon"</note> for it than if they had given it to God of their own will. And whoever does this gives their will and their goods through patience to God our Lord.

Whichever person is found impatient in hardship, the malice of that impatience does not come to him from the pain, but rather it happens
<margin>30</margin> to the person as it does to a **copper penny**: as long as it is not in the fire, it appears to be clear silver; but if it comes into the fire, it is revealed that it is copper. The fire did not make it copper <note>The author uses this metaphor to explain that suffering doesn't create a person's flaws, but simply reveals the "base metal" of their character that was already there</note>. Therefore our Lord has tested the saints here in suffering, so that they might be found righteous
<margin>35</margin> in all virtues and shine here in the night, and they shall shine in the eternal life eternally.

The second part is: when does the soul taste divine wisdom? Four things arise here. The first is **likeness**, which means that one makes oneself like God in all purity. The second is **divine light**, which shines through the soul as the sun shines through glass. The third is **union**, which comes from likeness, and right...

<summary>This page explains that true knowledge of God comes through silence and "unlikeness," using the example of Saint Elizabeth's renunciation and a metaphor of a copper penny to illustrate how suffering reveals one's true character.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes a discussion on the mystical union and the soul's capacity for God before beginning a new sermon (No. 74) on the theme of "Following Christ." It transitions from the abstract concept of God's "immeasurability" to the practical imitation of Christ's suffering.</meta>

union comes from similar things, like light from light. The fourth **(99 b)** is <term>measure</term> <note>original: "mazze"; refers to the theological concept of "proportio," the fittingness or capacity of the soul to receive the divine</note>, that God has measured the soul. But God can neither be diminished nor increased, because he is immeasurable and unchangeable, but the soul must be raised up and widened, for she is small and <term>unchangeable</term> <note>original: "unwandelheftik"; while this usually means immutable, here it likely refers to the soul being "stuck" or "fixed" in its own limited nature until God expands it</note>.
<margin>5</margin> Therefore she should be raised above herself; and however much she is widened, it is still small compared to God's immeasurability.
A master speaks: Man is a small thing, unless he be raised above himself. Then for the first time the soul receives perfect <term>delight</term> <note>original: "wollust"; in medieval mystical texts, this refers to spiritual bliss or joy rather than carnal desire</note> from God, as she becomes proportioned to him. Why does God not <term>taste</term> <note>original: "smakget"; a common mystical metaphor for experiential knowledge of God</note> the same to all souls?
<margin>10</margin> This is because they are not sufficient for it. May we become sufficient for it. That God may truly become flavorful in our souls, may God help us, etc.

### No. 74.

Fol. 89 b. Follow me, etc. <note>original: "Sequere me etc." from Matthew 4:19</note>
<margin>15</margin> Our Lord spoke to Saint Peter: "Follow me!"
I have said it often and I still think of it [now]: If no scripture were known to men, the order of creatures by nature could [still] lead to God by right. Yet this is not my word, rather **Saint Augustine** speaks it: "All creatures cry out to man that he should love
<margin>20</margin> God," and they also say: "We are not God; seek Him elsewhere!" There is no sinner so blind that, if he wants to recognize God in his creatures, he cannot come to the secrets of God. It greatly exalts a man that he is clothed the same as his Lord and bears the [arms] on his shield: a man should have immeasurable comfort that he follows God in
<margin>25</margin> scorn and in pain and in labor, and keeps His commandments; and His commandments are sweet and light; for everything that He commands is light. For if He commanded one to steal, then everyone would increase his goods; or to murder, then another would be stronger than he, and the like. Everything that He forbids
<margin>30</margin> is heavy, and what He commands is light.
Three things make our Lord's commandments light: everything that He commands, He has kept Himself. If a lord commanded a thing and just stood by, it would be heavy; but if the lord steps forward into the battle, no knight is so timid that he would dare to step back. So it is with us. Our Lord's
<margin>34</margin> suffering makes us bold and strong for all suffering.
<margin>35</margin> The second thing is that He Himself helps and is with us in our needs, as He Himself spoke to **Saint Anthony** <note>Saint Anthony the Great (c. 251–356), a Desert Father famous for his struggles against demonic temptations</note>, when he was in need and called upon our Lord: "Lord, where were You while I suffered such great need?" Then our Lord spoke:

<meta>This page continues the sermon on "Following Christ," moving from the initial call of Peter to the internal motivations that make following God's commands possible—specifically the power of love and the hierarchical structure of the soul's powers.</meta>

"I was with you and saw it, and it pleased me to see how you conducted yourself in the struggle." <note>This quote likely illustrates God's presence during spiritual trials.</note>
The third thing that makes the commandments easy for us is **love**. For the one who has love, all things are easy to do. Therefore, Saint John spoke to his disciples, as if he were giving a final discourse: "Children, have **(90 a)** love, have love!" <margin>5</margin>
He wanted to say: "If you have love, then all things will be easy and possible for you to do." Whatever I will to do or leave undone, if I do it for the sake of love, or leave it for the same, then I possess that much good in the eternal life. 
Therefore, no person is so weak that they cannot do what God commands, or leave undone what He forbids. This is well proven in <margin>10</margin>
the **Book of Kings**, how a young girl was captured and led out of the land where God was known into a land where He was not known—those were heathens—and she was a servant there. There was a commander who was **leprous** <note>original: "auzsetzik"; in the medieval world, leprosy was seen both as a physical affliction and a spiritual symbol of sin</note>; she directed him to the prophet **Elisha** so that he might make him healthy. And he came to him, and the prophet commanded him to wash seven times in the <margin>15</margin>
**Jordan**. And that seemed too small a thing to the commander; then his servants corrected him and said: "If the prophet had commanded you a great thing, you should rightly have done it; now that he has commanded you a small thing, you should do it gladly." And he washed himself and became healthy. The Jordan signifies **humility**, in which a person must wash seven times against seven types <margin>20</margin>
of **sins**. It is common that many a person takes more care for his shoes than for his soul; he feels less pain that he has not satisfied God for a year than that he walked barefoot for a day. What grace can a person expect, since all pain will be measured according to the measure of the pleasure of the sin! However small a penance I may assign him, he will <margin>25</margin>
yet find the measure of the pains he must suffer for the sin, either here or in **purgatory**.
The other parable of the young girl should be understood better: it signifies the highest power of the soul, which is called the <term>clinging power</term> <note>original: "zuhangent kraft"; the faculty by which the soul attaches itself to the Divine</note>, which constantly clings to God. As I have said before, the soul is created <margin>30</margin>
below God with a distinction, and the angels are more noble, yet it is one nature; for the light of God in the angels is purer and clearer, so that it is never corrupted by sins. **Saint Dionysius says** <note>original: "Sanctus Dyonisius dicit"; refers to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian</note> that God has made all things in an order like a chain: thus the highest power moves the lowest, and the lowest moves the highest. <margin>35</margin>
A **Greek master** <note>likely a reference to Pythagoras or Plato regarding the mathematical order of the universe</note> also says that all things are made according to a number. The first number is one; four is also a number. If one adds one to four, it is five; where five ends, six begins. If one takes the one away, it is five again, so it is again a number. So it is with the powers of the soul: the highest, which <margin>40</margin>
clings to God, moves all the lowest ones; whatever [happens] in the lowest...

<summary>This page explains that love and humility make divine commandments easy to follow, using the biblical story of Naaman the Leper as an allegory. It also introduces the mystical concept of the soul's "clinging power" and the hierarchical "chain" of spiritual order.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "land of recognition" and the "highest power" of the soul—often referred to in mystical theology as the "spark" or "synteresis"—which remains untainted even when the person falls into sin.</meta>

happens, that comes with recognition to the highest powers. Therefore, a master calls the highest power a **spring** <note>original: "brûnnen"; referring to the soul's source or spark</note> that never goes out. Through the recognition <note>80 b</note> of the soul, which it has in this power, however deep a person falls, it [this power] reproaches their sins at all times. <margin>5</margin> Therefore this power is demonstrated by the **young maiden**, who was captured and led out of the lands of recognition into a land of lack of recognition, and yet she still recognized God. Thus it happens to the soul: however far this power is led away from God, it still recognizes God and still tastes of divine nobility. Therefore it can never be extinguished <margin>10</margin> nor entirely blinded. For even when the other powers collapse and the soul is killed through sin, this power runs out and announces the person's damage, both here and there eternally. This matter is well proven in the **Book of Lord Job**, when his children were gathered together and four winds came and struck the house down and killed everyone who was in the <margin>15</margin> house, except for one person who remained alive, who announced the news to Lord Job and spoke:
> All your sons are dead and everything that was living there, except I alone live, so that I may announce the news to you.

### Nr. 75.

Fol. 90 $^b$. An evangelist says:
> Whoever receives a light, he shall not cover it.

<margin>20</margin> Our Lord Jesus Christ spoke:
> I have compassion on the crowd. <note>original: "Ich erbarm mich uber di schar"; a reference to the feeding of the four thousand in Matthew 15:32</note>

By "the crowd" the soul is signified. For when the soul goes out with its powers into the creatures and they scatter themselves, then it cannot receive God. By "the crowd" orderliness is also signified. When the crowd holds itself together in one will and in one body to ride against the enemy, it easily triumphs. <margin>25</margin> Thus it happens to the soul. When it has overcome the body, so that the body must be obedient to it in all things and in all its works, and when it holds itself toward God in all its powers and in all its being, then it achieves this: that God must come to the soul and into the soul; there God and the soul become **one spirit**. There the soul becomes one spirit with God through grace.
<margin>30</margin> For three reasons the soul must be silent and must become mute. The first is: when the soul looks upon itself and sees how small it is in itself, and then when it looks back above itself at God and sees the **immensity** and the **bottomlessness** and the **unspeakability** and the **abyss of the Godhead**, and that the soul contemplates that in the Godhead—and when the soul <margin>35</margin> then looks back upon itself and sees that it is so very small compared to the high riches it has seen in God, then it seems so very small to itself and it must be silent. For it cannot speak as it would like to according to the truth, and therefore the soul must be silent. On this point Saint...

It uses the biblical figures of the captive maiden, Job, and the 'crowd' to illustrate the soul's internal order and its relationship to God.</summary>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul's relationship with God, transitioning from the "spark" of the soul to the "three silences" required to perceive the divine. It draws on the authority of Pseudo-Dionysius and the Book of Wisdom to explain how human language and worldly power pale in comparison to spiritual recognition.</meta>

Dionysius, whenever he names God, his manner of speaking is always such that he calls God the **super-good** God or the **super-wise** God. <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite was a 5th-century philosopher whose "negative theology" argued that God is so far beyond human understanding that we can only describe Him by saying what He is not, or by using "super-" prefixes to show He exceeds all concepts.</note>

<margin>5</margin> The second silence of the soul occurs when the soul looks into itself and beholds its own **simplicity** <term>simplicity: in a mystical sense, the state of being undivided and free from worldly distractions</term>, and then looks back above itself to God and beholds the **multiplicity** of all things as we speak of them in God, and yet (91 ᵃ) sees **simplicity** in God which no one can express. For it sees His goodness and His wisdom and His power—briefly, many aspects that exist in God according to our speech, and yet they are <margin>10</margin> simple in God. For where two things weigh equally, they stand firm and are justly balanced; if one were heavier than the other, it could not help but tip over. So it is with the soul when it looks at God and sees that God has enclosed all things in equal potential and in equal nature; then the soul must be struck with wonder and must be silent. For if one thing in God were less than another, the soul could hide itself within Him; <margin>15</margin> but that is not the case. All things are equal in God according to how we speak of them, and are enclosed within His nature; and therefore when the soul gazes upon God, it must become as one who is mute. Regarding this, King David speaks in the Psalter: "In an unsteady spirit I said that all the world was false and a lie." <margin>17</margin> <margin>unsteady spirit</margin> <note>original: "In eim ungewegen geist"; this refers to Psalm 116:11, usually translated from Latin as "I said in my excess/alarm." The author interprets this "excess" as a spirit moved beyond its normal state.</note> By this I meant everything that all creatures can say is all too small compared to the nobility that is in God.

<margin>20</margin> The third silence of the soul, which the soul performs, is when the soul looks into itself and sees [that it] is so far beneath God, and then looks above itself and sees that God is so high above it, and sees all things in God, and yet in a single, simple being, etc.

In these words we can note how greatly we should esteem the spirit of wisdom and how we should come to the spirit. Two things we should note in these words, where he says: "I called upon God," and: "into me is come the spirit of wisdom." The first is the sweetness and the taste of the <margin>35</margin> spirit. <meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual perception, moving from the soul’s silence to the necessity of detaching from worldly riches to receive the "overflowing" nature of God. It echoes the previous page's emphasis on God's simplicity by arguing that transitory things are "nothing" compared to the light of divine wisdom.</meta>

...wealth cannot at all respond to the spirit of wisdom. He who has found what I now say, well knows what I mean; and for such a person, all transitory things are small compared to eternal things.

A master and Saint Gregory say: 
> If a person from childhood <margin>5</margin> were raised underground and believed a person who told him of the brightness of the sun and the beauty of the earth, he could not [truly] believe it. 

So it is with man: as long as he is mixed with earthly things, and the light of wisdom has not shone within him, whatever one might tell him of it, he does not believe it. Regarding this, <margin>10</margin> a pagan master says in a book—which he christens and calls a "fountain of life" and also a "piece of eternal (91 ᵇ) wisdom"—in this book he teaches his disciples and says: 
> If you wish to know spiritual things, you should pull yourself into the highest part of your soul and to that which is most spiritual, and you should withdraw yourself from the multiplicity of transitory <margin>15</margin> things and should strip yourself and break away from the prison of your own nature; then all transitory things will come to you in the center of your soul, and then you will behold that all transitory things are a nothingness compared to wisdom. 

Therefore, the wise man is not satisfied with anything that is not God; he is ever like a hand reaching before the table <margin>20</margin> of our Lord God, begging for alms from God. In this way, we are all beggars. Concerning this, King David says: God has regarded the longing of his poor. <note>Likely referencing Psalm 102:17: "He will respond to the prayer of the destitute."</note>

A wise master says that God is an **overflowing being** <term>overflowing being: *auzfliezzend wesen*; a central concept in medieval mysticism suggesting God's nature naturally pours out into creation like water from a spring or light from the sun</term> and carries much [in] His perfection. That same power and perfection with which He <margin>25</margin> has poured Himself entirely into the Son, He carries and offers to, and releases for, souls. Each one receives as much of Him as they are able. Why do all people not receive His perfection? Because they are not "poor of themselves." <note>To be "poor of oneself" means to be empty of selfish desires and worldly attachments, making room for God to enter.</note>

Now we should note that God is an overflowing being (if it passes by and is not received back, it gives no power). The good man should know God <margin>30</margin> with inwardness and with devotion and with desire; thus he receives and retains divine perfection within himself, like a **sluice-gate** <term>sluice-gate: *schůtzbret*; a wooden board used to block or direct the flow of water in a mill or stream</term> that holds the water so that it does not flow away. Just as one can notice with the sun, that it pours out its radiance into the air: yet one could not well recognize that radiance unless it gained a reflection from the earth and from <margin>35</margin> other coarse things. One may also notice this with glass: as long as it is transparent, one cannot perceive anything in it, unless a backing of lead is placed beneath it; then it receives an image and everything that is present before it. <note>The author is describing how a mirror is made; without the "backing" of a prepared soul, God's light passes through without leaving an image.</note> A master speaks a refined word: As long as the eye is something like—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual perception and the "nobility of the soul," contrasting the soul's imprisonment in the body with its potential for divine enlightenment. It uses the metaphor of a pure cloth to explain how God’s light reflects through different mediums while remaining simple and pure in itself.</meta>

...utility it finds in the things that are present to it, such as a house or other things, then it cannot know other things when it wishes to pour itself out further. Now we may observe the perfect revelation of our Lord God in all things, how the divine light is accustomed to manifest itself.
<margin>5</margin> Just as one can observe with a white, pure cloth: if one lays [it] upon red or upon black, it appears black and yet remains pure within itself; and because of the background upon which it falls, it appears manifold <note>original: "manikfalt"; referring to the complex or diverse appearance of things compared to the "simple" or "einfalt" nature of God.</note> and is simple within itself. Therefore, according to how the desire is pure and divine, so it will be received divinely and become like the divine light.

<margin>10</margin> A master tells his disciple much concerning the nobility of the soul <term>edelkeit der selen</term> and of spiritual works that God performs in the soul, which one cannot fully know as long as the soul is bound in the prison of the body (92 ᵃ). When the disciple heard this, it went so deeply to his heart that he threw himself from the wall and fell to his death, so that he might reach those things <margin>15</margin> he had heard about. And yet he acted foolishly, says the same master. We should not release ourselves <note>That is, we should not seek our own death through suicide.</note> before God releases us, whose prisoners we are. Even he who is rightfully imprisoned must not release himself before he is rightfully set free. A Greek <note>original: "Kriech"; likely referring to Socrates or Plato in the *Phaedo*, who discussed philosophy as a preparation for death.</note> says: All wise people desire to die. Also, a master says that through the knowledge <margin>19</margin> of wisdom we should die to all worldliness <note>original: "unveltikeit"; the state of being caught up in the manifold, worldly things as opposed to divine simplicity.</note>, and that a person should withdraw himself from the sweet bed of temptation <term>bekorung</term> and from all fleshly things and all earthly things.

A saint says: One thing brings me to shame and to fear when I consider that God has turned his eyes as completely toward me as if no other creature existed but me. And he turned again to his own failings and said: "You unclean spots, how long will you trouble the eyes of my Beloved, who loves me so truly? Depart from me! I will no longer suffer nor endure you, for you so often disturb his eyes, which look upon me so purely and so clearly." Who will grant me that God withdraws <margin>30</margin> from me for a while, until I am purified, and then returns and remains with me?

The second thing to note is the nobility of the works. A master and Saint Dionysius <note>Saint Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian who wrote about the celestial hierarchies.</note> speak of the angels, and what their works are: that they purify, enlighten, and perfect. That should be the spiritual work <margin>35</margin> in the soul: that it be purified and enlightened and practice perfection; for then it becomes like the angels and receives by grace what the angels possess by nature. That this may happen to us, may God help us, etc.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual perception and the disciple's quest for enlightenment. Having previously discussed the "nobility of the soul" and the danger of seeking death to reach the divine prematurely, the text now shifts to the necessity of following Christ and the mechanics of spiritual "taste" versus physical attachment.</meta>

### No. 77.

->Fol. 92 a. <note>original: "Qui mihi ministrat"; Latin for "Whoever serves me" (John 12:26).</note><-

These words were spoken by our Lord Jesus: Whoever serves me, he shall follow me; where I am, there shall my servant be with [me]. In these words one should note three things. The first is that one should follow and serve our Lord, as he says: Whoever serves me, he shall follow me. Therefore St. Secundus <note>Likely referring to Secundus the Silent, a philosopher often quoted in medieval wisdom literature for his views on renunciation.</note> adds these words, which mean as much as following God; for the spiritual person has left [all] physical (92 a) things behind, leaving those who wish to follow God so that nothing may hinder them from God. Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Early Church Father known for his preaching.</note> says: This is a difficult saying for those who have inclined themselves toward the world and toward physical things <term>leiphaftig dink</term>: for these are very sweet to possess and difficult and bitter to leave behind. Hereby one may note how difficult it is for some people to leave physical things behind if they do not recognize spiritual things. As I have also said before: Why do the ears not taste sweet things like the mouth? Because it [the ear] is not designed for that. Therefore a carnal person does not recognize spiritual things, because he is not prepared for them. Conversely, it is easy for an enlightened person <term>bekanten menschen</term> who recognizes spiritual things to leave behind all physical (92 a) things. Saint Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian mystic whose writings emphasize the "unknowability" of God and the transcendence of the spiritual realm.</note> says that God's kingdom of heaven is for sale <note>original: "veil"; meaning available or on offer.</note>, and nothing is as cheap <note>original: "snod"; implying something of little value or easily attained in price.</note> as the kingdom of heaven when it is for sale, and nothing is so noble and so blessed to possess when it has been paid for. Therefore it is called "cheap" because it is for sale to everyone for as much as he is spiritually <note>The original uses "gistlichen," likely a scribal error for "leisten" (to afford/accomplish) or a variant of "geistlich" (spiritually).</note> capable. Therefore a person should give everything he has for the kingdom of heaven: his own will. As long as he holds onto any part of his own will, he has not paid for the kingdom of heaven. For one who lets go of himself and his own will, it is easy to leave behind all physical things. As I have also said before, how a master taught his disciple how to reach the point of recognizing spiritual things. Then the disciple said: Master, according to your teaching, I am uplifted and recognize that all physical things are like a small ship floating in the sea, and like a bird flying in the air. For all spiritual things are elevated above the physical; the more elevated they are, the more they expand and encompass physical things, which are small compared to the spiritual; and the higher the spiritual things are, the greater they are; and the more powerful they are in their works, the purer they are in their essence.

I have also said it before, and it is a certain and true saying: If a person were starving to death, and one offered him the best food, yet he died of hunger before he ever tasted or bit into it—God's likeness would be therein. And if the person were freezing to death, no matter what clothes were offered him,

<meta>This page continues from page 82, concluding the discussion of the "enlightened person" and the teachings of St. Dionysius regarding the soul's ability to grasp the divine through its likeness to God. It then moves into the second and third points regarding the nature of divine service and the ultimate reward of mystical union.</meta>

...he could never have laid a hand upon it nor drawn it to himself, if God's likeness were not there. This concerns the first point: how one should leave all things and let God <note>The phrase "got lazzen" is a key concept in German mysticism, referring to "letting go" of the self to allow God to act within the soul.</note> [act].

The second <insert>[is]</insert> in what manner we should serve our Lord. Saint Augustine says: That is a faithful service, which in all its works seeks nothing but God’s honor alone. He also says regarding David: God is my Lord, I should serve Him, for He has served me; and although He did not need my service, He turned <insert>[sic!]</insert> <note>The original "indewant" is likely a scribal error for "inwandte," meaning turned or applied.</note> His honor toward me solely for my benefit; therefore I should serve Him in return and seek His honor alone. Other [earthly] lords do not do this; they seek their own benefit in their service, for they serve us only so that they may profit from us. Therefore, we are not indebted to God in that way: rather, the greatness and nobility of the service itself shall be the reward.

The third is that we examine the reward, of which our Lord says: "Where I am, there shall my servant be with me." <note>original: "Ubi ego sum, illic et minister meus erit" (John 12:26).</note> Where is the dwelling of our Lord Jesus Christ? It is in the union with His Father. That is a reward far too great, that all who serve Him shall dwell in union with Him. Therefore Saint Philip spoke, when our Lord had spoken of His Father: "Lord, show us your Father, and it <gloss>(93 a)</gloss> is enough for us." <note>original: "Domine, ostende nobis Patrem, et sufficit nobis" (John 14:8).</note> And if he spoke of being satisfied by the seeing, how much greater satisfaction shall we have in the union itself! Saint Peter also spoke, when our Lord was transfigured on the mountain and showed them a likeness of the clarity that is in heaven; there he begged our Lord to remain there eternally.

We should have an immeasurably great longing for the union with our Lord God. The union with our Lord God should be noted with a distinction. Just as God is unique in His persons, so He is one in His nature. Thus one should also understand the union of our Lord Jesus Christ with His Father and the soul. Just as white and black are distinct—one cannot endure the other, for white is not black—so it is with "nothing" and "something" <term>iht</term> <note>In Middle High German mystical texts, "iht" (something/being) refers to the fullness of God's existence, while "niht" (nothing) refers to the lack of divine being.</note>. "Nothing" is that which can take nothing from nothing; "Something" is that which can take something from something. Thus it is entirely so with God. Whatever is "something" is entirely within God; nothing is lacking there. When the soul is united with <insert>[God]</insert>, she has everything in Him that is "something" in all perfection. The soul forgets herself there and all things, as she is in herself, and recognizes herself in God divinely; as much as God is in her, so much does she love herself in Him divinely and is united with Him without distinction <note>original: "on underscheid"; a technical term for the highest state of mystical union where the soul's identity merges with God's.</note>, so that she uses nothing but Him and rejoices in Him. What more could a person want or know, when he is so blessedly united with God? For this union, our Lord has created humanity.

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the soul's ascent and the imitation of Christ. It transitions from the theology of service and reward to the return of humanity to Paradise, citing the mystical hierarchy of Pseudo-Dionysius and various scriptural interpretations found in the Pfeiffer collection of mystical tracts.</meta>

When Adam broke the commandment, he was cast out of paradise. Then our Lord set two kinds of guards before paradise: an angel and a fiery sword that cuts at both ends. This signifies two things through which man shall return to heaven, from which he <margin>5</margin> fell. The first is through angelic nature. Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century Christian philosopher whose works on "angelic hierarchies" were foundational to medieval mysticism.</note> says that angelic nature is the same as the ordering of divine light. And in the angels, through the angels, and in the light, the soul shall regain its way to God, until the soul comes back to its first origin.

<margin>10</margin> The second: through the fiery sword, which means the soul shall come through God and divine works, which are performed in fiery love for the sake of God and for mortal <note>The original "totlichen" usually means mortal or deadly; the scribe likely intended "fellow Christians."</note> Christians. May God help us that this happens to us.

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the soul's journey, transitioning from the previous discussion on Paradise and the "Our Father" into a deep Scholastic and mystical analysis of the nature of the Kingdom of God and the inner workings of the Trinity.</meta>

...what his kingdom may be. Now understand with earnestness: God’s kingdom is he himself and his perfect being. Secondly, one understands God’s kingdom to be in the soul. Therefore he says himself: "The kingdom of God is within you." <note>Luke 17:21.</note>

Now understand concerning the first kingdom! The divine masters <note>The "divine masters" refers to Scholastic theologians like Thomas Aquinas or Albert the Great, whose definitions were often debated by German mystics.</note> say that <margin>5</margin> God’s kingdom is the unity of the essence in the trinity of the persons. Now there is a question: where is God most blessed? <note>original: "selik" — meaning blessed, happy, or beatified.</note> (100.) To this one answers and says: God’s blessedness is in him equally according to unity, but according to our understanding, God is more nobly blessed according to the unity of the essence than according to the trinity of the persons, as we wish to prove. Such masters <margin>10</margin> say that in the Godhead there are three kinds of distinctions. The first are called rational distinctions <note>original: "redlich underscheid" — equivalent to the Latin *distinctio rationis*, a distinction made by the mind rather than in the thing itself.</note>, the others are called formal distinctions, and the third are called real distinctions <note>original: "dinkleich underscheid" — *distinctio realis*, a distinction between actual things.</note>, such as the opposing relations of the persons. Other masters say that the Godhead never has more than <margin>15</margin> two kinds of distinctions: rational and real.

Now take note of the distinction of the persons! Saint John says: "In the beginning was the Word." <note>original: "In principio erat verbum" (John 1:1).</note> This "beginning" or "origin" is the Father, as Augustine says. Now there is a question: whether the Father has a unique beginning, and to this one answers: yes, his beginning is primordial, not flowing out <note>original: "auzfliezzend" — refers to the concept of emanation or procession (the way the Son and Spirit proceed from the Father).</note>, as I wish to prove. The <margin>20</margin> divine masters say that one should understand "essence" <term>wesen; the inner nature of a thing</term> and "essentializing" <term>wesůng; the active process or manifestation of that essence</term> in the Godhead. Essentializing in the Godhead is the Godhead itself and is the first thing that one understands in God. The Godhead is a foundation of all divine perfection. Therefore the Godhead is in itself the immovable unity and the hovering stillness, and is a beginning of all outflowing. Therefore I posit a passive welling-up <note>original: "lidlich aufquellung" — "lidlich" (passive) suggests an emergence that happens of necessity within the divine nature rather than an act of will.</note> and <margin>25</margin> we call the first concept "essence"; for the most proper concept and the first according to formal establishment is essence in the Godhead, essence according to essential concepts. Therefore God is essence, and essence is not God. <note>A classic mystical paradox: God is identified with Being/Essence, yet his absolute nature transcends any category, even that of "Being."</note> How one should now understand the origin of the Father: that is in the establishment of passive necessity. 

In other terms: because the Godhead in itself is a rational being, <margin>30</margin> therefore the divine essence of the Godhead carries itself out into a difference of concepts; otherwise they would not be distinct from one another; for these distinctions are rational, not real. Thus the first person arises passively out of the Godhead, not from a temporal beginning. Therefore his beginning is without property.

Now the question is: what is the person of the Father? To this one answers and says: <margin>35</margin> that is the essence in the Godhead, not according to essential concepts, but according to the concept of fatherhood <term>veterlikeit</term>, which is a formal concept that formally establishes the [person]...

<summary>This page explores the Scholastic and mystical definitions of God's Kingdom and the Trinity, specifically debating whether God's blessedness resides more in His unified essence or His three persons. It introduces technical distinctions between essence (wesen) and the "welling-up" of the persons within the Godhead.</summary>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholastic and mystical analysis of the Trinity, moving from the definition of God's kingdom and blessedness into the specific relations between the Father and the Son, focusing on the concept of the "Word" and the eternal generation of the Son.</meta>

person of the Father. Thus the Father is a beginning <note>original: "beginne" — translating the Latin *principium*, meaning the source, origin, or principle of a thing.</note> of all the Godhead. For this reason, certain masters say—and if it were so (100 $^b$)—that even if there were neither Son nor Holy Spirit in the Godhead, the person of the Father would still remain.
<margin>5</margin>Now, regarding what Saint John says: "In the beginning was the Word," <note>original: "In principio erat verbum." John 1:1.</note> one should not understand this "beginning" to be the Godhead or the essence, for the Father alone is an effective <term>effective|wůrklich — acting as the cause or source of an action</term> beginning of the Son.
Now observe how the Son is in the Father. Certain teachers say this: in that the Father looks into himself, he grasps within himself <margin>10</margin> effectively his simple, inexpressible nature. Thus the property of the nature follows. This same nature, which maintains itself effectively in the Father, maintains itself receptively <term>receptively|enphenklich — having the capacity to receive or be acted upon</term> in the Son and is divided according to the properties.
The masters speak of the divine birth <note>original: "gebůrt" — referring to the eternal "generation" of the Son from the Father.</note>, saying that one understands this word "Word" in two ways: essentially and personally. That first word, which is called "essential," every person possesses contemplatively, but the Father has <margin>15</margin> it contemplatively and effectively. And that same Word, understood "personally," is the Son.
The same power which maintains itself effectively in the Father maintains itself passively <note>original: "lidlich" — from the root for "suffering," here meaning the passive reception of being rather than the active source of it.</note> in the Son. Thus the Son receives all his Godhead from the Father. This is how one understands "to beget": passivity from passivity, Godhead from Godhead, as they are understood in the property <margin>20</margin> of the persons, which are established by the essence. Thus the saints and the masters say: just as the persons go forth from the essence as from a source, so the essence is established by the persons and the persons by their properties. Just as "paternity" <note>original: "veterlikeit" — the state of being a Father.</note> is a formal expression which formally establishes the person of the Father, so "sonship" <note>original: "sůnlikeit"</note> is a formal expression which establishes the person <margin>25</margin> of the Son. From these formal expressions one understands the relation <note>original: "widerdrakůng" — equivalent to the Scholastic Latin *relatio*, the way the divine persons relate to one another.</note> of the persons. This property, which is called the formal relation—such as paternity and sonship—this is the least that one understands of the divine essence: paternity and sonship. These are divine accidents <term>accidents|zuvelle — in medieval logic, a quality that is not part of the essential nature but "happens" to it; here describing the relational properties of the Trinity.</term> and inherent properties.
<margin>30</margin>Now observe concerning the Word in the birth of the Father: how it remains within essentially and goes forth personally. <note>original: "quomodo verbum essentialiter maneat et personaliter exiat."</note> For the divine masters say that every intellectual grasping is followed by a rational word <note>original: "vernůftik wort" — the *verbum mentis* or "mental word," a concept produced by the mind's act of understanding.</note>. Since the Father grasps himself within himself, his own nature is an object of his understanding. Therefore, as the Father perceives himself, <margin>35</margin> the nature follows according to another property, which is called "being perceived." Thus the Son remains within essentially and goes (101 $^a$) forth personally. Just as this working is divided by the properties, so the Son is born and goes forth from the Fatherly heart. Thus Eternal Wisdom speaks—

<summary>This page provides a highly technical Scholastic explanation of the Trinity, describing the Father as the "beginning" or source who, by understanding his own nature, eternally generates the Son as the "Word." It distinguishes between the "essential" Word shared by the Trinity and the "personal" Word which is the Son.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Father, Son, Word, Logos, Paternity, Sonship, Divine Birth, Essence, Person, Scholasticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Trinity. It specifically transitions from the generation of the Son (the Word) to the procession of the Holy Spirit, using the scholastic distinction between "nature" (for the Son) and "will" (for the Spirit).</meta>

-hood: "I have come forth from the mouth of the Most High," <note>original: "Ich bin auzgegangen von dem mûnd dez aller hohsten" — A reference to Sirach 24:3, often applied to the Eternal Word (the Son).</note> that is, out of the essential grasping of the essential Word of the divine Father. Not that there are two words in the Godhead, nor [two] things; rather, it should only be understood or spoken of in this way.

<margin>5</margin> <note>original: "Et verbum erat apud deum." — And the Word was with God.</note> The Word was with God. That is to say, in the distinction of the persons from the Father, as is proven here.

Now observe these two, how they bring forth the Holy Spirit. <note>original: "Quomodo spiritus procedat a patre et filio." — How the Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son.</note> The masters say this: in that the Father pours himself out with love into the Son, the love reflects back, and the Son pours himself back into the <margin>10</margin> Father. This pouring out of these two with love is a common breathing <term>breathing|geistûng — the technical theological term "spiration," describing how the Holy Spirit arises from the mutual love of Father and Son</term> of the Father and the Son. Now one might think that this breathing power was equal in these two persons; <note>original: "quod spirans utrius spiritus sanctus non sit similis in patre et filio." — that the breathing of the Holy Spirit by both is not [exactly] the same in the Father and the Son.</note> and that is not so. For according to the formal beginning, this breathing power is no different in the Father than in the Son; for the Father is formally a different thing than the <margin>15</margin> Son, but when one takes this breathing power formally and effectively, there it is the formal speech, and the property which formally establishes the person of the Holy Spirit. Therefore, the Holy Spirit goes out from two formal origins as from one effective beginning.

Now there is a question: whether this property of the Holy Spirit—that he follows <margin>20</margin> after the nature—is like the property of the Son. To this, it is said: No! For if that were so, then the Holy Spirit would have to go out from the work of nature, just as the Son did; and that is not the case, for then there would be two Sons in the Godhead. But the property of the Holy Spirit is following after the divine nature [in a different way], for his outflow is from the free breathing of the will. <margin>25</margin> Therefore, if it were the case that one could separate the nature from the property of the Holy Spirit within the Godhead, the Holy Spirit would still subsist fundamentally in himself, apart from the nature. And that cannot be in the Son, for the Son flows out from the Father as an intellectual word out of the work of the Father’s productive <term>productive|auzuchbericher — generative or fruitful</term> nature; therefore, he could <margin>30</margin> not exist without the nature. And just as the nature follows after the property of the Holy Spirit, and just as the Holy Spirit is not without the nature, so (101 b) it is conversely true that the property of the Son follows after the nature. Therefore, the nature is not without the Son, for there the Son is an image of the Father, because he flows out naturally; and <margin>35</margin> the Holy Spirit is not that, because his outflow is from the freedom of the will. Therefore, the [personal] oppositions <note>original: "widerdrakunge" — the "opposite relations" that define the persons, such as Fatherhood and Sonship.</note> fall only between fatherhood and sonship. This is not the case with the Holy Spirit. Yet then it is reasonable...

<summary>This page explains the "procession" of the Holy Spirit as a mutual breathing of love between the Father and the Son. It distinguishes the Holy Spirit's origin (the will) from the Son's origin (the nature), explaining why the Spirit is not a "second Son."</summary>
<keywords>Holy Spirit, Spiration, Trinity, Divine Will, Divine Nature, Father and Son, Scholasticism, Procession of the Spirit</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the mystical exploration of the Divine Essence and the soul's union with God, following the previous discussion on the nature of the Trinity.</meta>

88

opposition, not material, between the common powers of the spirits in the fatherhood.
In this, you, noble soul, should seek with understanding the kingdom of God!
Now Saint John says: *And God was the Word.* <note>original: "Und got waz daz wort" / John 1:1</note> In this, one understands the unity of the essence. Oh, noble soul, lift yourself up into a divine wonder! Oh, this noble company! The three persons are united in a simple essence. A prophet says: *The Lord reigns eternally and beyond.* <note>original: "Der herre regnieret ewiklich und uber"</note> God’s reigning in the "beyond" is in the essential unity. There, God is His own kingdom, existing above all beings.
Now there is a question: whether the divine essence—according to essential reason without the formal distinctions which the masters call the "eternal ideas" <term>eternal ideas: "ewigen ewen," referring to the Platonic archetypes or divine patterns of all things existing in the mind of God</term>—could be the blessedness of God or of the creatures. To this one answers: no! For although essence is alike in God and in the creatures, God’s blessedness and also that of the soul is situated in the divine essence. This is so because the divine essence is in itself with all the perfections that are called the eternal ideas, which are essential to the essence. Now some teachers say that it would be possible for the soul to [perceive] one of these formal reasons [separately], but then every formal reason would have to be separated from the others, divided from the essence, and be an abyss <note>original: "abgrundik"</note> in itself; and that is impossible. Therefore, this sense is not true. Rather, this is the blessedness of the soul: that it understands these eternal ideas, which are the formal reasons of the divine essence, together in one single property. For there is no division there, because there God is super-essential unity and is His own blessedness and that of all creatures according to the being of His divinity. And be certain of this: that God, in this unity, never recognized any other distinction (102ª) than according to a simple property. In this union, God is at rest.
The Godhead never became "this" or "that," but God [as Person] works all things. Where God works, therein He is manifold and recognizes all multiplicity. Where God is One, there He is free and void of all works. In this unity, God recognizes nothing except only that He is super-essential in Himself. Thus one should understand: *God was the Word*, that is the unity of the essence.
*This was in the beginning with God*, equally wise and equally true and equally good and equal in all essential perfection, which are the eternal ideas and the formal reasons and the richness of the divine essence.

->And God was the Word. — This was in the beginning with God.<- <note>Latin: "et deus erat verbum. — hoc erat in principio apud deum."</note>

<summary>This page provides a mystical commentary on the Prologue of John's Gospel, explaining that true blessedness lies in the soul's realization of God's super-essential unity. It argues that the "eternal ideas" or archetypes of creation are inseparable from God's simple essence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mystical exploration of the "Kingdom of God," moving from the transcendent unity of the Godhead to how this kingdom exists within the human soul.</meta>

Thus one should understand the divine kingdom in the super-essential unity <note>original: "oberwesenlichen einikeit" — a term used by mystics like Meister Eckhart to describe a reality beyond all categories of being</note>, which the spirit seeks through knowing and through loving.

In the second place, one understands this kingdom of God in the soul, because the soul is created in the likeness of the Godhead. Therefore, everything that is spoken here concerning the kingdom of God—how God is a kingdom unto Himself—the same may be <margin>5</margin> spoken in truth concerning the soul.

Saint John says: *And all things were made through him.* <note>Latin: "Et omnia per ipsum facta sunt" (John 1:3)</note> This should be understood regarding the soul, because the soul is all things. The soul is all things in that she is an image of God, and there she is also the kingdom of God; for just as God is essentially in Himself without beginning, so is He in the kingdom <margin>10</margin> of the soul essentially without end. Thus a master says that God is so present in the soul that all His Godhead rests upon the soul. It is far more perfect that God is in the soul than that the soul is in God. That the soul is in God does not make her blessed, but that God is in the soul, by that the soul is blessed. Of this be certain: God is Himself blessed in the soul, for in <margin>15</margin> the breaking-forth <note>original: "auzbruch" — the creative act where God flows into the soul</note> wherein God works the soul, He keeps Himself so uniquely in the soul that He hides His divine treasure and His divine riches entirely within the soul. Therefore Christ says: *The kingdom of heaven is like a treasure hidden in a field.* <note>Matthew 13:44</note> This field is the soul: in her lies hidden the treasure of divine riches. Therefore God is blessed, and all <margin>20</margin> creatures, within the soul. Whatever we say of the soul should be understood as she is an image of God.

Now understand wherein this image in the soul is situated. A master says that it is situated in the powers <note>term: "kreften" — the faculties of the soul, such as memory, understanding, and will</note>, and this is commonly held. This sense is true for one who understands it well; but if one takes this sense to mean that the powers are <margin>25</margin> distinct, then it is not the closest truth; but if one takes them as they are one in the highest thing that the soul can achieve, then this sense is true. And there, in the divine working, the soul has a reflection <note>original: "widereinkaphen"</note> intellectually without mediation in the divine nature. In that work, <margin>30</margin> she grasps her own being super-essentially in God. In this work, all things are divine to the highest image, for this image is, in its own work, formally God Himself and blessed, but not by reflection <note>original: "widerwürflich" — meaning the soul becomes one with God's act rather than just looking at it from the outside</note>. Yet in the same "now" that this image gazes into itself, it grasps God essentially in itself without mediation. Therefore it is blessed formally in itself and by reflection <margin>35</margin> from the divine being. A master says that this highest light flows without mediation out of God, and in the same "now" it grasps itself in a work intellectually without mediation in God. Therefore its flowing-out and its remaining in God exist in one intellectual grasping...

<summary>This text explains that the soul is the "Kingdom of God" and a "hidden treasure," arguing that the soul's blessedness comes from God dwelling within her rather than the soul dwelling in God. It explores the mystical union where the soul's highest faculties grasp God's essence directly without any mediating images.</summary>

<keywords>soul as image of God, Kingdom of Heaven, divine indwelling, Meister Eckhart, mystical union, super-essential unity, intellectual grasping, hidden treasure</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the nature of the soul's "highest image" and its relationship to the Divine essence, following the previous discussion of the soul's interiority.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>

of a simple property of a rational work. Be certain in God that the soul, in this highest image, never knew a creature as a creature, nor did it ever possess time or place. For in this image all things are God—sour and sweet, good and bad, small and large—they are all
<margin>5</margin> alike in this image. Just as the divine nature is changed as little by all that is a creature, so too is this image changed as little by all that ever came into time; for it encompasses all things and enjoys all things according to the law <note>original: "ee"</note> of the Godhead.

Now one may ask: since this richness is within us, why is it unknown
<margin>10</margin> to us? To this one answers: Because the soul, according to the common inclination it has toward creatures, must take the beginning of all its working in creaturely images; and such people imagine that the image of the soul lies in these workings, but it is not so. These same people know very little of the nobility of the soul, for this working is in the common understanding, which is
<margin>15</margin> originated with a rational image in the highest thought, which is there formally caused by the images of the phantasy <note>The "phantasy" refers to the faculty of the mind that processes sensory data into mental images.</note> and essentially caused by the highest God-beholding image, from which the soul receives all the richness that it can truly understand of all things. Following this understanding is
<margin>20</margin> the common will, which is nothing other than the inclination of the senses. Therefore, this common understanding takes all things as a truth, and the will takes all things as good. Thus all things are the objects <note>original: "widerwurf"</note> of these two works; therefore they are foreign and far from God. For (103 a) God is
<margin>25</margin> neither "good" nor "truth" <note>The author suggests God transcends human categories of morality and logic.</note>. In the same way that God is free and cut off from all that a creature can understand, so the highest image enjoys the Godhead.

There is a question whether there is any opposition between God and creatures. To this, answer thus: God has no opposition toward the creature, for God looks at nothing except into Himself; but the creature has opposition toward God, for everything that ever flowed out has a turning-back
<margin>30</margin> into God. Now let us take the highest image. The highest image, in the same moment that it shines out from God, looks back in with an uncovered face to grasp the divine being, without mediation from the work from which it has all its being. This image is God in His work, and for that reason it is called an
<margin>35</margin> image of the soul.

O, now noble soul, consider yourself and the nobility that is in you, for you are honored in that you are an image of God above the nobility of all creatures; and despise small things, for you are created for great things!

<margin>40</margin> Thus one should understand how the soul is the kingdom of God.

<summary>This text explores the "highest image" of the soul, which exists beyond time and sensory perception, asserting that while humans are distracted by earthly images, the soul's true essence is a divine reflection that transcends human concepts of good and truth. It concludes with an exhortation for the soul to recognize its own nobility as the kingdom of God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the soul's journey toward God, likely following a discussion on the nature of divine union. The text focuses on the "noble nature" of the soul and the necessity of self-denial.</meta>

- 91 -

First, seek the kingdom of God. This should be our entire earnest focus and all our searching: how we might recognize the nobility of God and the nobility of the soul. Now understand how we should seek the kingdom of God. In the *Book of Love* <note>original: "minnen büch"; referring to the Song of Solomon 1:8</note> it is written: "If you do not know yourself, O most beautiful among women, then go out and follow the tracks of your shepherds." This <margin>5</margin> word is understood to refer to the soul, for she is the most beautiful among all creatures. If it is the case that she recognizes her own beauty, then she must go out. Now note three kinds of "going out" from the three kinds of being that the soul possesses. The first being is the being of her createdness. The second being is the being that she has in the Personal Word of the Trinity <note>The "Word" or Logos, the second person of the Trinity</note>. The <margin>10</margin> third being is that which she has in the primordial nature <note>original: "auzuckteriger natur"; referring to the soul's pre-existence in the divine mind</note> which stands effectively in the Father, who is the beginning of all creatures.

Now note the first "going out," how she should go out from the being of her createdness! Christ says: "Whoever wishes to follow me, let him take up his cross and deny himself and follow me." Be certain of this, as surely as God lives: as long as a person is not as free of himself <margin>15</margin> or stands as he did when he was nothing, so long will he never go out into a denial of himself. The teachers <note>The Scholastic theologians</note> say that man is to be understood in two ways: as an outward and as an inward man. The works of the inward man (103 b) are spiritual; the works of the outward man are <margin>20</margin> physical. With the inward man, one seeks God through the contemplative life <term>contemplative life: a life focused on internal prayer and meditation</term>; with the outward man, one seeks God in the active life <term>active life: a life focused on external works of mercy and labor</term>. Now note this with earnestness! I have said before and I say it still: all outward practice is of little use, for it is only good <margin>25</margin> for subduing the nature that is not yet disciplined. And you should understand that all outward works that a person can perform may well subdue nature, but they do not kill it. The "dying of nature" lies in spiritual works. Now one finds many people who remain within their own self-opinion <note>original: "gütdünken"; a sense of self-righteousness or personal judgment</note>, that is, they do not deny themselves. I say this in truth: these people <margin>30</margin> are all deceived, for it is against human reason, against the exercise of grace, and against the witness of the Holy Spirit. They hold outward practice as their best; I do not wish to say that they are lost, but rather that they shall not come to God without great purgatory <margin>35</margin>. For these same people do not follow God because they do not let go of themselves; they follow their own opinion, in which they keep themselves. For God is as little to be found in all physical practice as He is to be found in sins. Yet these people, who have much of this outward practice, are highly esteemed in the eyes of the world, and that comes from a likeness; for people who understand nothing else than...

<summary>This page discusses the soul's nobility and the three modes of its existence, emphasizing that true union with God requires a total denial of the self and the "created" nature. The author warns that outward religious practices are merely tools to discipline the body and cannot replace the internal "death of nature" required for spiritual perfection.</summary>
