<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's progression from outward sensory experience to the inward "death of the spirit" and the direct vision of God.</meta>

— 92 —

bodily things, which they value greatly—that life which they can grasp with the senses. In such a way, one donkey loves another! <note>A common medieval trope used to describe purely carnal or sensory affection devoid of spiritual reason.</note>

Secondly, one understands the work of the inner man, which is called "beholding" in knowing and in loving. In this work lies the beginning of a holy life. Through these works, the essence of the soul is described. Thus the masters <note>Refers to scholastic authorities or Church Fathers.</note> say that every being exists for the sake of its own work. Since we cannot understand this essence except through these two powers <note>The intellect (knowing) and the will (loving).</note>, these are therefore the noblest works that exist within man. I have previously said that virtue is a middle point between vice and perfection; now, love <note>original: "minne" — specifically divine or courtly-style spiritual love.</note> is the form of all virtue, without which no virtue is truly virtue. When a person performs a virtuous act, the works of virtue should be the works of love and not of the person; for every work of virtue has its power in love, so that it may bring the person into God. Thus says Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian.</note>, that love is of such a nature that it transforms the person into the things that he loves. Therefore, a person should be such that his whole life is love. In this way, all practices are to be praised, whether they be outward or inward. Thus says David: They shall go from virtue to virtue, then shall be seen the God of gods in Zion. <note>Psalm 84:7. original Latin: "Ambulabunt de virtute in virtutem, videbitur Deus deorum in Sion."</note> Beholding God is above the virtues. Just as I have said that virtue is a middle point between vice and perfection, so the fruit of virtue—which is the end that virtue intends—will never be grasped unless the soul is taken up above the virtues. Therefore, be certain: as long as a person holds onto themselves with a sense of ownership <note>original: "eigenschaft" — the spiritual obstacle of claiming deeds or the self as one's own property rather than God's.</note> within virtue, they shall never taste nor possess the fruit of virtue, which is nothing other than beholding the God of gods in Zion.

Beholding God is not found in "Jacob," which is the practice of virtue, but beholding God is in "Zion." Zion is interpreted as "a little mirror" and signifies an uncovered beholding with a simple sight of the divine essence. Be certain of this: that no virtue ever entered into this beholding.

Now one might ask whether one should then give up virtue. To this I say: no, one should practice virtue, not possess it! That is the perfection of virtue: that the person stands free of virtue. Therefore Christ says: When you have done all that you are able, then say: we are unprofitable servants. <note>Luke 17:10.</note> Thus one should understand how the soul should go out from all its works.

But the question of how it should lose its own being must now be understood. The masters say that all things God has made are set in such nobility that no thing can wish that it did not exist. Now the soul must let go of what it is. Here begins the death of the spirit. If the soul is to accomplish this death, it must leave itself and all things.

<summary>This page explores the transition from practicing virtues to the mystical "beholding" of God in Zion, emphasizing that true perfection requires "letting go" of the self and even the ownership of one's virtues. It introduces the concept of the "death of the spirit" as the ultimate stage where the soul transcends its own created essence to unite with the Divine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the "death of the soul" and the necessity of total detachment, likely from a sermon by Meister Eckhart or one of his followers.</meta>

->- 93 -<-

and keep himself and all things as little as if they did not exist. Christ says: *Unless this grain of wheat dies, it remains alone.* <note>John 12:24</note> To die is the deprivation of all life. Therefore, be certain of this: as long as a person lives and "it" <note>the ego or self-will</note> lives in them, that person knows nothing of this death. Saint Paul says: *I live now, not I.* <note>Galatians 2:20; original Latin: "Vivo autem, iam non ego"</note> Such people understand this death in this way: <margin>5</margin> that the person should not live for God, nor for themselves, nor for any creature. This is true, because dying is the deprivation of all life. Furthermore, I will speak even better: even if the person were dead to all things, to God and to the creatures, yet God still finds a place in the soul wherein He can live, then the soul is not yet dead nor has it gone out into the "nearness" of its created being. <margin>10</margin> For dying, properly speaking, is nothing other than a <term>becoming-nothing</term> <note>original: "entwerdung"; the process of stripping away one's created identity</note> of all "something." I do not mean to say that this essence of the soul becomes nothing in the way it was before it was created, but this <term>annihilation</term> <note>original: "vernihtigung"</note> should be understood in terms of keeping and possessing. Here the soul loses all things, God and all creatures. <margin>15</margin>

This sounds wonderful <note>in the sense of "strange" or "miraculous"</note>, that the soul should lose God; and I say: it is more necessary in such a way, to become perfect, that the soul should lose God than that it should lose the creature; yet all must be lost. The soul's "best" must be in a free nothingness. This is entirely God's intention, that the soul should lose God: for as long as the soul has God and recognizes God and knows God, it is far from God. It is God's desire that God should make Himself into nothing in the soul, so that the soul may lose itself. For that God is called "God," He has from the creatures. When the soul became a creature, it had a God; when the soul loses its "creatureliness," then God remains in Himself, what He is; and that is the greatest honor the soul can do for God: that is, that the soul lets God be in Himself and stands free of Him.

Thus one should understand the "least" death of the soul, by which it shall become divine. These people are hardly known, for Saint Paul says: *You are dead, and your life is hidden with Christ in God.* <note>Colossians 3:3</note> <margin>30</margin>

Now one might ask whether these people practice any virtue outwardly, and I say: Yes! Just as Christ was, who is an image of all perfection, [who] was a light from without for the necessity of all people, so these people are in divine order and in a free openness, turned toward all people without regard for themselves. Thus one should understand the first "going out" that the soul must make out of the createdness of its being, to seek the kingdom of God.

Secondly, it must go out from the being that it has in the eternal image. The masters say that the soul's image is a divine understanding. That divine understanding is the Son personally. Therefore the Son is an exemplar of all creatures and an image of the Father, in...

<summary>The text explores the mystical concept of "Entwerdung" (becoming-nothing), arguing that the soul must lose even its concept of "God" to truly unite with the Divine. It describes the soul's journey from its created state back toward its eternal exemplar in the divine understanding.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the soul's return to God, moving from the realization of the soul's "uncreated image" to the necessity of "breaking through" that image to reach the absolute Unity of the Divine Essence.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>

in which image hovers the being of all creatures. As the soul now loses its created being, the uncreated image shines within it, in which the soul finds itself according to its uncreated state; for all things are one in this image according to the property of the image.

Now the soul must go out from this very image, wherein it finds its being according to the uncreated nature of the image, and the soul must do this with a divine death. And the soul finds this within itself: that this image and this being are not that which it seeks, for the soul recognizes itself in this <term>manifoldness</term> <note>original: "feltikeit"; referring to the plurality of forms even within the divine ideas</note>. For the least that we understand of the Godhead is multiplicity. Because the eternal being, in which the soul finds itself according to the property (105 a) of the eternal image, exists in multiplicity—since the Persons <note>The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit</note> exist in distinction—the soul breaks through its eternal image so that it may come to where God is rich in unity. Therefore a master <note>Likely referring to Meister Eckhart</note> says that the soul's "breaking-through" is nobler than its "out-flowing." Now Christ says: *No one comes to the Father except through me.* <note>John 14:6</note> Christ is the eternal image. Now, the soul's remaining is not within him; rather, it must come *through* him, as he himself says. This breaking-through is the second death of the spirit, which is much greater than the first. Concerning this, Saint John says: *Blessed are the dead who die in the Lord* <note>Revelation 14:13</note>, that is, in God.

Now hear wonder upon wonder! How can there be a dying in him who says of himself that he is the Life? To this one answers thus: Just as in the birth of the Son all creatures have gone forth and have received life and being, so all things are formed in a living way in the Son. Since the soul is now to enter back in, it must "lose" the Son. Thus the masters say: Where the Son turns inward toward the unity of the nature, there he is not a "Person" according to [personal] property; thus the Son loses himself in the unity of the essence. I say the same also of the soul: As the soul breaks through and loses itself in its eternal image, that is the dying by which the soul dies in God. Thus says Saint Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-6th century Christian mystic</note>: Just as God is "nothing" to the spirit, so also the eternal image—which is nonetheless its origin—is "nothing" to it. In the image, the soul has equality <note>or "likeness"</note>, for the Son is equal to the Father. But where they are one in the essence, there they are not "equal," for equality exists only in distinction. Thus I say of the soul: If it is to come into the divine unity, it must lose the equality which it has in the eternal image. Therefore Dionysius says that the greatest delight of the spirit lies in the "nothingness" of its image. A pagan master <note>Often a reference to Aristotle or Proclus in medieval texts</note> says: God is a "nothing" that fills all things, and his "something" is nowhere. Therefore God's "something" is not found by the soul unless it has become nothing, wherein it finds itself or can find itself, created or uncreated, as here...

<summary>This page describes the mystical "second death" where the soul must move beyond the "image" of Christ (the Son) to reach the absolute, undifferentiated unity of the Divine Essence. It emphasizes that even divine distinctions must be transcended to achieve total union with God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the soul's journey back to God, specifically focusing on the "death" of the self and the distinction between God's active nature and His silent essence.</meta>

spoken of the eternal image. This is the second death and the second
exit, where the soul goes out of her being, which she has in the eternal image,

The third being, from which the soul shall go out, is the unutterable divine <margin>5</margin>

but only by her dying.

Ah, now take note! The Godhead floats in itself and is to itself
all things. Therefore God, according to His Godhead, is above everything that any creature
ever understood as a creature or ever may understand. For Saint Paul
says: God dwells in a light that no one can approach. <margin>25</margin>

soul has gone out of her created being and her uncreated being,

nature, and she still does not grasp the kingdom of God, and she recognizes
that into the kingdom of God no creature can come, then the soul loses the sense of herself
and goes her own way and seeks God no more; and right here she dies <margin>30</margin>
her highest death. In this death the soul loses all desire and all
images and all understanding and all forms and is robbed of all being.
And be certain of this, as God lives: just as little as a dead person, who is physically
dead, can move himself, just as little can the soul, which is thus spiritually
dead, present any way or any image to any person, for <margin>35</margin>
this spirit is dead and is buried in the Godhead, for the Godhead lives for
none other than itself.

Oh, noble soul, now examine this nobility! For as long as that is in you,
that you do not leave yourself all at once and drown yourself in this
bottomless sea of the Godhead, you cannot know this divine <margin>40</margin>
death. The wise man says: The Lord has possessed me in the...

<summary>This page describes the soul's mystical "highest death," where it must move beyond even the active nature of God to reach the silent, idle essence of the Godhead. It emphasizes that true union requires the total stripping away of images, desires, and the self.</summary>

obedient to God in death, then He shall exalt it and give it a new name,
which is above all names. Just as the Godhead is nameless and without
40 a name, so also the soul is nameless like God, for it is the same as...

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the union of the soul with God, transitioning from the nature of divine friendship to the practical application of seeking the Kingdom of God in one's daily actions.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
he is. Therefore Christ says: I call you now no longer my servants but my friends, because everything that I have heard from my Father, I have revealed to you. "A friend is another self," says a pagan <note>Likely a reference to Aristotle or Cicero's definition of friendship: *alter ipse*</note>. God became "another self" for this reason: so that I might become "another He." As Saint Augustine says: God became man, so that man might become God. In God, the soul receives a new life. Here the soul rises up out of death into the life of the Godhead; here God pours into her all His divine richness; right here she receives the new name, which is above all names. This is what Saint John says: We come out of death into life, because we love.

Thus one should understand what Christ says: First seek the kingdom of God and His righteousness.

Now note how all things shall be cast unto us (106 b). This meaning is understood in two ways: the first meaning is that whatever perfection exists in all things, we find that in the first kingdom. The second meaning is this: to maintain perfection in all our works. Thus a person should perform all their works out of the order of the divine kingdom. Be certain of this: if it happens that a person works in such a way that their works make them "lesser," then they are not working out of the order of the divine kingdom. And therefore, when works are performed according to human nature, then trouble and unrest often fall into the works of men; but when a person performs their work in the divine kingdom, then the person remains at peace in all their works. As the Scripture says: When God had made all things, He looked upon them, and they were good. So I say also of the soul: when she beholds all her works in the divine kingdom, then all her works are perfect, because all works are equal there: my least is there my greatest, and my greatest is there my least. But if it is the case that the works are external to the person, then the works are imperfect, because the works in themselves are manifold <note>meaning "fragmented" or "distracting"</note> and bring the person into multiplicity; therefore the person is often set into unrest in those works. Therefore Christ said: Martha, you are anxious about many things; one thing is necessary!

Be certain of this, it is necessary for perfection that a person so raises themselves up in their works that all their works are performed as one single work; that must happen in the divine kingdom, where the person is God <note>A bold mystical claim of *theosis* or "divinization," where the soul acts in total union with the divine will</note>. There, all things answer him divinely, and there the person is a master of all his works. For I tell you truly: All the works that a person performs outside of the divine kingdom, those are all dead works; but those which a person performs in the divine kingdom, those are living works. Therefore the prophet says: God loves His work as little as God becomes...

<meta>This page begins a series of numbered spiritual instructions, likely from a mystical or monastic context, focusing on the elevation of the soul toward God.</meta>

## No. 1.

Our conversation is in heaven. <note>original: "Nostra conversacio in celis est" (Philippians 3:20). In this context, "conversation" refers to one's way of life or citizenship.</note> Saint Paul says that our dwelling place shall be in heaven, and our Lord also says in the Gospel: Where your treasure is, there also is your heart. If our Lord is then your treasure, your heart will most often be with Him. <margin>5</margin>

Now there are nine special reasons why our dwelling should be in heaven. The first is that the soul should suck and draw grace and sweetness in heaven, just as the little bee sucks sweetness and honey from the flowers. In the same way, the soul should suck sweetness from the most beautiful flowers of the Godhead. Of this, Augustine says: Your memory is sweeter than honey, etc. <note>original: "Memoria tua super mel dulcis est"</note> <margin>10</margin> Lord, the memory of You is sweeter than honey; thinking of You is better than sweet food; speaking of You is full of comfort; clinging to You is eternal life; being separated from You is eternal death. Therefore, we should cling to God like the branch to the tree: so the soul shall take all its virtue from God. For this reason, David said: It is good for me to cling to God, <note>original: "Mihi adherere deo bonum est" (Psalm 73:28)</note> <margin>15</margin> Lord, it is good for me that I cling to You.

The second reason is that the soul should learn the language of the angels; for just as the angels do not speak with words, so must you learn to speak with God through virtuous thoughts. But whoever never leaves the land where he was raised cannot speak otherwise than as people speak there. <margin>20</margin> So it is also with hearts that never wish to come into heaven, or do so only seldom; they can do nothing but carry on with useless, vain words, for their hearts deal only with vain things. And John [says]: He who is of the earth, etc. <note>original: "Qui de terra est" (John 3:31)</note> He who is of the earth speaks also of the earth and of the things that are upon the earth: <margin>25</margin> and the hearts of such people are fed with vices. But the virtuous soul flies over all that is on the earth and over every creature. As the eagle flies over all birds, so should the soul fly over all creatures until it finds God.

The third reason is that the heart may be enlightened; for whoever dwells in the light shall be enlightened, as our Lord says: I am the light of the world, <note>original: "Ego sum lux mundi" (John 8:12)</note> <margin>30</margin> I am a light of the world. Whoever is enlightened by the light hardly ever falls; but whoever seldom comes to the light stumbles often. Therefore, a person should always set his heart toward the light. The soul is like the woman whom Saint John saw, who stood upon the moon and was clothed with the sun and had upon her head a crown with twelve stars. <note>A reference to the Woman of the Apocalypse from Revelation 12:1.</note> <margin>35</margin>

<summary>This text outlines the first three of nine reasons why a believer's soul should dwell in heaven, using metaphors of bees, eagles, and celestial light to encourage spiritual focus. It emphasizes that the soul's "language" and "nourishment" are determined by where it chooses to reside.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Paul, Saint Augustine, Saint John, spiritual dwelling, heavenly citizenship, virtue, enlightenment, mystical metaphors, bee and honey, eagle</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
Thus is the soul, which has trodden all transitory things under its feet, and is clothed with the light of God. And upon her head she has a crown with twelve stars, that is, with manifold virtues, which signify a crown with twelve joys. <note>This imagery is drawn from the Woman of the Apocalypse in Revelation 12:1.</note>

<margin>5</margin> The fourth reason (Folio 86) is that the soul becomes known in the kingdom of heaven and becomes at home there; for whoever is often with God in heaven with his thoughts and with his heart, God recognizes him. But he who never, or seldom, thinks of it—how should he be recognized there? And so when these

<margin>10</margin> come on the Last Day to the gate of heaven and say: "Lord, open to us!" then God says: "Truly, I do not know who you are; I never saw you in the kingdom of heaven." <note>A reference to the Parable of the Ten Virgins in Matthew 25.</note> But to those who are well known there, he speaks most lovingly: "I recognize you well; I have often seen you here. You were often with me in your thoughts; you shall also be with

<margin>15</margin> me here forever."

The fifth reason is that the heart should not be defiled with impure thoughts; for whoever often rubs himself against a [sooty] kettle becomes stained. Thus it happens to the soul and the heart that deals with evil things: it becomes defiled. But the person who dwells often with God, his heart remains

<margin>20</margin> pure, and our Lord says: "Blessed are those who are of a pure heart, for they shall see God." <note>Matthew 5:8.</note> God desires to rest in a pure heart.

The sixth reason is that the soul is strengthened in God’s service and that the person becomes ready for all labor, and that nothing is too great for him to suffer and to do for God's sake. For it happens to him as it does to wild horses: when

<margin>25</margin> one first tries to ride them, they are quite timid and shy away from every little thing. So do people who are not strong in God’s love and do not know the joys of the kingdom of heaven. When they must suffer a little labor for God's sake, they take fright like the wild horse. That happens because their dwelling and their way of life is seldom in heaven, and they

<margin>30</margin> have not become powerful through love.

But the people who are often in heaven become bold in God’s service and in His love, for no thing so great can happen to them that it would separate them from God, as the soul says in the Song of Songs: "Love is strong as death." <note>original: "Cantica: Die minne ist stark als der tod." Referring to Song of Solomon 8:6.</note> For just as death separates the soul from the body, so

<margin>35</margin> love separates the person from all things and binds the soul to God; for sooner than she would leave God, she would leave her life.

The seventh reason is that the soul follows the virtues that she recognizes in heaven; for nothing was ever so good for one who must learn a thing as having a good model. Now, whoever wants to learn virtue

<margin>40</margin> should look often upon God (Folio 86b): He is a noble model of all good things, for He is the origin of all virtue. Just as when our Lord commanded Moses...

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual benefits of "dwelling in heaven" through contemplation, explaining how it makes the soul known to God, preserves purity of heart, and grants the strength to endure suffering. It emphasizes that love for God should be as powerful as death, separating the believer from worldly attachments.</summary>
<keywords>contemplation, purity of heart, Song of Songs, spiritual strength, imitation of God, Moses, virtues, Last Judgment</keywords>

### ->103<-

make wondrous things, so He spoke: "You shall make it as you see in me upon the mountain." <note>A reference to Exodus 25:40, where God instructs Moses to build the tabernacle according to the heavenly pattern.</note> Thus the soul should do: when she wishes to learn virtue, she should look upon God and press the golden seal into herself; thus she herself becomes like the seal.

The eighth reason is that the heart or the soul is in safety in heaven: for the kingdom of heaven is a secure place and a free land. Whatever comes there is safe and preserved from all enemies. Therefore we should at all times flee with our hearts to that place; and Gregory says concerning the blessed Job: <note>original: "dicit Gregorius de beato Job"</note> Since his goods and his children were upon the earth, the devil took them; but his heart he had caused to flee into heaven, therefore no harm could happen to the soul. Our Lord desires nothing else but the heart or the soul; for this reason, the devil desires nothing so much as the soul or the heart. We should send it into that free place, into the kingdom of heaven, so that it is guarded from the devils and from all vices <term>untugenden: literally 'un-virtues' or moral failings</term>. God says: "Give me your heart and do what you will."

The ninth reason is that our desire may be stirred at all times toward God and toward the virtues. For whoever thinks often of God and of the joys of heaven must have all the more longing <note>original: "iamers," implying a painful or yearning sorrow of love</note> for God. Let us pray, etc. <note>original: "Rogemus etc."</note>

### ->No. 2.<-

"Eat, my friends, and be intoxicated, most dearly beloved." <note>original: "Comedite, amici mei, et inebriamini, karissimi." from Song of Solomon 5:1</note> Song of Songs. <note>original: "Cant." for Canticle of Canticles</note>
Hold a feast and a banquet with me and I with you, my friends; eat and drink me and I you. I want myself in you and you in me. O, tender soul, now look and take heed how completely He wishes to satisfy you, for He says: "Eat, my friends, my beloved ones, my tender ones, my chosen ones, my servants, my followers, my disciples, my children! I want to unite you with myself, to enjoy you, nobly, lovely, supernaturally, wondrously, fatherly, and divinely!" O, tender soul, now look and take heed how He means you, loves you, speaks with you, offers Himself to you, unites Himself with you and you with Him. So also should you unite yourself with Him, offer yourself to Him lovingly, and constantly invite Him to you. For in the course of love there is nothing by which it grows so fast and to nothing else as from hot desire and burning thirst and constant mutual invitation between the lovely God and the beloved soul. O, tender soul, invite your beloved lovingly and say, as in the Song of Songs: <note>original: "Cant."</note> "Come, my beloved, into your garden and eat your fruit; <note>original: "Chom, min geminneter, in dinen garten und izze din fruht"</note> I have no one but you alone! I am your house, your temple, your crown, your garden, and your kingdom! You are my beloved, my chosen one, my spouse, my creator, my redeemer, my preserver, my teacher, my leader, my comforter."

<summary>This page concludes a list of reasons for the soul to dwell in heaven and begins a mystical reflection on the Song of Songs, emphasizing the intimate, reciprocal "eating" and union between God and the soul.</summary>
<keywords>mystical union, Song of Songs, spiritual safety, heart, Gregory the Great, divine banquet, bride of Christ</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the union between the soul and God, specifically using the metaphor of mutual consumption or "eating."</meta>

->104<-

My father, my lord, my God! Now come and eat your fruit, which is I; eat me! You have given the body the power to transform <term>undigested food</term><note>original: "undowigen spis"</note> into itself and eat it; likewise, work also in me and transform yourself into me!
<margin>5</margin> Now note how you must be prepared so that He may eat you as His food. Look and take heed: just as His food is digested in many ways in the body, so shall you be digested in the blood of Jesus Christ, and in His soul, and in the Eternal Word. You shall first be digested in the blood; you shall take a drink and shall drink into yourself the burning
<margin>10</margin> <term>heart-blood</term><note>original: "hertzplüt"</note> which eternal love has poured out; that shall digest out of you all bodily pleasure, comfort, joy, tenderness, pastime, and sweetness—whatever it may be called—and shall pour into you bitterness, labor, sorrow, sighing, and weeping, and shall make you die to all things.
You shall, for the second meal, be digested in the tender soul of Jesus
<margin>15</margin> Christ, which is rich in grace; (13 b) she shall pour into you a divine drink that digests you and works out of you all bodily <term>exercises</term><note>spiritual practices or disciplines</note>, words and works, cares, forms, and images. There you shall drink into yourself grace and all virtue and all perfection and all the <term>influences</term><note>original: "inflüzze," referring to the flowing of divine grace from the head to the limbs</note> that a limb may receive from its head, so that you become a food of God and are digested in Him.
<margin>20</margin> You shall also, for the third meal, be digested in the Eternal Word. There shall be digested out of you the kingdom of heaven, the earth, all angels, and all saints; you shall also be digested out of yourself, along with all creatures as they exist in themselves. You shall ascend above all creatures into the Eternal Word, in which all things shine more truly and nobly than in themselves. There
<margin>25</margin> the person shall leave all things and forget all things, for "God is a consuming fire." <note>original: "verderbendez für"</note> Deuteronomy 4:24. He desires nothing that anyone might offer as a gift, except for those who are His alone.
Now you have well heard how the human being is God’s food; now note how God is the human being’s food. Ecclesiasticus 24:29: "Those who eat me will still hunger, and those who drink me will still thirst." <note>The text cites "Eccl. 4," but the quote is from Sirach/Ecclesiasticus 24.</note> The more you eat and drink of God,
<margin>30</margin> the more you will hunger and also thirst for Him, because God is the primary good, a pure good, a single good as good, an immeasurable good, an eternal good. Therefore He is so fluid that He flows out and flows through at all times, until He
<margin>35</margin> meets an obstacle, as all fluid things do. And where He finds an elect soul who is worthy of Him and desirous, He pours through, flows through, illuminates, and penetrates her senses and powers; then the soul becomes so completely increasing <note>growing or expanding in capacity</note> that she is moved at all times and flows back into God with
love, with affection, with praise, with honor, with thanks, and with service. And thus
<margin>40</margin> the lovely soul is fed and grows and increases in God, and is thus filled "God with God," so that wonderful things happen there.

<summary>This page describes a three-stage mystical "digestion" where the soul is consumed by Christ's blood, soul, and the Eternal Word, eventually leading to a reciprocal relationship where God and the soul "feed" on one another.</summary>
<keywords>mystical union, Eucharist metaphor, Christ's blood, Eternal Word, divine hunger, deification, consuming fire, spiritual growth</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the union of the soul with God, specifically detailing the stages of spiritual ecstasy and the "banquet" of divine love.</meta>

-> 105 <-

When the bodily sense sleeps, then fleshly desire falls silent, external things flee, discernment is transformed, knowledge is renewed, the will is fulfilled, and the soul is ravished <note>or "raptured"</note> from itself. This compels one to leave external things behind, to avoid habitual things, to press beyond oneself, and to leap into the naked Godhead. <margin>5</margin>

This banquet has six stages: these are sweetness, greed <note>here meaning a spiritual "hunger" or "craving" for God</note>, satiety, drunkenness, security, and rest. Sweetness and delight are two things that belong well together. Now there are no two things that belong and fit together better, or are more inwardly united, than God and the soul. Therefore <margin>10</margin> you should know that God is in the soul just as the sun shines in the air, as the fire burns in the iron, as the color adorns the wool, as honey sweetens the water, as bodily food incorporates itself into the body, and as the soul makes the body living.

Even greater and even more inward is the union of God and the soul: it is inexpressible and unthinkable to all the senses; it is so <margin>15</margin> powerful that it separates the spirit from the soul and unites it inwardly with God, so that it becomes one spirit with God. <note>original: "mit got ein geiste wirt," referencing 1 Corinthians 6:17</note> Oh, tender soul, now unite yourself with your Lover! There you will find, gathered together, pure and overflowing, everything that you desire in all manifold things.

The second stage is greed <note>spiritual longing</note>, for the more the soul <margin>20</margin> perceives divine sweetness, the more the desire is kindled within it.

The third stage is satiety; that is, the more the soul perceives of God, the more bodily sweetness displeases it. Thus the soul freely despises all bodily things, and they become so repugnant to it that their presence is a pain and a torment. This food and this banquet are eaten by the poor <margin>25</margin> and not the rich: for one does not find them in the earthly realm where one lives for pleasure and cultivates bodily sweetness.

The fourth stage is drunkenness. When the soul is so overflowed with divine sweetness, it despises all adversity and rejoices in labor <note>or "suffering/toil"</note>. <margin>30</margin>

The fifth stage is security: for when the soul, through divine love and divine comfort, despises all bodily love and offers itself at all times to willing suffering, then it becomes entirely secured in God and says with Paul: "I know well and am certain that no thing, neither death, nor angels, nor any creature, can separate me from the love of Christ Jesus." <margin>35</margin> <note>original: Romans 8:38-39</note>

The sixth stage is the heart's rest and peace, so that neither joy nor sorrow can move or trouble it. There the soul rests in God alone, free from all things, like a person who has eaten well: then he rests and falls asleep. This rest Saint John calls a silence, Saint Paul a peace, and David a sleep. Therefore in the Song of Songs <note>original: "Cantica"</note> she says... <margin>40</margin>

<summary>This page describes the mystical union between God and the soul through the metaphor of a six-stage banquet, progressing from initial sweetness to a state of total spiritual security and rest.</summary>
<keywords>mysticism, spiritual marriage, six stages of love, Saint Paul, divine sweetness, ecstasy, soul and body</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the union of the soul with God, transitioning from the internal state of the "loving soul" to a formal scholastic and mystical sermon on spiritual freedom.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>106</header>

The loving soul: *I sleep and my heart wakes.* <note>A reference to Song of Solomon 5:2.</note> Fleshly desire sleeps and the spirit wakes; the outer sense sleeps and the inner wakes; discretion <note>original: "bescheidenheit," meaning the faculty of judgment</note> sleeps and pure knowledge wakes. Knowledge sleeps and love wakes; the soul sleeps, the spirit wakes; the human sleeps, but God wakes within the human.
Now mark well how that may be, and be free of all cares. *Therefore let us pray, etc.* <note>original: "Rogemus ergo etc."</note>

## No. 3.
### *Where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is liberty.* 1st Corinthians 4. <note>The author cites 1 Cor 4, though the verse is actually 2 Cor 3:17.</note>

It must be noted when the soul stands in the greatest liberty; but first, it must be seen when a soul is said to be free. It is said to be so when it is bound by no creature nor by anything that is not God—that is, "attached" <note>original: "beheft"</note>—insofar as a bond is concerned that can create a barrier between God and the soul. Then the soul is called free, when it shies away from all "accidents" <note>original: "zúvelle," referring to non-essential or worldly circumstances</note> of temporal things, and moreover raises itself above the power of the body toward the highest good, which is God. Then also the soul is called free, when it not only denies that which annihilates it before God, such as sin; nor is it enough for one to deny creatures—such as "avoiding" or "leaving" or "alienating" oneself from creatures—but rather the soul shall pull itself upward and have an "uplifting" or an "upward-flowing" into the Divine Being, which offers itself more to the soul and more lovingly than the soul does to itself. When the soul now perceives God in this uplifting, then the consolation of this false world takes its leave.

Now let us see three points that show when the soul stands in the greatest liberty. Blessed Augustine says: *When we know and love eternal things, then we are placed outside of this world.* Let us say, therefore, with that female lover: *Draw me after you, etc.* <note>Song of Solomon 1:4</note>, as if she were saying: "My own strength is not enough for me to know and understand You, since You exceed all things; but by Your strength, the soul can be raised above itself, so that I may arrive at this." When, therefore, by supernatural [grace], the soul knows that the Father is the origin and the "fount-like cause" <note>original: "causa fontalis"</note> of the Persons in the Godhead—of those things which are within and those which are without. It also considers the production of the Word <note>The Son</note> and the Holy Spirit, and since the highest liberty is in the Divine Persons. But first, it must be seen how the Father is the origin. The Father is an "origin" <note>original: "ursprung"</note> of the Son and the Holy Spirit, personally and essentially. You say, "How is this?" You must know that the intellect of the Father must form and speak itself in an imitation of a natural likeness, for without that, the Word would not be a Son.

<summary>This page explores the mystical concept of spiritual liberty, defining it as the soul's detachment from worldly "accidents" and its elevation into the life of the Trinity. It begins a theological explanation of the Father as the source of the Son and Holy Spirit.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual liberty, Trinity, mystical union, St. Augustine, detachment, divine intellect, Song of Solomon</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the eternal birth of the Word within the soul, a central theme in Rhenish mysticism. It transitions from a Latin-inflected scholastic argument into a German sermon (Number 4) regarding the Annunciation.</meta>

This must be a "looking back" <note>original: *widerblicken*—a reflexive gazing or contemplation</note> of God upon Himself, directed toward the highest in an eternal constancy, from which the birth is eternal. Here apply the word <margin>5</margin> of Hilary <note>Saint Hilary of Poitiers, 4th-century theologian</note>, namely: *The Father is always generating.* Also the word of Augustine on the Trinity: *If fire were eternal, etc.* <note>referring to the analogy that brightness is co-eternal with fire</note>. Take the example of the sun in the continuity, integrity, and constancy of the emission of its rays.

If God were to leave His intellect in a spiritual idleness, then all the distinction of the Trinity according to personality would pass away, and there would remain nothing of God but a bare essence, such as Jews and heathens believe concerning God, who deny the <margin>10</margin> outflowing of the Persons. The Word is eternally in the origin of its birth, and therefore it is forever conceived and being born, for its birth is eternal.

Therefore, one may properly apply the word from Maccabees: *The sun shone upon the golden shields, etc.* <note>original: "Refulsit sol in clipeos aureos," citing 1 Maccabees 6:39</note>. The sun cast its radiance into the golden shields, and from this the mountains reflected the light. The sun is the light of the divine <margin>15</margin> essence. That light gleams out from the Father into the golden shields of the divine Trinity—that is, into the Son and into the Holy Spirit—and from this the mountains reflected the light; these are the many chosen souls [made] in the image of the Holy Trinity. In themselves, the Persons in the Godhead are not reflected [back] when they mutually know one another in one and the same numerical knowledge <margin>20</margin> which transcends all else; by this knowledge, no creature has ever seen or known God. Thus, the blessed souls and the angels who stood firm see the Divine clearly and by intuition, and yet they have never seen God in the way that the Persons in the Godhead mutually see and know one another. In a similar way, I speak of love: for the Persons in the Godhead love one another through a bond of love, which love is nothing other than God, etc.

---

## No. 4.

In that time, the angel Gabriel was sent from God.
He who asked me: why do we pray? why do we fast? why do we do all good works? why are we baptized? why did God become man, which is the highest thing? I say: for this reason, that God <margin>30</margin> might be born in the soul and that the soul might be born in God. For this reason all the Scripture is written, for this reason God created all the world and all angelic nature: so that God might be born in the soul and the soul might be born in God. The nature of all grain intends wheat, and...

In the moment when this word is first received

Saint Paul says: "In the fullness of time, God sent his

15 no longer any time, there is the fullness of time. Then the day is full, when there is

this birth begins, for there is nothing that hinders this birth so much

20 [soul]; if it were not so, she would not be a soul; and if God could be touched by time,

then God could nevermore be born in her, and she could

all time must have fallen away, or she must have escaped from time with
25 will and with desire.
Another sense of the "fullness of time": He who had the art and the
power to pour back time and everything that ever happened in six thousand years and
what shall yet happen until the end, into a
present "Now"—that would be the fullness of time. That is the "Now" of eternity,
30 where the soul knows all things in God as new and fresh and present,
and in the [same] delight as I have them right now. I read recently
in a book: he who could ground it [would see] that God makes the world right now
just as on the first day when He created the world. Here God is rich. And
35 this is God’s kingdom. The soul in which God is to be born must
escape time, and she must fall away from time and shall lift herself up and
shall stand in a gazing <note>original: "inkaffene"</note> into this richness of God. There is width without width
and breadth without breadth: there the soul knows all things and knows them there
perfectly.
40 The masters who describe how wide the heavens are—it would be

<meta>This page continues the mystical discourse on the nature of the soul, the vastness of God's kingdom, and the hierarchy of angels, likely following a discussion on the Annunciation or the birth of God in the soul.</meta>

->109<-

wider than the wide heaven; I say nothing of the intellect <note>original: "vornunft"</note>, which is wide without limit. In the head of the soul, in the power of understanding, I am as near to a place over a thousand miles beyond the sea as I am to the spot where I currently stand. In this vastness and in this wealth of God, the soul knows all; there, nothing escapes it, and there, it waits for nothing.

The angel was named. The masters <note>Refers to scholastic theologians like Thomas Aquinas or Albert the Great</note> say that the multitude <margin>5</margin> of angels is a number beyond number; their multitude is so great that no number can grasp it, nor can their number even be conceived. He who could perceive distinction without number and multitude, to him a hundred would be as one. If one perceives the three persons in the Godhead according to their distinction, just as they are one, then <margin>10</margin> there would be neither multitude nor number, and yet one would recognize nothing but one God. Unbelieving people and some unlearned Christian people wonder at this, and even many priests <note>original: "phaffen" - often used disparagingly for clergy who lack spiritual depth</note> know as much about it as a stone: that the three persons are one God. They take the three like three cows or <margin>15</margin> three stones; but he who can perceive the distinction in God without number and without multitude, he recognizes that the three persons <insert>98</insert> are one God.

The angel is also so high. Our best masters say that every single angel possesses an entire nature. If there were a man who had all that all men have in power and in wisdom and in all <margin>20</margin> things, that would be a wonder, and yet <unclear>he</unclear> would still be nothing but one man; for though that man had everything that all men have, that would still be far below the angels. Thus, every single angel has a complete nature and is as distinct from the others as one animal species is from another, <margin>25</margin> being of a different nature. In this multitude of angels, God is rich, and he who recognizes this recognizes God’s kingdom. They manifest God’s kingdom, just as a lord is manifested by the multitude of his knights. Therefore, He is called a God of hosts <note>original: "got des heris"</note>. And all this multitude of angels and their dominion, however high <margin>30</margin> they are, they have a cooperation and a helping role in that place where God is born in the soul; that is, they have delight and joy and bliss in that birth. They do not perform the work—there is no work of creatures there—because God alone performs the birth; rather, the angels have a service-like work <term>dinsthaftig werg: a subordinate or ministerial action rather than the primary creative act</term> toward this. Everything <margin>35</margin> that works toward this, the angels and the creatures, that is a service-like work.

The angel was named Gabriel; he also observed the truth: he was as little named Gabriel as he was named Conrad <note>A common German name used here to illustrate that human names are arbitrary for spiritual beings</note>; no one can know the angel’s name. Where the angel is named, no master nor intellect ever reached. Very likely he is <margin>40</margin> nameless. The soul also has no name; just as little as one can find a name for God, so little can one find the soul’s own name, even though great books have been written about this. But where it has an outward expression <note>original: "uzlugen"</note> toward its works, from that one gives it names. A carpenter, that is not <margin>45</margin>

110

...his name is not [his essence]. Rather, he takes his name from the work [he performs].
Gabriel took his name from the work for which he was a messenger. Gabriel means
"Power" <note>original: "Craft"; Gabriel is traditionally interpreted from Hebrew as 'Man of God' or 'God is my strength'</note>. In this birth, God worked powerfully or works power. What
does all the power of nature intend? That it wants to express itself. What does all
nature intend when it works to give birth? That it wants to produce itself. The nature
of my father wanted to produce a father in his nature; since that
could not be, it wanted to produce one who was equal to him in all things.
Where power failed, nature produced one as similar as it could, which was
a son. I am certain of this: that the nature of my father has produced in me
the closest likeness it could; I am certain of this and find it in my
un-reason <note>original: "unvornunft"; likely referring to a state of mystical unknowing or intuition beyond rational intellect</note> through likeness, that I am a son, and thus I am called a son. But where
power is even more lacking or a mishap occurs, nature produces an even
more dissimilar human being. But in God there is full power; therefore
He produces His equal in His birth. Everything that God is in power, in truth,
and in wisdom, He births all at once into the soul.
Saint Augustine says: What the soul loves, it becomes
like; if it loves earthly things, it becomes earthly; if it loves God—then
one might ask: does it then become God? Should I say that, which would sound
unbelievable to those who have a weak mind and do not perceive it? But
Saint Augustine says—I do not say it, but I point you to the
Scripture, which says: *I have said, you are gods.* <note>Psalm 82:6; John 10:34</note>
Whoever had something of the riches I spoke of before, a
glimpse or even a hope or a confidence, would understand (98 b)
this well. There was never a birth so kindred, nor so equal, nor [so] one, as
when the soul becomes God in this birth. If it is hindered in anything, so that
it does not become equal to Him in all things, that is not God's fault. As
far as its failings fall away, so far does He work the soul into His likeness. That a
carpenter cannot build a beautiful house out of worm-eaten wood
is not a failure of his art, but of the wood. If the least of angels
were to be imagined or born from God in the soul,
all this world would be as nothing against it; for in a single
spark of an angel, all this world grows green, blossoms, and shines. But this
birth God works Himself. An angel can perform no work there except
a servant's work.
*Ave* <note>Latin: "Hail"</note>—that is "without woe" <note>A common medieval folk etymology: A-ve = "without woe" (vae)</note>. She who is without creaturely attachment is without woe and without hell,
and [she who] has and is the very least of creatures has the very least woe.
I once spoke a word: She who has the very least of the world has
the most of it. To no one is the world so much their own as to those who [renounce] all the world.

<meta>This page continues a mystical sermon, likely by Meister Eckhart or a follower, focusing on the "birth of God in the soul" and the distinction between divine grace and worldly action.</meta>

...has left behind. Do you know why God is God? God is God because He is without creature <note>original: "creature"; referring to any created thing or being as opposed to the Creator</note>. He did not name them <unclear>ch</unclear> in time; in time there is creature and sin and death; these have a kinship in one sense. And when the soul has escaped from time, therefore there is no longer woe nor pain; indeed, hardship there becomes a joy, and everything that could be imagined of pleasure and of joy, of bliss and of loveliness, that is as nothing compared to the bliss that is in this birth and the joy that is there.

Full of grace. The smallest work of grace is higher than all angels in their nature. Saint Augustine says: It is a work of grace that God performs when He converts a sinner and makes him into a good person; that is greater than if God were to create a new world. It is as easy for God to turn heaven and earth around as it is for me to turn an apple in my hand. Where grace is within my soul, it is so pure and is so like God and so kin to Him; and grace is without works, just as in the birth I just spoke of, there is no work. Grace performs no work. Saint John never performed any miracle <note>original: "czeichen"; literally 'sign' or 'token'</note>. The work that the angel has in God is so high that no master nor intellect could ever reach it so as to comprehend that work; but from that work falls a splinter, as from a beam that one hews. A "glance" <note>original: "blichen"; likely referring to a flash or emanation</note>—that is, where the angel touches the heaven with his lowest part—from that, everything that is in this world grows green and blooms and lives.

I speak sometimes of springs <note>original: "bornen"; fountains or sources</note>: although we are wondrous people, we must speak according to our understanding. A spring from which grace flows out is that in which the Father gives birth to His Son; in that same place grace springs forth, and there grace goes out from that same spring. There is another spring from which the creatures flow out of God, as far from the spring where grace flows as the heaven is from the earth. Grace does not work. Where fire is in its own nature, it does not burn nor does it do harm; the heat of the fire burns down here, but where the heat is in the nature of the fire, there it does not burn and is harmless; indeed, <unclear>where</unclear> the heat is in the fire, there it is in the (99) true nature of the fire, as far as the heaven is from the earth. Grace performs no work; it is too delicate for that. Work is as far from it as the heaven is from the earth. An indwelling <note>original: "insin"</note> and an attaching and a uniting with God: that is grace, and God is with it. For that follows immediately thereafter.

God be with you. There the birth happens. No one should think it strange to come to this. What does it matter to me how difficult it is for me, since He performs it? All His commandments are easy for me to keep; let Him command whatever He will, I do not mind it at all; it is a small thing to me, if He gives me His grace for it. Some say they do not...

<summary>The text explores the nature of divine grace, arguing that it is a state of being or "birth" rather than a "work" or action. It contrasts the purity of the soul's union with God against the limitations of time, sin, and physical creation.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Birth, Grace, Saint Augustine, Nature of God, Mysticism, Time and Eternity, Creation vs Creator</keywords>

->- 112 -<-

If you do not have it, then I say: that is a sorrow to me; but if you do not even desire it, that is an even greater sorrow to me. If you are not able to possess it, then at least have a desire for it; if one cannot have the desire, then let one at least desire to have a desire. Therefore the prophet says: "I have desired a <margin>5</margin> desire, Lord, for your righteousness." <note>original: "Ich habe begert einer begerunge" - This reflects a common mystical emphasis on the 'will' or 'desire' as the starting point of spiritual life, even when the feeling of God's presence is absent.</note> That we may so desire God, so that He may be born in us, may God help us. Amen.

# A

Likewise, the books written hereafter belong to the convent here at Saint Catherine’s in Nuremberg, of the Order of Preachers <note>The Order of Preachers is the formal name for the Dominicans.</note>.

I. Likewise, the first book of the Bible contains within it: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, Deuteronomy, Joshua, Judges, and Ruth.
II. Likewise, the second book of the Bible contains within it: the four books of Kings <note>In medieval catalogs, the books of Samuel and Kings were often grouped together as the four books of Kings.</note> and the two books of Chronicles <note>original: "Paralippomenon"</note>.
III. Likewise, the third book of the Bible contains within it: Ezra, Nehemiah, Tobit, Judith, Esther, Job, the Psalter, Ecclesiastes, the Song of Solomon <note>original: "Cantica"</note>, and the Book of Wisdom.
IV. Likewise, the fourth book of the Bible contains within it: Sirach <note>original: "Ecclesiasticus"</note>, the prophecy of Isaiah, Jeremiah, the Lamentations of Jeremiah, Baruch, Ezekiel, and Daniel.
V. Likewise, the fifth book of the Bible contains within it: the prophecies of Hosea, Joel, Amos, Obadiah, Jonah, Micah, Nahum, Habakkuk, Zephaniah, Haggai, Zechariah, Malachi, and the two books of Maccabees.
    Likewise, the five aforementioned books of the Bible were gifted to this place by Jörg Keypper for Sister Clara Keypper.
VI. Likewise, the sixth book of the Bible, which is called the book of the New Christian Law <note>original: "newen cristlichen e," referring to the New Testament</note>, contains within it: the Gospels of Saint Matthew, Saint Mark, Saint Lucas, and Saint John; the Epistles of Saint Paul; the Acts of the Apostles; the Epistles of Saint James, Saint Peter, Saint John, and Saint Jude; and the Revelation <note>original: "Apocalipsim"</note>; also the Mass for the Seasons and for the Saints, and the Gospel of Nicodemus <note>An apocryphal gospel popular in the Middle Ages.</note>.
    Likewise, the aforementioned book was given to us by the late <note>original: "selig," literally "blessed," used for the deceased.</note> elder Stefan Tetzel; it belonged to the Keypper woman.
    Likewise, after these stand four new books of the Bible.
VII. Likewise, the first new book of the Bible contains within it: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, Deuteronomy, Joshua, Judges, and Ruth.

XXIV. Item, a little book that contains within it: concerning the seven shops <note>or "stalls," a metaphor for Christ's merits</note> where our Lord was a merchant; concerning beautiful examples and concerning unity; concerning letters; concerning internal distress; concerning confession; how one should learn to die well; concerning the Holy Sacrament and good prayers; and seven hours of our Lord’s Passion and many other good materials, etc.

XLIV. Item, a little book that contains within it: the *Whosoever will be saved* <note>original: "Quicunque vult"; the Athanasian Creed</note> and concerning the commandments in the New Law <note>the New Testament</note>; concerning three kinds of death; concerning the Holy Spirit’s disciplined pupils; and much good teaching on patience and other virtues; concerning divine love and the loving soul; and how the Eternal Word inclined itself into the body of Mary; concerning the Holy Sacrament and a beautiful rosary of our Lord.

XLV. Item, a little book that contains within it: a teaching on how a person should direct their life; and concerning divine love; and concerning the physical and spiritual works of mercy; and the Ten Commandments; and the *Our Father* and *Hail Mary* and the Creed; concerning confession; and seven hours of our Lord’s Passion; and how useful and good suffering is; concerning a dying person; concerning the three prayers and other good teaching; concerning the twelve steps of patience. This little book is in a protective cover.
Item, our venerable Father Vicar gave us this little book.

XLVI. Item, a thin little book, bound in parchment, which contains within it: the sermon concerning the holy, worthy relics, as they are shown every year; and concerning the indulgence that is granted on our dear Lord’s Corpus Christi day and during the octave; and concerning the indulgence when one carries our dear Lord’s Body <note>the Eucharist</note> to the sick.

XLVII. Item, a little book, bound in red leather, which contains...

I. Item, the two aforementioned books were written by Sister Kunigunde Niklasin.

Item <note>Latin: "Also" or "In the same manner," used here to denote a new entry in the list.</note>, a book that contains several legends of the dear saints, as they were preached; and at the very beginning it speaks of the faith, of open confession, of praying and petitioning, and of the Sunday.

XXX. Item, a book containing the legend of our holy father Saint Vincent <note>Likely St. Vincent Ferrer, a popular Dominican preacher.</note> and his preaching and teaching, and the master whom a peasant taught, and otherwise many beautiful sermons and teachings.

XXXI. Item, a book starting with the receiving of our Lord’s body <note>The Eucharist.</note> and good subject matter—this is in the Netherlandish <note>Dutch or Low German dialect.</note> language—and of the master and the layman, and otherwise various collected materials.

XXXII. Item, a book on parchment <note>original: "pergemein"</note> concerning forty-two virtues, as the Great Albert <note>Albertus Magnus, the famous 13th-century scholar and saint.</note> wrote, and thereafter of repentance and confession and of sins.
Item, Sister Clara Baumgartner brought this book into the convent.

XXXIII. Item, a little book with many good teachings and sermons, and of the "Fragrant Wisdom" <note>Likely a reference to the "Little Book of Eternal Wisdom" by Henry Suso.</note>.
Item, this little book belonged to Sister Anna Kopin.

XXXIV. Item, a book of virtues and how one should attain them, and of the Holy Trinity, a beautiful sermon on Saint Catherine, and many other things.

XXXV. Item, a little book starting with the Eternal Wisdom.
Item, the two aforementioned books were brought in by Sister Ursula Höschlin.

XXXVI. Item, a book in a green cover <note>original: "grunen conpert"</note> containing the life of Saint Francis.
Item, Sister Clara Keiperin wrote <note>This indicates she was the scribe of this specific manuscript.</note> that book.

XXXVII. Item, a printed book <note>original: "aufgedruckt," referring to early incunabula or printed works.</note> of the twenty-four harps, as preached by Master Johannes Nider, Prior of the Preachers <note>The Dominicans.</note>.

XXXVIII. Item, a printed book of the legal triumph of Christ against Satan, the prince of hell; thereafter a fine story on whether it is useful for a man to take a wife or not.
Item, the two aforementioned books were given to us by Kunz Topler.

# M

I. Item, a sermon book <note>original: "predigpuch"</note> which contains within it the exposition of the **Eight Beatitudes** and the **fifteen grades of true patience** and the **fifteen signs of true humility**, and the exposition of the **Angelic Salutation** <note>The "Ave Maria" or Hail Mary</note>, and how it happens that a person cannot remain long without mortal sin, and concerning the **spiritual war** and the **spiritual trees**, and concerning **virginal purity**, and concerning the **Passion of our Lord**, and how a person should take up their cross, and concerning the **four falls**, and a sermon on *The Lord guided the just man* <note>original Latin: "Justum deduxit," a common reference to Wisdom 10:10 used in sermons for confessors</note>, and why our Lord retained His **five love-tokens** <note>The five holy wounds of Christ</note>, and concerning prayer, etc.; Saint Paul’s epistle: *Put on the new man*, concerning the **seven mortal sins**, and the *Ave Maria*.
The aforementioned book came to us from the maiden **Anna Grasserin** and from the maiden **Elspet Sailerin**.

II. Item, a book which contains within it how a person should take up their cross, and concerning the **four falls**, and a sermon on *The Lord guided the just man*, and why our Lord retained His **five love-tokens**, and concerning prayer and the *Pater Noster* <note>The Lord's Prayer</note>, and the epistle of Saint Paul: *Put on the new man*, and concerning the **seven mortal sins**, and concerning **blessedness**, and how a **cloister is likened to the earthly paradise**, and concerning the **physical beauty of our Lord and our Lady**, and the **fifteen steps of our Lady**, and a sermon on **our Lady’s departure** <note>The Assumption of Mary</note>, and concerning the **Ten Commandments**, and concerning the **Holy Three Kings**.
Of the aforementioned book, the first part was here before the **reformation** <note>Refers to the internal monastic reform of the convent's discipline, not the Protestant Reformation</note>, the second part the sisters wrote themselves, and the third part **Sister Margret Imhoff** brought into the house.

III. Item, a book which contains within it the **ascent and descent of the soul**, and two sermons for the **Church Dedication** after Easter, and a sermon on the **angels**, and a sermon on **Saint Jerome**, and a sermon on why God withdraws Himself from the soul, and a sermon from the epistle on the **seventeenth Sunday after the octave of the Holy Trinity**: *Walk worthily according to your calling*, and a sermon for **All Saints**, and a sermon on the **Holy Spirit**, and a sermon on **Saint John the Baptist**, and a sermon from the Gospel concerning the **ten lepers** <note>original: "sundersichen," literally "the specially sick"</note>.

VII. Item: a book which contains within it the Holy Mass and many beautiful <term>figures</term><note>Likely illustrations or allegorical types.</note> from the Old Testament, and a beautiful teaching between the Master and the Disciple, and the *Mirror of Love*, and concerning sins and confessions, the Ten Commandments, the seven deadly sins, the five senses, the six works of mercy, the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit, the eight beatitudes, the nine foreign sins, the four crying sins, the sins against the Holy Spirit, etc.; and the Hours of Our Lady, and the Office of Our Lady, and the *Mirror* in which sinners behold themselves, and concerning the Mass and what the priest's vestments signify, etc., and concerning the war between virtue and vice.

VIII. Item: a book which contains within it the Name of Jesus, and the <term>Pater Noster</term><note>The Lord's Prayer.</note>, and the love of God, and a sermon on the Kingdom of God, and how the city of Rome was built and by whom, and also concerning the churches and indulgences in Rome, and the life of Saint Dominic, and the holy <term>Langmannin</term><note>Adelheid Langmann (1306–1375), a famous mystic from the Engelthal convent.</note>, and a prayer with fifteen Pater Nosters, and the life of Saint Francis, and the life of Saint Clare.

IX. Item: a book wherein stands first the six names of our Lord's Body, thereafter the seven sacraments which signify seven fountains, and the <term>Rule of the Savior</term><note>original: "regel salvatores"; the Rule of the Bridgettine Order.</note> and several others, and <term>collects</term><note>Short liturgical prayers.</note>, and concerning our Lord's Passion, and concerning the Holy Sacrament—why our Lord received Himself—and other matters.

X. Item: a book containing a beautiful sermon beginning: *Be strengthened* <note>original: "Confirmamini"</note>, and a public confession of debt.

XI. XXI. Item a book, how several convents were reformed, and the names of the masters of the order and the provincial <note>A "provincial" is a high-ranking official in a religious order in charge of a specific geographic region.</note>.
Item Sister Kunigunde Hirsvogel and Sister Clara Keiper wrote this book.
XXII. Item a book about several convents and how devoutly the sisters lived therein.
XXIII. Item another book about several convents and how devoutly the sisters have lived therein.
XXIV. Item a small book, contains the churches and indulgences <note>term: indulgence (remission of temporal punishment for sins)</note> at Rome and at other holy places.
XXV. Item a book, contains an epistle <note>a formal letter</note> against the Jews and several prayers and other matters.
XXVI. Item a book, contains what the Mass signifies, and the prayers for it, and various prayers to our Dear Lady <note>The Virgin Mary</note> and the saints.
XXVII. Item a book, contains [information] about the convent at Weiler and several devout sermons and an example <note>term: exemplum (a moralizing story used in preaching)</note> of a novice concerning the souls.
XXVIII. Item a book, contains the legend of Saint Margaret of Hungary and about the four inspirations <note>original: "einsprechen"</note> and about the four feasts, about the Christ Child and how good the convent life is, and about the passion of Christ and several sermons.
Item the life of Saint Margaret was sent here from Tulln, and the sisters have written a part of it, and one quire <note>term: quire (a set of leaves of parchment or paper folded together)</note> was [written by] Sister Keiper.
XXIX. Item another small book, also contains how several convents were reformed.
XXX. Item a small parchment book, contains within it good material for a soul from many *miserere* <note>Latin: "Have mercy"; refers to Psalm 51 or prayers of repentance.</note>.
XXXI. Item a small book, contains the interpretation of the Mass, and Sister Ursula Hoschlin brought it here.
XXXII. Item a book about the Holy Sacrament and what the evil spirit says through a spiritual person, quite various matters.

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the administrative records of the convent's library management, specifically documenting the donation of surplus books to other religious houses.</meta>

<page-num>160</page-num>
<header>— 160 —</header>

Likewise, in the year counted as 1489 <note>original: "MCCCC und LXXXIX"</note>, the venerable Mother Veronica Berhartin was Prioress <term>Prioress: the superior or head of a convent</term>. She, with the advice of the Mother Sub-prioress and the Librarian <term>puchmaisterin: literally 'book master,' the sister responsible for the care and organization of the library</term>, Sister Kunigund Loffelholz, gave several small-format books to our [affiliated] convents. This was done because there were so many surplus books, often with the same subject matter appearing three or four times, which was simply too much. Since the sisters in Regensburg and Gotteszell and elsewhere had a shortage, [these books were sent], and one can find what was given away marked according to the letters of the alphabet. And although some things were given away, many other copies of the same texts remain written here within [our library]. The former Mother Prioress, Kunigund Hallerin, also gave a portion of them away while she held the office of the Prioress.

-><image-desc>A small, ornate, centered divider with scrollwork</image-desc><-
